### [1] [X-CoSD: Communication-Efficient Cross-Vocabulary Collaborative Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09166)
*Jaeduk Lee,Wan Choi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes X-CoSD and X-CoSD-E, new collaborative speculative decoding frameworks that work with different vocabularies between a small on-device model and a large server model, reducing communication while preserving the server model’s output distribution and speed/quality trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing collaborative speculative decoding assumes the same vocabulary for both device SLM and server LLM, and requires large communication overhead because residual resampling needs full token distributions sent over the network. This limits practicality for heterogeneous models and bandwidth-constrained settings. The paper aims to make CoSD work efficiently when the SLM and LLM use different vocabularies, reducing communication without sacrificing correctness.

Method: Introduce cross-vocabulary CoSD (X-CoSD) that enables collaboration between an SLM and LLM with heterogeneous vocabularies via a hybrid resampling (HR) scheme. HR splits residual resampling into two regions: the common-vocabulary tokens are handled on-device, while LLM-only tokens are handled on the server. This way, only the partial distribution over the common vocabulary is transmitted. They also propose X-CoSD-E based on server resampling with device verification (SR-DV): the server performs resampling and sends only replacement candidate tokens and associated probabilities for the device to verify locally. Both variants are theoretically shown to be lossless with respect to the server LLM distribution.

Result: Theoretically prove that X-CoSD and X-CoSD-E exactly preserve the target distribution of the server LLM despite using an approximate SLM for drafting. Empirically demonstrate that both methods notably accelerate token generation (higher throughput / lower latency) compared with baseline CoSD and standard LLM decoding, while generation quality—measured by standard metrics—remains comparable to that of the standalone server LLM.

Conclusion: Cross-vocabulary speculative decoding is feasible and beneficial: X-CoSD and its enhanced variant X-CoSD-E support heterogeneous SLM/LLM vocabularies, substantially cut communication costs, and maintain the exact server LLM distribution. This enables faster, bandwidth-efficient collaborative inference between edge devices and server-hosted LLMs without degrading output quality.

Abstract: This paper investigates collaborative speculative decoding (CoSD), a distributed large language model (LLM) inference framework in which an on-device small language model (SLM) drafts candidate tokens and a server LLM verifies them. Existing CoSD methods assume a shared vocabulary between the SLM and the LLM and incur substantial communication load because residual resampling requires token distribution exchange between the user device and the edge server. To address these limitations, we propose cross-vocabulary CoSD (X-CoSD), a lossless and communication-efficient CoSD framework for heterogeneous SLM-LLM vocabularies. X-CoSD is built on hybrid resampling (HR), which splits residual resampling across the common-vocabulary region on the device and the LLM-only region on the server, so that distribution transmission is required only for the common-vocabulary region. We further propose X-CoSD-E, an enhanced variant based on server resampling with device verification (SR-DV), in which the server sends only replacement candidates sampled from the server LLM and their corresponding probabilities for local verification at the device. We prove that both X-CoSD and X-CoSD-E preserve the server LLM distribution, and experiments show that they significantly improve token generation speed while maintaining generation quality comparable to that of the server LLM.

</details>


### [2] [StochBench: A Domain-Specific Benchmark for Stochastic Processes in Lean](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09264)
*Idan Davidovich,Debargha Ganguly,Vikash Singh,Vipin Chaudhary*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduce StochBench, a Lean 4 benchmark of 450 graduate-level stochastic processes problems for evaluating theorem-proving LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing formal theorem-proving benchmarks are small, competition-math focused (IMO, Putnam) and do not represent domain-specific, applied mathematical fields like stochastic processes. There is a need for a larger, more representative benchmark with paired natural-language sources.

Method: Construct StochBench: 450 graduate stochastic-process problems covering a wide range of subtopics (Markov chains, martingales, Brownian motion, etc.), formalized in Lean 4, each linked to its original natural-language statement. Evaluate an Opus 4.8-based automated theorem-proving agent on this benchmark with a 15-minute time limit per problem.

Result: The Opus 4.8-based agent proves 157 out of 450 problems, corresponding to a 34.9% proof success rate under the given time budget, demonstrating that the benchmark is challenging even for strong contemporary provers.

Conclusion: StochBench is a more realistic, domain-specific benchmark for applied mathematics in formal theorem proving, filling a gap in Mathlib and remaining difficult for advanced automated provers.

Abstract: Leading benchmarks for formal theorem proving with large language models are small collections drawn from competition math, such as the IMO and Putnam, that poorly represent field-specific applications. We introduce StochBench, a Lean 4 benchmark of 450 graduate stochastic-processes problems at varying abstraction levels, each paired with its natural-language source. Addressing a field underrepresented in Mathlib, it covers finite and countable Markov chains, renewal processes, random walks, martingales, stopping times, queues, Brownian motion, stochastic calculus, weak convergence, and Poisson and continuous-time Markov processes. Our Opus 4.8-based agent achieves a 34.9% proof rate (157/450) under a 15-minute per-problem limit. StochBench better represents domain-specific applied mathematics while remaining challenging for advanced provers.

</details>


### [3] [Osprey: Target-agnostic Pre-training Makes Stronger Drafters in Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09338)
*Fengxiang Bie,Yuqing Jian,Yifan Yu,Zhongzhu Zhou,Zelei Shao,Ben Athiwaratkun,Shuaiwen Leon Song,Chenfeng Xu,Xiaoxia Wu,Tianyi Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents Osprey, a reusable, pretrained speculative decoding drafter that can be efficiently adapted to many different large language models, substantially improving speed and acceptance rates, especially out-of-domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speculative decoding drafters are tightly coupled to a single target LLM: they are trained on that model’s hidden states and logits over a narrow data distribution. As a result, their acceptance rates drop sharply when the workload or domain shifts, and each new target model requires expensive, target-specific pretraining. This contradicts the broader LLM trend where generalization is achieved via large-scale pretraining that can be reused. The authors aim to make speculative drafters similarly generalizable and reusable, so that a single drafter backbone can serve many targets and remain robust to distribution shifts.

Method: The authors introduce Osprey, a drafter architecture built by bootstrapping from a small, off-the-shelf pretrained language model. First, they prune the small LM into a shallow backbone suitable for low-latency speculative decoding. Then they restore and refine its language-modeling capacity via target-agnostic next-token pretraining so it remains broadly capable. For each target LLM, they perform a lightweight adaptation step: (1) align vocabularies between the drafter and target; (2) expand attention QKV parameters with zero-initialization to accommodate interaction with the target’s hidden states; and (3) distill from the target model’s output distribution so the drafter’s token proposals match the target. Crucially, the main pretrained computation of the backbone is preserved, enabling reuse across many targets.

Result: Using one shared Osprey backbone and only per-target adaptation, the method improves speculative decoding performance across several LLMs. It increases mean acceptance length by 16.1% for Qwen3-8B, 21.2% for Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct, and 22.7% for the 229B MiniMax-M2.5. For MiniMax-M2.5, it also yields 17.5% higher tokens-per-second throughput. The gains are particularly strong on out-of-domain and multilingual workloads, where conventional, narrowly trained drafters tend to fail. These results demonstrate that a single, broadly pretrained drafter can generalize and transfer effectively across diverse targets.

Conclusion: Osprey shows that speculative decoding drafters can benefit from the same kind of broad, reusable pretraining that has driven advances in LLMs themselves. By pruning and reusing small, pretrained LMs as shallow drafter backbones and applying light per-target adaptation techniques—vocabulary alignment, structured QKV expansion, and distillation—the authors obtain a single drafter that transfers across multiple target LLMs, improves acceptance lengths and throughput, and is more robust to domain shifts. This reframes drafter training from bespoke, target-specific work into a largely reusable pretraining problem, reducing engineering cost while enhancing performance in real-world, heterogeneous workloads.

Abstract: Speculative decoding is critical for accelerating LLM inference. However, the speedup is fragile: drafters are typically trained against a narrow distribution for a single target model, and their acceptance rate collapses under workload shifts. This is a striking inversion of modern LLM development, where target models are valued precisely for the broad generalization they acquire through large-scale pretraining. We argue that the natural remedy, pretraining, has been hard to apply to drafters because existing recipes are target-specific: the drafter consumes the target's hidden states and is distilled on the target's logits, so pretraining must be repeated for each target. We introduce Osprey, which instead bootstraps drafters from off-the-shelf pretrained small language models, treating broad pretraining as a reusable, target-agnostic asset and reducing per-target work to a lightweight adaptation step. Realizing this requires overcoming two challenges: small LMs are far deeper than a latency-bound drafter can afford, and their pretrained computation must remain intact while the drafter learns to ingest target hidden states and emit tokens in the target's vocabulary. Osprey addresses both by pruning to a shallow backbone, restoring its language-modeling capability with target-agnostic next-token pretraining, and adapting it to each target through vocabulary alignment, zero-initialized QKV expansion, and distillation from the target model's output distribution. Empirically, a single pretrained Osprey backbone transfers across targets and improves mean acceptance length by 16.1% for Qwen3-8B, 21.2% for Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct, and 22.7% for the 229B MiniMax-M2.5 (with 17.5% higher tokens per second), with the largest gains on out-of-domain and multilingual data. Our code is available at https://github.com/LeanModels/Osprey.

</details>


### [4] [SWORD: Wikidata-based Distortions Reveal Hidden Cross-Lingual Inconsistencies in LLM Factual Error Rejection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09349)
*Sanghyeok Park,Minji Kang,Hosung Kwak,Jinhyuk Yun*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces SWORD, a new benchmark to test whether multilingual LLMs can reliably detect factual errors rather than just pick correct answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multilingual benchmarks mainly test answer selection and overall accuracy, which can hide whether models actually understand and verify facts. The authors want to probe genuine factual verification and reveal hidden weaknesses across languages.

Method: They construct SWORD by systematically distorting Wikidata triples to produce fluent but factually wrong statements in eight major languages. Distortions range from random entity swaps to more semantically plausible, property-based substitutions. Models are then evaluated on their ability to reject these incorrect statements across languages.

Result: Models surprisingly perform better on semantically plausible distortions than on obviously random ones, implying reliance on surface distributional patterns instead of true fact-checking. Additionally, despite similar standard accuracy across languages, many models suffer large performance drops on (East) Asian languages when evaluating distorted statements, with cross-lingual gaps up to 28 percentage points (a 49% relative reduction).

Conclusion: Standard multilingual benchmarks obscure important asymmetries in factual reasoning. SWORD reveals that models often depend on distributional familiarity and have notably weaker factual verification abilities in some languages, especially (East) Asian ones, highlighting the need for more diagnostic, distortion-based evaluations.

Abstract: Modern LLMs demonstrate impressive multilingual performance, yet standard benchmarks primarily reward selecting correct answers rather than evaluating genuine factual understanding. We introduce Systematic Wikidata-based Object-Relation Distortion (SWORD), a benchmark that evaluates whether models consistently reject factual errors across languages. SWORD generates syntactically well-formed but factually incorrect statements in eight widely spoken languages through controlled perturbations of Wikidata triples, ranging from random entity substitutions to semantically plausible property-based selections. Our distortion-based evaluation surfaces two critical insights that remain entirely obscured by conventional benchmarks. First, models counterintuitively achieve higher accuracy on semantically plausible distortions than on nonsensical random substitutions, suggesting reliance on distributional familiarity rather than genuine factual verification. Second, models exhibiting comparable baseline accuracy across languages show substantial performance degradation specifically on (East) Asian languages when presented with distorted statements, with cross-lingual performance gaps reaching up to 28 percentage points (49\% relative reduction) in some models. These findings demonstrate that multilingual factual reasoning involves asymmetric capabilities that aggregate accuracy metrics systematically obscure.

</details>


### [5] [Auditable Emergency Triage for Maternal and Newborn Care in India](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09356)
*Shobhit Jagga,Aman Dalmia,Niharika Priyadarshini,Neelima Devadas,Amrita K Prasen,Nikhil Nalin,Santhosh SJ,Sreeram Nurani Ramasubramanian,Muhammed Afeer K,Anubhav Arora*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They improved WhatsApp-based medical emergency triage by splitting it into LLM symptom extraction plus a deterministic rule engine, boosting accuracy and auditability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The existing LLM-only triage system was opaque, hard to debug, and risky to update. Clinicians had an implicit decision tree that was never encoded, and current prompts relied on a flat danger-sign list, making it difficult to trace errors, avoid regressions, and ensure safe, scalable emergency identification in 50k+ monthly queries.

Method: They decomposed emergency triage into two stages: (1) an LLM maps each free-text caregiver query into canonical symptoms and patient context using a clinician-authored vocabulary; (2) a hand-crafted deterministic rule engine, reflecting clinical decision pathways, determines whether the case is an emergency. This separation lets experts inspect each component (query interpretation, symptom extraction, context inference, and rule coverage) and iteratively add or refine rules without retraining the model or re-running full evaluations.

Result: The new system increased emergency detection performance (recall from 0.565 to 0.810, F1 from 0.606 to 0.702). Most gains came from the structured rules. Since deployment it has triaged 152,421 queries, flagging 28,535 (18.7%) as emergencies, with a 17.8% over-escalation rate and no rise in missed emergencies. Clinicians have authored 48 additional rules post-deployment, demonstrating easier, safer updates.

Conclusion: Decomposing LLM-based clinical triage into interpretable symptom extraction plus a deterministic rule engine yields better accuracy, safety, and maintainability than an end-to-end LLM classifier. This architecture surfaces specific failure modes, enables targeted rule updates without costly evaluations, and creates a faster learning and correction loop for clinicians operating a large-scale digital health service.

Abstract: At Noora Health, our nurses answer more than 50,000 medical queries per month on our WhatsApp-based service that provides caregivers with on-demand support. Their most time-critical task is emergency triage: deciding which queries need immediate in-person attention. To support them, we built a system that uses a large language model (LLM) to classify whether a message is an emergency and provide a rationale for interpretability. But the system was opaque: analyzing mistakes meant reading reasoning chains for each message, which is infeasible at our scale. Prompt changes meant re-running a full evaluation to prevent regressions, which was both costly and operationally challenging. Clinicians follow a decision tree to make this call, but it was never documented or passed to the model, which relied on a flat list of danger signs. To address these issues, we decomposed triage into two steps: an LLM extracts canonical symptoms and patient context from the query using a clinician-authored vocabulary, and a deterministic rule engine captures the scenarios that indicate an emergency. We show that the new system raised recall from 0.565 to 0.810 and F1 from 0.606 to 0.702, with structured rules driving most of the accuracy gains while the decomposition provides auditability: clinical experts can inspect each stage of the new system to see whether the query was mistranslated, symptoms were incorrectly extracted, patient context was wrongly inferred, or the necessary rules were missing. They can add new rules independently without causing regressions and avoid running costly evaluations. Since deployment, the new system has triaged 152,421 patient queries and flagged 28,535 (18.7%) as emergencies. The over-escalation rate has been 17.8%, without any increase in missed emergencies. Clinicians have also added 48 new rules since deployment, evidence of the faster correction loop we set out to build.

</details>


### [6] [Do LLMs Make More Mistakes If They Do Not Believe the Input Data?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09363)
*Peter Kochelka,Aleš Manuel Papáček,Vojtěch Dvořák,Ondřej Dušek*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how much large language models stick to provided context when it conflicts with their internal knowledge, focusing on local Czech/Slovak facts and multiple languages, and finds only a weak conflict plus strong sensitivity to the choice of LLM judge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often hallucinate or override given context with their own parametric knowledge, which is problematic for retrieval-augmented generation and data-to-text tasks. The authors want to understand when and how a model’s internal knowledge conflicts with externally provided information (context-memory conflict), especially in scenarios where the context contains local or low-resource knowledge that the model might not fully know. They also aim to see whether non-English and low-resource languages exacerbate these issues and how to reliably measure faithfulness using LLM-based evaluators.

Method: They construct RDF triples describing local Czech and Slovak knowledge and categorize them as factual (aligned with world knowledge), counterfactual (intentionally contradicting world knowledge), or fictional (invented). Using these triples as input, they have LLMs generate text in four languages: English, Czech, Slovak, and Upper Sorbian. They then evaluate how faithfully the generated texts follow the input triples, using both human annotation and LLM-based judges (notably Kimi K3). They compare faithfulness scores across factual vs. counterfactual vs. fictional conditions and also investigate how different choices of LLM judge affect the observed strength of context-memory conflict.

Result: Empirically, they find that LLMs remain fairly faithful to the provided RDF context even when it contradicts their internal knowledge; the expected strong context-memory conflict is not prominent in human-annotated samples. For the Kimi K3 LLM judge, which matches human judgments reasonably well, counterfactual triples yield faithfulness scores only slightly lower than factual triples (a decrease of about 0.05 on a 1–5 scale). However, when using other, less suitable LLM judges, the apparent context-memory conflict becomes exaggerated, suggesting that evaluation choice can significantly distort conclusions.

Conclusion: The study concludes that LLMs may be more willing to follow provided context than previously assumed, even when it contradicts their internal knowledge, at least in the tested multilingual and local-knowledge settings. The hypothesized strong context-memory conflict appears weak under careful human and Kimi K3-based evaluation. Crucially, the work highlights that selecting an inappropriate LLM judge can lead to overestimating the severity of context-memory conflict, underscoring the need for validated evaluators in faithfulness studies.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are prone to hallucinating or misinterpreting facts, which impairs their usability in retrieval-augmented generation or data-to-text systems. We analyse how faithfulness of LLMs to provided context depends on how plausible they perceive the context to be (context-memory conflict). To better identify error patterns, we make use of the increased difficulty of non-English and low-resource language text generation and input data based on local knowledge, only partially captured in models' parametric knowledge. We let the models generate text in English, Czech, Slovak and Upper Sorbian from factual (FA), counterfactual (CFA) and fictional (FI) RDF triples containing local Czech and Slovak data. Contrary to our expectations, we observe only a weak context-memory conflict on the human-annotated sample. For Kimi K3 as an LLM judge, which agrees well with human annotations on the sample, counterfactual inputs receive only slightly lower faithfulness scores than factual ones (-0.05 on a 1-5 scale). We also find that a suboptimal choice of LLM judge would lead to overestimating the strength of the context-memory conflict.

</details>


### [7] [Benchmarking Hybrid Deep Research Across Database Querying and Web Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09410)
*Ruofan Wu,Peiran Xu,Xiaolong Li,Fan Shu,Soyoung Yoon,Yite Wang,Xiaodong Yu,Boyi Liu,Feng Yan,Debiao Li,Yuxiong He,Zhewei Yao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces HybridDeepResearch, a benchmark requiring both web search and SQL for deep research tasks, revealing current agents struggle to maintain constraints across structured and unstructured data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing autonomous research agents and benchmarks treat web search and database querying separately, ignoring real-world tasks that require combining unstructured web text with structured relational data while preserving constraints during handoffs between tools.

Method: Construct a 380-task benchmark (HybridDeepResearch) grounded in LiveSQLBench-Base-Lite databases and public web sources, categorized into three reasoning patterns (SQL2S, S2SQL, Parallel), with tasks validated via automated checks and human review; then evaluate multiple proprietary and open-weight LMs under varied agentic scaffolds and measure Pass@8 performance.

Result: State-of-the-art models such as GLM-5.2, Claude-Sonnet-4.6, and GPT-5 achieve only about 50–54% Pass@8 on the hardest tasks, and analyses show that directional reasoning between SQL and web (SQL2S and S2SQL) is significantly harder than parallel intersection of evidence.

Conclusion: Bridging structured and unstructured information while preserving constraints is still a major open challenge; HybridDeepResearch offers a first benchmark to systematically evaluate and drive progress on multi-environment deep research agents, with code and data publicly released.

Abstract: While autonomous agents have made significant strides in "deep research" by iteratively navigating the open web to synthesize information, real-world problem-solving is rarely confined to a single environment. Complex analytical tasks inherently require agents to weave together evidence from both ambiguous unstructured text (e.g., the open web) and highly precise structured data (e.g., relational databases). However, existing benchmarks evaluate these modalities in isolation, failing to capture the critical "handoff" - the ability to preserve constraints when moving evidence between systems. We introduce HybridDeepResearch, to our knowledge the first deep-research benchmark that requires both web search and SQL to form a complete, verifiable answer. The benchmark contains 380 tool-dependent tasks grounded in LiveSQLBench-Base-Lite databases and public web corpora, validated through automated checks and human review, and covering three reasoning patterns: SQL2S, S2SQL, and Parallel. Evaluations across proprietary and open-weight models under various agentic scaffolds reveal that even state-of-the-art models like GLM-5.2, Claude-Sonnet-4.6 and GPT-5 achieve only about 50-54% Pass@8 on the hard subset. Notably, results show that directional reasoning is substantially more difficult than parallel intersection, highlighting that bridging structured and unstructured information spaces without losing constraints remains a major open challenge for agentic systems. Code and datasets are publicly available at GitHub (https://github.com/Snowflake-AI-Research/HybridDeepResearch) and Hugging Face (https://huggingface.co/datasets/Snowflake/HybridDeepResearch).

</details>


### [8] [Edu-QuRating: Multi-Dimensional Educational Data Curation with Distilled Pairwise Judgements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09425)
*Oliver G. B. Garrod,Robin A. A. Ince,Meng Liu,Mohamed Huti,Moritz Boos,Amy Waldock,Dominic Andrews,Paul Atherton*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build an LLM-based pipeline (Edu-QuRating) that scores educational texts along multiple rubrics, then show it helps both data filtering for pretraining and reward design for post-training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing educational data filters usually collapse “educational value” into a single scalar score, which is too coarse when you care about different aspects such as accuracy, engagement, structure, and audience appropriateness, especially on already education-heavy corpora. The authors want a way to score and curate data along multiple explicit pedagogical dimensions so that resources can be tailored to specific educational applications and downstream training procedures.

Method: They adapt the QuRating framework to education: define education-specific rubrics and criteria; use a strong LLM (GPT‑4.1‑mini) as a judge to label pairwise preferences between documents along each criterion; then distill these pairwise judgements into trainable “Edu‑QuRater” models (sequence classifiers) that can assign scalar scores to individual text chunks on each educational dimension. They evaluate these scorers by how well they reproduce held‑out LLM‑judge preferences. Then they apply them in two ways: (1) score ~322M documents from FineWeb‑Edu‑Fortified and filter/weight them to create a new pretraining mixture for small LMs, comparing performance to a FineWeb‑Edu baseline across nine benchmarks; (2) use Edu‑QuRater scores as components in a GRPO reward function to post‑train a Qwen3‑4B model, and evaluate via held‑out LLM‑judge pairwise comparisons on pedagogical quality and instruction following.

Result: The best Edu‑QuRater sequence classifier reproduces held‑out GPT‑4.1‑mini pairwise judgements with a mean accuracy of 0.917 across six educational criteria and two backbone models. For corpus filtering, small LMs pretrained on Edu‑QuRating‑filtered mixtures achieve higher aggregate accuracy across nine benchmarks than those trained on the unfiltered FineWeb‑Edu mixture, though improvements are task-dependent. For GRPO post‑training, combining Edu‑QuRater rewards with answer-structure rewards yields model responses preferred over the Qwen3‑4B base model on both pedagogical and instruction-following dimensions in LLM-judge pairwise evaluations.

Conclusion: Multi-dimensional, rubric-based educational scoring distilled into compact Edu‑QuRater models can closely match strong LLM‑judge preferences and is practically useful: it improves pretraining data selection for small LMs and serves as an effective reward signal for RL-style post-training. Treating educational quality as a rich vector of criteria, rather than a single scalar, leads to measurable gains in downstream task performance and pedagogical response quality.

Abstract: Educational data filters have become a practical way to improve language-model pre-training, but most filters treat educational value as a single scalar property. This may be too broad for some applications, especially if the data set already features a high density of educational material. Useful learning material needs to be accurate, engaging, well structured, and appropriate for the intended audience and application (e.g. learner- vs teacher-facing). Following QuRating (Wettig et al. 2024), we introduce Edu-QuRating: a pipeline for multi-dimensional educational data scoring and curation. Edu-QuRating defines education-specific rubrics, uses an LLM judge to label sampled document pairs and distills those pairwise preferences into reusable Edu-QuRaters, which can score individual text chunks on a set of educational criteria. Across two sequence-classification base models and six educational criteria, the best Edu-QuRater recovers held-out GPT-4.1-mini pairwise judgements with mean accuracy 0.917. We then apply the resulting scorers in two applications. First, we investigate the potential of Edu-QuRaters for corpus filtering to improve pretraining of small language models. We scored 322.25M FineWeb-Edu-Fortified documents to obtain a filtered pre-training mixture. In matched single-run pre-training comparisons, models trained with Edu-QuRating-based mixtures reached higher observed aggregate accuracy across nine benchmarks than the FineWeb-Edu baseline, with gains concentrated in particular tasks. Second, we used Edu-QuRater scores as reward terms for GRPO post-training. In held-out pairwise judge evaluations, combining Edu-QuRater and answer-structure rewards produced responses preferred to the Qwen3-4B base model on both pedagogical quality and instruction following.

</details>


### [9] [The Mutations of Machine Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09496)
*Mauricio Figueroa*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how algorithmic systems transform “speech” across three stages—search, social media, and generative conversational AI—and explains how law not only reacts to but shapes these transformations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify and systematize the fragmented debate around algorithmic speech, showing how legal frameworks actively constitute the conditions, forms, and power of algorithmic outputs in digital environments that organize social life.

Method: Conceptual and doctrinal analysis: the author traces historically and analytically three “mutations” of machine speech (search engines, social media engagement, and generative conversational systems), connecting each to its underlying legal architectures and to scholarship on free expression, privacy, and communication.

Result: The paper identifies three distinct mutations of machine speech—speech-as-data (search), speech-as-engagement (social media), and speech-as-conversation (generative AI)—and shows for each how law, corporate design, and socio-technical practices intertwine to produce new regimes of visibility, attention, and knowledge production.

Conclusion: Law is not merely a reactive constraint on algorithmic systems but a constitutive force in how algorithmic speech emerges, operates, and acquires power; by offering a structured conceptual framework, the paper equips legal and interdisciplinary audiences to better understand, critique, and regulate the evolving landscape of algorithmic outputs.

Abstract: Algorithmic outputs now populate the digital environments through which contemporary life is organized. The role of law in facilitating and constituting (rather than merely responding to) these processes is gaining increasing traction across scholarly accounts. This inquiry traces the evolution of algorithmic outputs attending to their legal underpinnings and social implications, surfacing the mutations of machine speech.
  The first mutation redefined speech as data to be queried: search engines transformed the web from a space of information retrieval into an economic regime of algorithmic visibility. The second mutation reframed speech as engagement: social media platforms fused moderation with amplification, turning expression into a metric of attention, governed by corporate architectures. The third mutation emerges in conversational systems and interfaces, where generative text displaces information retrieval, bringing with it dense technolegal entanglements and profound epistemic consequences.
  Scholars of freedom of expression, informational privacy, and communication studies have long grappled with these dynamics, yet their implications for broader legal thought have also become urgent. This piece seeks to organize and clarify the evolving debate around algorithmic speech, making this critical but often fragmented discourse more accessible to wider legal and interdisciplinary audiences. In doing so, it bridges the gap between observing technological transformation and critically assessing the constitutive role of law within it, offering a conceptual resource for researchers, students, policymakers, and practitioners navigating and contesting this evolving landscape.

</details>


### [10] [TEFM: Token-Efficient Faithful Modeling for Structured Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09552)
*Zhichao Hou,Lingdao Sha,Xueyu Mao,Yang Liu,Peijie Qiu,Rui Song*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes TEFM, a token-efficient and faithful modeling framework for structured data with LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are hard to apply in critical domains because they are expensive in tokens and often provide unfaithful or post-hoc rationales. The paper aims to jointly address token efficiency and faithfulness when analyzing structured data in areas like healthcare and security.

Method: Introduce TEFM (Token-Efficient Faithful Modeling), which compresses long structured observations into compact Behavioral Code tokens to save tokens, and trains with a dual-fidelity objective that enforces both accurate reconstruction of the original data from codes and high prediction-level fidelity, leading to minimal sufficient feature subsets as rationales.

Result: Across multiple structured-domain datasets and several LLM backbones (Qwen3, Gemma-2, Phi-4), TEFM matches or closely approaches standard classification accuracy while using only about 1–2% of the original tokens, and simultaneously yields faithful rationales tied to the input data.

Conclusion: TEFM demonstrates that it is possible to make LLMs both token-efficient and faithful for structured data analysis in critical domains, by compressing inputs into Behavioral Code tokens and optimizing a dual-fidelity objective, achieving strong accuracy, huge token savings, and faithful explanations.

Abstract: In this paper, we solve two fundamental obstacles in applying LLMs to critical domains: token efficiency and faithfulness. To address both constraints jointly, we present TEFM (Token-Efficient Faithful Modeling), a framework designed for structured data analysis in critical domains. TEFM achieves token efficiency by compressing lengthy structured observations into compact Behavioral Code tokens, dramatically reducing token consumption with minimal information loss. Moreover, TEFM enables faithful rationalization through a dual-fidelity objective that jointly optimizes code-level reconstruction and prediction-level fidelity, identifying minimal sufficient feature subsets grounded in input data. Comprehensive experiments across various domain datasets and model backbones (Qwen3, Gemma-2, Phi-4) show that TEFM achieves competitive classification accuracy with dramatic token reduction (approximately 1\% token retention in clinical and 2\% in security domains) while producing faithful rationales.

</details>


### [11] [BuzzASR: A Swarm of 100+ Monolingual Speech Recognition Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09554)
*Shivam Singh,Aditya Yadavalli,Catherine Arnett,Alex Warstadt*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BuzzASR fine-tunes Whisper into 102 language-specialized ASR models, greatly improving accuracy—especially for low-resource languages—and releases them open source.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilingual Whisper models underperform on low-resource and underrepresented languages because their training is dominated by high-resource languages. Although simple monolingual fine-tuning can adapt models effectively, it has only been done for a few languages. The paper aims to systematically and massively scale language-specific adaptation to many languages and explore more advanced adaptation methods to further improve ASR for these languages.

Method: Starting from Whisper (particularly Whisper-large-v3), the authors perform language-specific adaptation for 102 languages in the FLEURS dataset. They use two strategies: (1) straightforward fine-tuning on monolingual speech-text data; and (2) a more advanced approach that replaces Whisper’s multilingual BPE tokenizer with language-specific tokenizers and applies data augmentation via text-only fine-tuning. They then evaluate the resulting BuzzASR models on FLEURS and Common Voice test sets, focusing on character error rate (CER) and tokenization efficiency (compression rate, measured as characters per token).

Result: BuzzASR models outperform Whisper-large-v3 on 77 of the 102 languages, achieving more than a 2.8× average reduction in CER. They reach state-of-the-art CER among open-source systems on 27 languages across FLEURS and Common Voice tests. The tokenizer replacement approach significantly improves tokenization efficiency, with an average 3.3× better compression rate than Whisper’s multilingual BPE and up to 21.7× for some languages.

Conclusion: Massively scaling monolingual fine-tuning of a strong multilingual ASR model like Whisper is highly effective for language-specialized ASR, particularly for underrepresented languages. Combining this with language-specific tokenizer replacement and text-only data augmentation yields substantial accuracy and tokenization-efficiency gains. The work demonstrates that relatively simple adaptation strategies, applied at scale, can set new open-source benchmarks for many languages. All models, code, and detailed benchmarks are publicly released as BuzzASR.

Abstract: We introduce BuzzASR, a collection of language-specialized fine-tuned Whisper models adapted for automatic speech recognition (ASR) in 102 languages. Large end-to-end Transformer-based ASR models such as Whisper have revolutionized ASR, but most prominent models are highly multilingual. As a result, these models often perform poorly on languages less well-represented in their training set. While it has long been known that effective language adaptation can be achieved through simple fine-tuning on monolingual data, this strategy has only been applied to a small number of languages. We massively scale up this simple approach to 102 languages covered in the FLEURS dataset, while also implementing a more complex language adaptation strategy that integrates monolingual tokenizer replacement and data augmentation using text-only fine-tuning. BuzzASR models outperform Whisper-large-v3 on 77 out of 102 languages, reducing character error rates (CER) by a factor of over 2.8 on average. Our models achieve state-of-the-art CER among open-source systems on 27 of 102 languages on the combined FLEURS and Common Voice test set. Our tokenizer replacement strategy yields an average 3.3x improvement in compression rate (characters per token) over Whisper's multilingual BPE, with gains of up to 21.7x. We release all models, code, and detailed results: https://lemn-lab.github.io/buzz-asr

</details>


### [12] [Towards Automatic Evolution Tree Generation from Citation Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09561)
*Zexing Zhao,Yuntong Hu,Liang Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EvoTree is a three-stage framework for automatically inducing time-aware taxonomies of AI subfields that better handle transitional papers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual and survey-based taxonomies of AI subfields cannot keep up with the rapid publication rate, and existing automatic taxonomy-induction methods are not time-aware and mostly attach papers only to leaves, causing errors for transitional or foundational work.

Method: EvoTree first learns a stable conceptual backbone using a graph-aware encoder and distribution-based hierarchical clustering. Then it performs temporal refinement that re-attaches marginal or transitional papers to internal nodes under monotonic-path constraints. Finally, a large language model assigns human-readable labels to the discovered concepts without changing the learned structure. The authors also construct an annotated benchmark across 11 AI subfields for evaluation.

Result: EvoTree outperforms baseline methods in normalized mutual information (NMI), citation-direction accuracy, and concept purity on the new annotated benchmark, and it is uniquely effective at detecting marginal papers that do not cleanly belong in terminal categories.

Conclusion: EvoTree provides a more accurate, temporally consistent taxonomy of AI subfields than prior methods, especially in handling transitional and marginal papers, and the accompanying benchmark establishes a standard for evaluating such approaches.

Abstract: Surveys remain the primary way researchers grasp the lineage of methods within an AI subfield, but they scale poorly against the current rate of publication. Existing taxonomy-induction methods are largely leaf-bound and time-agnostic; they tend to force transitional papers into mature leaves and can create topological inversions between ancestors and descendants. We propose EvoTree, a staged framework that decouples conceptual backbone learning from temporal refinement: a graph-aware encoder with distribution-based hierarchical clustering yields a stable taxonomy backbone; temporal fine-tuning then re-attaches marginal papers to internal nodes under monotonic-path constraints; a final LLM pass labels concepts without altering the topology. We release the first annotated benchmark for this task across 11 AI subfields. EvoTree attains the highest NMI and citation-direction accuracy among all baselines and the best concept purity on the annotated benchmark, and is the only method with non-trivial marginal-paper detection on the annotated set.

</details>


### [13] [Reproducing Omitted Temporal Expressions in Japanese News for Retrieval-Augmented Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09569)
*Tomoaki Yasuda,Shotaro Ishihara*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces jaROTE, a rule-based system that restores omitted temporal expressions in Japanese news by grounding them to concrete dates using the article’s publication date, improving retrieval and RAG stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Omitted or underspecified temporal expressions (like “on the 3rd” without a month or year) in news articles cause temporal mismatches and unstable interpretation when such texts are indexed or used in search and retrieval-augmented generation with LLMs. Existing pipelines typically index the text as-is, ignoring that readers and writers implicitly rely on the publication date to resolve such expressions. This leads to degraded time-sensitive retrieval and inconsistent temporal reasoning by LLMs. There is thus a need for a practical method that normalizes temporal expressions with awareness of publication dates, especially in Japanese news where such omissions are prevalent.

Method: The authors design jaROTE, a rule-based pipeline for Japanese news that takes each article’s publication date as external context and uses it to restore omitted temporal expressions. They build on existing temporal-expression extraction and normalization techniques, then add rules informed by manual analysis of Japanese news to determine how relative or partial dates (day-only, month-only, vague references) should be grounded to concrete calendar dates or intervals. The system is implemented as a preprocessing step so that articles with normalized dates are then indexed for search and RAG applications.

Result: On two Japanese news corpora, jaROTE achieves strong performance in reproducing omitted temporal expressions, with accuracy comparable to or competitive with large language models while being faster and cheaper. The experiments also show that applying jaROTE before indexing improves performance in time-constrained lexical retrieval tasks, indicating better alignment between queries and documents when temporal expressions are concretized using publication dates.

Conclusion: Publication-date-grounded temporal normalization is both feasible and beneficial for Japanese news processing. A carefully engineered rule-based system like jaROTE can match LLM-level performance at much lower computational cost, and its use as a preprocessing step enhances time-sensitive retrieval quality. This suggests that similar approaches could be valuable in other languages and RAG or search settings where temporal omissions are common.

Abstract: News articles often contain omitted temporal expressions, such as day-only or month-only mentions, which must be interpreted with reference to the publication date. When such articles are indexed or processed as standalone text in search and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems, these omissions can cause temporal mismatches and unstable interpretation by large language models. We focus on reproducing omitted temporal expressions as concrete dates or intervals using the publication date as external context before the articles are indexed for search and RAG applications. Specifically, building on established temporal-expression extraction and normalization techniques and informed by a manual analysis of Japanese news articles, we propose jaROTE, a rule-based pipeline for Japanese news. Experiments on two news corpora demonstrate that jaROTE achieves high performance, and remains competitive with LLMs while providing a fast, low-cost pipeline. We further show that temporal reproduction improves time-constrained lexical retrieval, demonstrating the practical value of publication-date-grounded normalization for Japanese news retrieval.

</details>


### [14] [Beyond Top Words: MonoTM for Topic Modeling with Interpretable Monosemantic Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09575)
*Una Joh,Bei Yu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MonoTM is a topic modeling framework that uses sparse autoencoders to separate the tasks of document-topic mixture estimation and human-interpretable topic description, yielding more meaningful topic semantics than word lists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional topic models rely on top-ranked words to summarize topics, which often inadequately capture topic semantics. Sparse autoencoders can extract interpretable features from dense representations, but the connection between these features' interpretability and the quality of topic inference is not well understood. There is a need for a framework that leverages SAEs to improve both mixture estimation and semantic interpretability while clarifying their distinct requirements.

Method: The authors design MonoTM, which employs a sparse autoencoder to derive a high-dimensional bag-of-features representation for documents. Document–topic mixtures are inferred using the full set of SAE features. Separately, with the mixture assignments held fixed, the model learns topic descriptors over a distinct vocabulary consisting of corpus-grounded semantic features, rather than raw words. They empirically compare different SAE configurations and feature subsets across three benchmark corpora to see how they respectively support mixture estimation vs. interpretation.

Result: Experiments on three benchmark corpora show that the optimal SAE settings and feature subsets for accurately estimating document–topic mixtures differ from those that yield the most interpretable semantic features. MonoTM, by decoupling these roles, can maintain high-quality topic mixture estimates using the full SAE representation while learning more meaningful, human-interpretable topic descriptors from a separate semantic feature vocabulary.

Conclusion: MonoTM successfully separates the objectives of topic inference and semantic interpretation in topic modeling. By using sparse autoencoder features for mixture estimation and a different set of corpus-grounded semantic units for topic description, it preserves global topic structure and produces topic representations that are more semantically rich and useful for downstream corpus analysis than conventional word-based topic models.

Abstract: Topic models summarize large text corpora, but top-ranked words often provide only a limited representation of topic semantics. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) offer a way to move beyond word-level descriptors by extracting interpretable features from dense representations, yet how feature interpretability relates to topic-inference quality remains unclear. We introduce \textbf{MonoTM}, an interpretable topic modeling framework that decouples these roles. Across three benchmark corpora, we show that document--topic mixture estimation and semantic interpretation favor different SAE configurations and feature subsets. MonoTM estimates mixtures from the full SAE bag-of-features representation and, with them fixed, learns topic descriptors over a separate vocabulary of corpus-grounded semantic features. This design preserves global topic structure while representing topics with semantic units more meaningful than individual words, making them more useful for downstream corpus analysis.

</details>


### [15] [SEA-SpeechBench: A Large-Scale Multitask Benchmark for Speech Understanding Across Southeast Asia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09672)
*Jingyi Liao,Wenyu Zhang,Zhuohan Liu,Yingxu He,Geyu Lin,Xunlong Zou,Shuo Sun,Syed Ali Redha Alsagoff,Ai Ti Aw*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces SEA-SpeechBench, the first large-scale, multitask speech understanding benchmark for 11 Southeast Asian languages, highlighting significant performance gaps in current audio and multimodal LLMs, especially for temporal understanding, emotion recognition, speech translation, and low-resource language prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing audio and multimodal large language models are primarily evaluated on English, leaving Southeast Asian languages underrepresented and making it difficult to understand model performance and limitations for these languages and tasks. A comprehensive, multilingual, and multitask benchmark is needed to drive inclusive model development and fair evaluation.

Method: The authors constructed SEA-SpeechBench, a large-scale benchmark comprising 97,194 audio-text samples (597 hours) across 99 evaluation sets in 11 Southeast Asian languages. The benchmark covers 9 tasks in 3 categories: (1) speech processing (ASR, speech translation, spoken QA), (2) paralinguistic analysis (emotion, gender, age, speaker recognition), and (3) a novel temporal understanding category involving timestamped content queries and temporal localization in up-to-3-minute audio clips. They also implement multilingual prompting in both native SEA languages and English and evaluate a range of open-source and proprietary audio/multimodal LLMs on the benchmark.

Result: Evaluation of leading open-source and proprietary systems on SEA-SpeechBench reveals substantial performance gaps. Models perform particularly poorly on temporal understanding, emotion recognition, and speech translation tasks. Furthermore, prompting in low-resource languages like Burmese and Tamil yields up to 41 percentage points lower performance compared to English prompts, indicating a strong language resource bias in current systems.

Conclusion: SEA-SpeechBench exposes key limitations of current audio and multimodal LLMs for Southeast Asian languages, especially in challenging tasks and low-resource settings. The benchmark demonstrates that existing models are far from robust or equitable across languages and tasks, underscoring the need for more inclusive, multilingual model training and evaluation. The resource is publicly released to catalyze future research and improvement in SEA speech understanding.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of audio and multimodal large language models has unlocked transformative speech understanding capabilities, yet evaluation frameworks remain predominantly English-centric, leaving Southeast Asian (SEA) languages critically underrepresented. We introduce SEA-SpeechBench, to the best of our knowledge, the first large-scale multitask benchmark that evaluates speech understanding in 11 SEA languages through 97,194 samples across 99 evaluation sets and 597 hours of curated audio data. Our benchmark comprises 9 diverse tasks across 3 categories: speech processing (automatic speech recognition, speech translation, spoken question answering), paralinguistic analysis (emotion, gender, age, speaker recognition), and temporal understanding, a novel dimension featuring timestamped content queries and temporal localization within extended audio sequences up to 3 minutes. We implement multilingual prompting in both native SEA languages and English to reflect user interactions with audio-language models. Evaluation of leading open-source and proprietary systems reveals marked performance gaps. Across all models, performance remains underwhelming on temporal understanding, emotion recognition, and speech translation. Prompting in low-resource languages such as Burmese and Tamil lags behind English by up to 41 percentage points. Our findings expose critical model limitations and underscore the need for inclusive model development. The SEA-SpeechBench benchmark is available at https://zwenyu.github.io/SEA-SpeechBench/.

</details>


### [16] [X2-NativeCursor: Native-Token Text Progress Tracking for Incremental-Text Streaming Codec TTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09677)
*Zehan Liu,Carl Chen,Rime Wen,Kaiqi Fu,Altman Lin,Shawn Qin,Lights Shi,Roy Gan,Hao Wang,Qian Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose X2-NativeCursor, a lightweight token-level progress tracker for streaming TTS that aligns text with speech before waveform decoding, achieving much lower tracking error and latency than waveform-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Streaming TTS systems need to know how far along the speech is in the input text for features like synchronized highlighting, interruption, and dialogue management. Existing methods typically rely on waveform-based alignment, which either needs the full audio or adds extra acoustic processing during streaming, causing delay and computational overhead. There is a need for an efficient, low-latency, and accurate way to track speech progress online without modifying the main TTS generator.

Method: They introduce X2-NativeCursor, an external 'observer' that operates on native speech tokens emitted by the TTS model before waveform decoding. First, a normalization plan maps spoken labels (e.g., tokenized or normalized text units) back to spans in the original text. Then, separate encoders for text and native speech tokens feed a local matching module that estimates which label position is currently being spoken. An additional output rule converts these potentially noisy, revisable position estimates into a monotonic cursor that never moves backward. The observer is trained separately and can be attached to different TTS backbones like Qwen3-TTS or CosyVoice2 without changing them.

Result: On Chinese streaming TTS with Qwen3-TTS, X2-NativeCursor achieves a mean absolute error of 0.151 Chinese characters using only 80 ms lookahead, while an online waveform-based baseline has 1.253 characters error even with 320 ms lookahead. The alignment real-time factor is also reduced from 0.3598 (baseline) to 0.0180, indicating much lower computational cost. Similar advantages and low tracking error are observed when evaluated against an alternative automatic alignment reference. The method is also successfully adapted to CosyVoice2 by training backbone-specific observers.

Conclusion: Token-level observers like X2-NativeCursor can provide accurate, low-latency online progress tracking for streaming TTS without changing the underlying generator or performing heavy waveform-based alignment. This enables better synchronized highlighting and interaction features while significantly reducing computational overhead, and the approach generalizes across different TTS backbones.

Abstract: Incremental-text streaming text-to-speech (TTS) needs online text progress tracking for synchronized highlighting, interruption handling, and dialogue-history updates. Input text arrives before it is spoken, so text arrival alone cannot indicate speech progress. Existing waveform-based alignment requires complete audio or adds acoustic processing during streaming. We propose X2-NativeCursor, a lightweight observer that tracks progress from native speech tokens before waveform decoding without changing the TTS generator. Its normalization plan links spoken labels to their original-text spans. Text and native-token encoders feed a local matcher that estimates the current label position. A separate output rule converts revisable position estimates into a cursor that never moves backward. Mean absolute error against an automatic reference is 0.151 Chinese characters with 80-ms lookahead, versus 1.253 characters with 320-ms lookahead for an online waveform baseline. Alignment real-time factor also decreases from 0.3598 to 0.0180 relative to this baseline. Lower tracking error is retained under a second automatic alignment reference. We evaluate X2-NativeCursor on Qwen3-TTS and validate its adaptation to CosyVoice2 by training a separate observer for each backbone. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/X-Square-Robot/X2Streaming-TTS.

</details>


### [17] [Which Medical Questions Deserve Rationales? Perturbation-Sensitive Selection for Robust QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09684)
*Yuexin Wu,Dayou Yu,Vasile Rus*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They study how best to select which existing, labeled medical QA questions should receive costly rationale annotations under a fixed token budget, proposing a robustness-based selection method (RMS-RSP) and finding modest accuracy gains but clearer improvements in robustness and consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-quality rationales for medical QA are expensive and scarce, but most prior work assumes we choose which questions to label from scratch. Here, all questions are already answer-labeled; the practical question is which ones should additionally get rationale supervision given limited annotation tokens, and whether smarter selection can improve performance and robustness over random choice.

Method: They define an offline selection setting where candidate rationales are visible only to the selector. They introduce RMS-RSP, which perturbs hidden representations at rationale tokens and measures changes in the margin between the gold and best distractor answers. Questions whose rationales induce large boundary sensitivity are prioritized for rationale supervision. They compare RMS-RSP against ten non-robustness-based selectors and a full-supervision baseline across five medical QA datasets using the MedGemma-4B-IT model and multiple random seeds.

Result: Under fixed rationale-token budgets, RMS-RSP slightly outperforms random selection in standard accuracy (60.61% vs. 60.08% on average) but achieves statistically significant gains only on AfriMed-QA. Its full-budget accuracy area is comparable to random. However, when evaluating robustness to answer-option reorderings and semantic consistency, RMS-RSP yields consistent gains (about +1.9 and +2.9 points on average, respectively) across all datasets. Using all available rationales boosts macro accuracy to 63.74% but requires vastly more tokens and does not consistently improve robustness.

Conclusion: RMS-RSP does not provide universal accuracy improvements over random rationale selection, but it does systematically enhance robustness and semantic consistency under formatting perturbations. The work suggests that selecting examples based on rationale-local decision-boundary sensitivity is a promising criterion for deciding where to spend limited rationale-supervision budget, especially when robustness rather than raw accuracy is the main objective.

Abstract: Medical question-answering datasets often contain answer labels, whereas high-quality rationales remain scarce, noisy, or costly to validate. This changes the acquisition question: rather than asking which questions should be labeled, we ask which already-labeled questions should receive rationale supervision under a fixed token budget. We study an offline version of this problem in which candidate rationales are visible to the selector but withheld from downstream training unless selected. We propose root-mean-square Robustness-based Sample Prioritization (RMS-RSP), which perturbs hidden states only at rationale tokens and measures the resulting shift in the gold-versus-best-distractor margin. Across five medical QA datasets, MedGemma-4B-IT, three training seeds, ten budgeted non-RSP selectors, and an unbudgeted full-supervision reference, RMS-RSP provides a deliberately qualified result. Its locked-budget accuracy is 60.61% on average versus 60.08% for Random, with a statistically resolved gain only on AfriMed-QA (+1.44 points). Its full-budget accuracy area is not better than Random. However, after three answer-option reorderings, RMS-RSP improves robust accuracy and semantic consistency by 1.91 and 2.85 points on average, respectively, with the same direction on all five datasets. Training on every pool rationale raises macro accuracy to 63.74%, but consumes 29--254 times more rationale tokens and does not uniformly improve robustness. These findings do not establish universal accuracy gains; they instead suggest that rationale-local boundary sensitivity can identify supervision that improves invariance to semantically equivalent formatting changes.

</details>


### [18] [Looped GPT-BERT: Trading Parameters for Computation in Small Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09691)
*Tingshuo Fan,Hongtao Mu,Tianyu Zhou,Hansen Liu,Tao Ji*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates parameter-efficient language modeling via depth-wise parameter sharing (looping) in a GPT-BERT architecture under the BabyLM 2026 Strict-small benchmark, showing comparable performance to larger baselines with far fewer parameters but with some task-specific limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve language-model performance under strict data and parameter constraints without simply increasing parameter count, by exploring whether reusing a small set of parameters multiple times (looping) can match or approach the performance of larger models.

Method: They use a Looped GPT-BERT model that combines masked next-token prediction with causal language modeling, and apply depth-wise parameter sharing so a small number of physical layers are reused multiple times (recurrent traversals). They train on a 7.48M-word English corpus in the BabyLM 2026 Strict-small track, systematically vary objective ratios, loop vs non-loop architectures, and numbers of loops, and compare models on BabyLM leaderboard metrics and benchmarks like BLiMP and GLUE.

Result: Their best model, with 4 physical layers and 12 recurrent traversals (4×12) and 12.18M parameters, achieves BabyLM 2026 leaderboard scores of 35.42 (Overall Average) and 48.48 (NLP Average). It performs comparably to public BabyLM 10M Strict-small GPT-2 and GPT-BERT baselines on several linguistic and downstream tasks such as BLiMP and GLUE while using fewer parameters. Ablations show that more loops can improve training dynamics and preserve strong performance on some linguistic tasks.

Conclusion: Depth-wise parameter sharing via looping in a GPT-BERT architecture is a viable parameter-efficient strategy under low-resource constraints, achieving near-baseline performance with substantially fewer parameters. However, the reduced number of distinct physical layers constrains the representational capacity, which leads to degraded performance on certain tasks, highlighting a trade-off between parameter efficiency and expressivity in looped designs.

Abstract: When training data are limited, increasing parameter count is not the only way to improve language-model performance. A small parameter set, when repeatedly applied, can also deliver comparable performance. We study Looped GPT-BERT in the BabyLM 2026 Strict-small setting, combining GPT-BERT's masked next-token and causal language-modeling objectives with depth-wise parameter sharing. We train on a preprocessed 7.48M-word English corpus and compare objective ratios, non-looped and looped architectures, and loop counts. Our final $4\times12$ model uses four physical layers for twelve recurrent traversals and contains 12.18M parameters. The BabyLM 2026 leaderboard reports an Overall Average of 35.42 and an NLP Average of 48.48. Compared with public BabyLM 10M Strict-small GPT-2 and GPT-BERT baselines, it achieves comparable performance on selected linguistic and downstream metrics, including BLiMP and GLUE, with fewer parameters. The loop ablations show that additional recurrent computation can improve training and preserve strong performance on selected linguistic tasks, whereas poorer performance on other tasks may reveal an inherent limitation of the looped design: using only a few physical layers restricts the model's representational space.

</details>


### [19] [When Auditors Fabricate: Batch-Size Degradation and Confident Hallucination in LLM Detection of Planted Document Contamination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09696)
*Karan Parekh,Sanjana Pendyala Ravinder,Sana Mhapsekar,Medina Maloku*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper tests how reliable a large language model (Gemini 3.0 Pro) is at detecting known, planted errors in academic papers, finding that its performance collapses and becomes deceptively hallucinated when asked to audit many documents at once.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether large language models can be trusted as automated auditors of document quality, especially in detecting realistic errors in academic texts, and to characterise how their reliability changes as the auditing task scales up.

Method: The authors build a contaminated corpus of 150 academic papers in supply chain management and medical research, injecting 450 known errors of three types: typographical corruption, semantic reversal, and absurd out-of-context insertion. They then test Gemini 3.0 Pro on a 180-error subset across 60 documents, under three prompting setups of increasing batch size (single document, small batch, large batch), and measure how many planted contaminants the model correctly recovers, along with its error modes.

Result: Gemini 3.0 Pro recovers 50% of known contaminants when auditing single documents, 60% for small batches, but only 2.8% for large batches. At large scale, the model does not merely abstain but hallucinates: it confidently reports fabricated contaminants (e.g., "telepathic squirrel", "quantum-powered toaster") that are stylistically similar to planted absurdities but do not exist in the text. Detection rates differ by error type: absurd insertions are recovered at 75% in completed evaluations, while semantic reversals and typographical corruptions are each recovered at about 50%, meaning more realistic errors are more often missed.

Conclusion: LLM-based document auditing degrades sharply and deceptively as task scale increases: instead of clearly signaling limitations, the model fabricates findings. Because more plausible, real-world-like errors are less likely to be caught, naive deployment of such systems is unsafe. The authors argue that robust LLM auditing requires strict operational constraints—such as limiting batch size, ensuring direct inclusion of source content in prompts, and mechanically verifying every reported issue against the original text.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly proposed as automated auditors of document quality, yet their reliability as detectors of planted errors is poorly characterised. We construct a contaminated corpus of 150 academic papers spanning supply chain management and medical research, injecting 450 known contaminants of three types: typographical corruption, semantic reversal, and absurd out-of-context insertion. We then evaluate Google Gemini 3.0 Pro's ability to recover a 180-contaminant answer-key subset across 60 documents under three prompting regimes of increasing scale: single document, small batch, and large batch. Detection holds at small scale and then collapses: 50% recovery on single documents, 60% on small batches, and 2.8% on large batches. The failure mode at scale is not abstention but fabrication. Rather than reporting incomplete processing, the model produced confident findings including invented contaminants of its own, absurdities such as "telepathic squirrel" and "quantum-powered toaster" that mimic the style of the planted material but do not appear in any document. Detection also varies by contamination type: absurd insertions were recovered at 75% in completed evaluations, while semantic reversals and typographical corruptions were each recovered at only 50%. The corruptions most likely to occur in the wild, plausible ones, are the ones most often missed. We conclude that LLM document auditing degrades not gracefully but deceptively, and outline the harness such systems require: bounded batch sizes, direct content injection, and mechanical verification of every reported finding against source text.

</details>


### [20] [Scaling E-Commerce Attribute Extraction with Parallel Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09716)
*Nikhita Vedula,Dushyanta Dhyani,Bryan Wang,Shervin Malmasi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents a two-stage LLM pipeline that first identifies the most purchase-relevant attributes per product category and then extracts their values using a compact, fine-tuned LLM, achieving high accuracy with significantly lower cost for large-scale e-commerce catalog structuring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: E-commerce catalogs are messy and unstructured, and standard AVE systems treat all attributes equally, leading to large, noisy, and inconsistent attribute sets that don’t match how customers actually compare products. There is a need for a scalable way to automatically discover and extract the small set of attributes that truly drive purchase decisions, in order to improve product discovery, comparison, and downstream applications.

Method: The authors propose a two-stage pipeline: (1) use a large foundational LLM to automatically discover, for each product category, a compact and ranked schema of purchase-discriminative attributes—i.e., the attributes that best differentiate products for buyers; (2) distill this capability into a smaller, fine-tuned LLM (Qwen3-4B) that performs Attribute Value Extraction for just those key attributes, accelerated via Hyper-Parallel Decoding to reduce inference costs while maintaining accuracy.

Result: The compact Qwen3-4B model with Hyper-Parallel Decoding achieves around 85% attribute extraction accuracy, comparable to the larger foundational LLM it is distilled from, while reducing inference costs by approximately 92%. The pipeline yields concise, consistent attribute sets for each category instead of large, noisy attribute lists produced by standard AVE systems.

Conclusion: Automatically discovering and then extracting a compact, ranked set of purchase-discriminative attributes per product category enables accurate, low-cost structuring of e-commerce catalogs at production scale. The resulting structured, category-level representations function as product knowledge bases, providing consistent and comparable attributes that can effectively ground downstream, knowledge-intensive applications such as product discovery, recommendation, and search.

Abstract: Customers rely on specific product attributes to compare products and make purchasing decisions, but e-commerce catalogs are messy and unstructured, making it difficult to identify which attributes matter most and extract them at scale. Standard Attribute Value Extraction (AVE) systems treat all attributes equally, producing large, inconsistent attribute sets that do not reflect the factors consumers use to differentiate products. We introduce a two-stage LLM pipeline that first discovers a compact, ranked schema of purchase-discriminative attributes for each product category, then extracts their values from catalog text using a fine-tuned compact LLM (Qwen3-4B) with Hyper-Parallel Decoding (HPD). This pipeline achieves 85% extraction accuracy, on par with the foundational LLM it was distilled from, while reducing inference costs by 92% over foundational LLMs, enabling production-scale use for product discovery and catalog enrichment. The resulting category-level structured representations effectively constitute automatically constructed product knowledge bases, providing consistent, comparable attributes across varied product categories that can ground downstream knowledge-intensive applications.

</details>


### [21] [StreamAlign: Streaming Text-Aligned Speech Tokenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09719)
*Kang-wook Kim,Jinyoung Park,Jinsoo Kim,Sehun Lee,Sang Hoon Woo,Gunhee Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: StreamAlign is a streaming, text-aligned speech tokenization framework that aligns speech with LLM token spaces in real time, reducing latency and improving recognition and spoken language modeling quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-aligned speech tokenizers depend on offline ASR, which requires full utterances and suffers from vocabulary mismatches between ASR and LLMs. This prevents real-time streaming applications and degrades acoustic granularity by aligning only at the word level. A method is needed that can tokenize speech in a way that is both compatible with LLM token spaces and suitable for low-latency, streaming scenarios while maintaining or improving recognition quality.

Method: The paper proposes StreamAlign, which performs online speech-text alignment using a combination of character-level RNN-Transducer (RNN-T) alignment and word-level ASR guidance. This dual-level alignment mitigates vocabulary mismatch between ASR and LLM tokenizers while maintaining recognition accuracy. Additionally, the method introduces a proactive word boundary classifier that predicts when words complete at chunk boundaries, enabling earlier emission of text-aligned tokens and thus reducing latency. They then train StreamAlign-SLM, a spoken language model, on units produced by StreamAlign to evaluate downstream performance on speech continuation and consistency tasks.

Result: StreamAlign reduces tokenization latency from 560 ms to 270 ms thanks to its proactive boundary prediction. On the LibriSpeech dataset, it achieves both the lowest word error rate (WER) and the highest UTMOS (a MOS prediction metric) among all evaluated speech tokenizers. The StreamAlign-SLM model, trained on StreamAlign units, surpasses other end-to-end spoken language models on speech continuation tasks and achieves the best overall consistency scores on SALMon and spoken StoryCloze benchmarks.

Conclusion: StreamAlign enables low-latency, streaming, text-aligned speech tokenization that is better matched to LLM token spaces, improving both ASR quality and downstream spoken language modeling. By combining character-level RNN-T alignment with word-level ASR guidance and a proactive word boundary classifier, it overcomes the limitations of offline ASR-based tokenizers. The resulting units support more effective spoken language models, as evidenced by superior performance on standard speech recognition and generative speech benchmarks.

Abstract: Text-aligned speech tokenization methods have emerged to better align speech tokens with LLM token spaces, enabling more effective utilization of pretrained LLMs. However, they rely on offline automatic speech recognition (ASR), leading to two key limitations: (i) the need for complete utterances before tokenization, precluding real-time streaming, and (ii) vocabulary mismatch between ASR and LLMs, which reduces acoustic granularity from the subword to the word level. We introduce StreamAlign, a text-aligned speech tokenization framework that enables streaming tokenization for real-time speech-text joint modeling. StreamAlign performs online speech-text alignment by combining character-level RNN-Transducer alignment with word-level ASR guidance, mitigating ASR-LLM vocabulary mismatch while preserving recognition accuracy. A proactive word boundary classifier anticipates word completion at chunk boundaries, reducing tokenization latency from 560 ms to 270 ms. On LibriSpeech, StreamAlign achieves the lowest WER and highest UTMOS among evaluated tokenizers. Furthermore, StreamAlign-SLM, a spoken language model trained on StreamAlign units, outperforms other end-to-end spoken language models in speech continuation while achieving the strongest overall consistency on SALMon and spoken StoryCloze.

</details>


### [22] [SocialRL: Refining LLMs' Social Intelligence through Multi-turn Reinforcement Learning and Reward Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09764)
*Jianing Wang,Xintao Wang,Aili Chen,Jie Shi,Hongcheng Guo,Jun Gao,Wenxuan Zhao,Chengkun Lang,Yuanli Guo,Yanghua Xiao*

Main category: cs.CL
