TL;DR: The paper introduces SocialRL, a multi-turn reinforcement learning framework that optimizes both long-horizon outcomes and fine-grained social-process rewards to improve goal achievement and relationship quality in social dialogue for language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As language models are increasingly used as autonomous collaborators in multi-turn social interactions, they need social intelligence: the ability to understand context, infer intentions, and manage the trade-off between achieving goals and maintaining relationships. Existing RL approaches focus on single-turn responses and sparse final rewards, leading to short-sighted behavior that fails to handle goal-relationship tensions over longer conversations. The paper aims to overcome these limitations to build more effective and trustworthy human-AI interactions.

Method: The authors propose SocialRL, a multi-turn RL framework based on PPO. It propagates delayed outcome rewards back to each turn for long-horizon planning. Additionally, they define six process-level reward dimensions capturing the trade-off between task success and relationship quality (e.g., goal advancement, relational attunement, contextual coherence). A reward model generates dynamic, fine-grained scoring criteria for each dimension, and a stage-aware weighting schedule changes their importance over the dialogue: emphasizing relationship-building early, goal progress in the middle, and balanced closure at the end.

Result: On multiple social-dialogue benchmarks, SocialRL outperforms the underlying base models, improving Goal Achievement by an average of 9.2 percentage points. The improvements hold across synthetic and real-world social settings, and across both standard and more challenging social scenarios.

Conclusion: SocialRL effectively addresses limitations of prior single-turn, sparse-reward RL methods for social dialogue by incorporating multi-turn credit assignment and rich, stage-aware process rewards. This leads to better goal achievement while managing relationship dynamics in multi-turn conversations, demonstrating its promise for building more socially intelligent language agents.

Abstract: Social intelligence enables agents to read social context, infer intent, and adapt over sustained dialogue. As language models become autonomous collaborators, it is central to building effective and trustworthy human-AI interaction. Existing reinforcement learning methods optimize single-turn utterances and sparse outcome rewards, producing short-sighted policies that struggle to manage goal-relationship tensions across multi-turn interactions. We propose SocialRL, a multi-turn reinforcement learning framework addressing both challenges. First, we apply multi-turn reinforcement learning using PPO that propagates delayed outcome rewards back to each turn, enabling long-horizon planning. Second, we design six process reward dimensions capturing the goal-relationship trade-off, including goal advancement, relational attunement, contextual coherence, etc. A reward model dynamically generates fine-grained scoring criteria for each dimension, while a stage-aware weight schedule prioritizes relationship-building in early turns, goal advancement mid-way, and balanced closure late. Across multiple social-dialogue benchmarks, SocialRL improves Goal Achievement by an average of 9.2 percentage points over the corresponding Base models. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of SocialRL across synthetic and real social scenes, as well as standard and challenging social scenarios.

</details>


### [23] [CARRE: Counterfactual Action Retrieval and Reason Evaluation for Explainable Churn Prescription](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09766)
*MinJoo Kim,SanJin Park,SeungHwan Cho*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes CARRE, a three-stage framework that not only identifies high-risk churn customers but also recommends specific, cost-aware retention actions and explains why they are appropriate, outperforming SHAP-based baselines in risk reduction and explanation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional churn models flag customers with high churn risk but do not indicate which concrete, feasible retention actions should be taken for each customer or provide clear, individualized reasons for the recommended action. Existing explanation tools like SHAP highlight influential features but are not action-oriented and often ignore intervention costs. The paper aims to bridge this gap by producing retention prescriptions that are both effective in reducing churn risk and understandable, while rigorously evaluating each component of the pipeline.

Method: The authors propose CARRE (Counterfactual Action Retrieval and Reason Evaluation), a three-stage pipeline: (1) Retrieval-augmented candidate generation: retrieve applicable retention actions from a predefined action catalog for each customer; (2) Cost-aware counterfactual scoring: explicitly modify relevant features for each candidate action, estimate new churn-risk scores, and compute expected risk reduction adjusted by action cost; (3) LLM-based reasoning and explanation: use a large language model to select among top candidate actions, generate structured churn reasons, and produce profile-grounded, human-readable justifications. They evaluate CARRE on the IBM Telco Churn dataset, tune retrieval parameters (e.g., k for candidate actions), and perform both quantitative and LLM-judged qualitative evaluations of risk reduction, agreement, and explanation quality, including an ablation on retrieval depth and a prompt-refinement study.

Result: On 313 high-risk test customers, CARRE achieves 79.8% higher mean model-predicted churn-risk reduction than a plain SHAP baseline and 80.4% higher reduction than a SHAP+Cost baseline, with a 10.5% gain in cost-normalized efficiency over plain SHAP. In a 136-case subset used for reason-stratified evaluation, diagnosis-driven prompt refinement improves LLM weak-label agreement from 79.4% to 90.4% without violating auxiliary constraints, though the post-refinement estimates are not out-of-sample. For 135 generated explanations, cross-vendor LLM judges give mean scores of 4.02 to 5.00 out of 5, with no detected contradictions among 66 verifiable profile claims. Retrieval ablations indicate that using k=5 retrieved actions strikes the best trade-off between candidate coverage and reasoning agreement for this dataset.

Conclusion: CARRE demonstrates that retrieval of candidate actions, model-based counterfactual scoring, and LLM-based explanation can be modularized and jointly evaluated in a churn-prescription setting. The framework produces more cost-effective risk reductions and higher-quality, profile-grounded explanations than SHAP-based baselines on the IBM Telco dataset. The work suggests a promising direction for moving from churn prediction to actionable churn prescription, while highlighting the importance of careful retrieval design, prompt refinement, and component-level evaluation; however, some evaluation elements, such as post-refinement agreement, require further validation on held-out data.

Abstract: Churn models typically identify high-risk customers but do not specify which feasible retention action should be considered or why that action is appropriate. We present CARRE (Counterfactual Action Retrieval and Reason Evaluation), a three-stage framework that combines retrieval-augmented candidate generation, cost-aware counterfactual scoring, and large language model (LLM) reasoning. CARRE retrieves a predefined catalog of retention actions, estimates model-predicted churn-risk changes under explicit feature transformations, and generates a structured churn reason and a profile-grounded explanation for the selected action. On the IBM Telco Customer Churn dataset, CARRE achieves 79.8% greater mean model-predicted risk reduction than the plain SHAP baseline and 80.4% greater reduction than the cost-controlled SHAP+Cost baseline across 313 high-risk test cases; its cost-normalized efficiency is 10.5% higher than that of plain SHAP. On a 136-case reason-stratified evaluation sample, diagnosis-driven prompt refinement increases weak-label agreement from 79.4% to 90.4%, with no auxiliary-plan constraint violations; because the same sample was used for error diagnosis and re-evaluation, the post-refinement result is not an independent estimate of generalization. For 135 explanations generated using the pre-refinement v2 reason outputs, two cross-vendor LLM judges assign mean scores ranging from 4.02 to 5.00 out of 5, although one judge saturates on actionability, and a deterministic audit finds no contradictions among 66 verifiable profile claims. Retrieval ablations show that k=5 provides the best evaluated compromise between high candidate coverage and downstream reasoning agreement in this dataset. These results illustrate how retrieval, model-based counterfactual scoring, and language generation can be separated and jointly evaluated in a prototype churn-prescription pipeline.

</details>


### [24] [SymbolicLight V2: Hybrid Neuromorphic Architecture and Sparse Execution for Low-Energy Language Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09772)
*Ting Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SymbolicLight V2 is a 194M-parameter hybrid neuromorphic language model optimized for sparse integer execution on FPGA and ARM, achieving higher throughput and substantially lower energy per token via event sparsity and architectural changes, though with lower quality than a dense control at equal budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce energy consumption and improve throughput for language model inference—especially on edge and accelerator hardware—by exploiting event sparsity and neuromorphic-like computation rather than conventional dense floating‑point GPU execution.

Method: Extend the prior SymbolicLight V1 neuromorphic architecture with graded signed events, additional sparse projections, and softmax‑free local attention; implement the 194M‑parameter model using fixed‑point arithmetic on an Alveo U50C FPGA and sparse integer execution on an ARM Cortex‑A76 CPU; design three FPGA decode pipelines with optimizations such as active‑row weight gathering and valid‑state KV loading; measure throughput and energy per generated token across varying prefix lengths and compare energy to an RTX 5090 FP32 baseline, keeping the model checkpoint fixed for all hardware experiments.

Result: On the FPGA at 175 MHz, architectural optimizations increase decode throughput from 474.6 to 643.2 tokens/s and reduce estimated gross card energy per generated token from 0.06087 J to 0.04407 J (27.6% reduction), with overall request energy (including prefill) reduced by 24.4–27.7% depending on prefix length; analysis shows 82.8% of card energy is due to loaded idle, so shorter token latency yields notable savings. Compared with a compiled‑FP32 RTX 5090 baseline, integer FPGA execution uses 89.1% less estimated card energy for short‑context decode, though precisions and configurations differ. On a ROCK 5T (four Cortex‑A76 cores), the system delivers 65.4 tokens/s at 9.80 W and 0.151 J/token at the AC adapter. The mechanisms demonstrate that event sparsity can translate into omitted computation and memory transfers, leading to significant energy gains.

Conclusion: SymbolicLight V2 demonstrates that hybrid neuromorphic language architectures with sparse event-driven computation and carefully optimized integer implementations on FPGA and ARM can substantially reduce energy per token and improve throughput versus a high‑end GPU FP32 baseline, largely by cutting idle and unnecessary work. However, because the evaluated sparse checkpoint underperforms a dense control at the same parameter or compute budget, the work establishes a promising efficiency mechanism rather than proof of equal‑quality efficiency; future work must close the quality gap while retaining the demonstrated energy and latency benefits.

Abstract: SymbolicLight V2 combines sparse event computation with continuous-state processing in a hybrid neuromorphic language architecture. Extending V1's spike-gated dual paths, it adds graded signed events at further projections and softmax-free local attention. We implement the 194M-parameter model on an Alveo U50C FPGA using digital fixed-point arithmetic and on an ARM CPU using sparse integer execution. Across three same-checkpoint FPGA implementations at 175 MHz, active-row weight gathering and valid-state KV loading raise decode throughput from 474.6 to 643.2 tokens/s for a 32-token prefix and 128 outputs. Estimated gross card energy falls from 0.06087 to 0.04407 J per generated token, a 27.6% reduction. Complete-request energy, including prefill, falls by 24.4-27.7% across three prefix lengths. An independent idle split attributes 82.8% of gross card energy to loaded idle, explaining the benefit of shorter token latency. Against the recorded RTX 5090 compiled-FP32 baseline, integer FPGA execution uses 89.1% less estimated card energy during short-context decode; arithmetic precisions differ, and the GPU baseline is not the lowest-energy tested configuration. On four Cortex-A76 cores of a ROCK 5T, complete requests reach 65.4 tokens/s at 9.80 W and 0.151 J per generated token at the adapter's AC input. These results connect event sparsity to omitted computation and data movement. The mechanisms also support other dedicated V2 implementations: increasing throughput by a greater factor than active power lowers energy per generated token. Evaluation holds the deployed checkpoint fixed; its quality trails a same-budget dense control, so the results do not establish equal-quality efficiency.

</details>


### [25] [ROAM: Robust Organization of Atomic Memories for Agents through Semantic Relations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09778)
*Jianjie Zheng,Peng Lai,Sijie Cheng,Jiehui Zhao,Lei Yang,Guanhua Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ROAM is a relation-guided memory management framework for LLM agents that structures and fuses atomic memories to improve long-term reasoning accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term language-model agents accumulate many atomic memories that become redundant, overlapping, or conflicting. Existing approaches rely on an LLM manager to directly edit the memory, tightly coupling semantic interpretation, storage decisions, and content generation, which is error-prone and leads to poor retrieval and use of stored information. There is a need for a principled way to manage atomic memories over time so that agents can maintain accurate, concise, and useful external memory.

Method: The paper proposes ROAM, a relation-guided framework for managing atomic memories. For each pair of incoming and stored atomic memories, ROAM classifies their relation into one of four types: independent, equivalent, directionally subsuming, or conflicting. Based on these relations, ROAM organizes observations into active Primary memories and supporting Evidence memories. It then applies a fusion mechanism that merges complementary details and temporal updates into compact, possibly non-atomic views. Only these Primary fused views are retrieved at answer time, while supporting Evidence roles help maintain underlying detail without introducing redundancy or outdated information into retrieval.

Result: Empirical evaluation across different language models and benchmarks shows that ROAM improves answer accuracy by up to 29.8 percentage points compared to baselines. Ablation studies demonstrate that each relation type contributes complementary benefits, and that the fusion step yields consistent performance gains beyond just role organization. Mechanism-focused analysis indicates that ROAM increases recall of answer-critical sources by 15.6 percentage points and reduces the proportion of confounding (distracting or misleading) tokens by 11.5 percentage points. Additionally, ROAM remains effective across different sizes of the LLM manager model, indicating robustness to manager scale.

Conclusion: ROAM offers a principled way to manage long-term external memory for LLM agents by leveraging relations between atomic memories and structured fusion. This decouples semantic interpretation from direct memory editing and mitigates issues of redundancy, conflict, and outdated information. The framework leads to more accurate and robust answering in long-term settings, with demonstrated improvements in both end-task accuracy and intermediate memory-use metrics, and shows stability across different model scales.

Abstract: Long-term language-model agents rely on external memory across interactions. Atomic memories are particularly useful: their fine-grained semantic boundaries enable precise retrieval and direct comparison between observations. Yet accumulating atoms inevitably become redundant, overlapping, or conflicting. Existing methods often ask an LLM manager to add, update, delete, or rewrite memories directly, coupling semantic interpretation, storage decisions, and content generation in one error-prone operation. We introduce ROAM, a relation-guided framework that uses atomicity for management while allowing richer answer-time representations. ROAM classifies incoming--stored atom pairs as independent, equivalent, directionally subsuming, or conflicting, then organizes observations into active Primary and supporting Evidence roles. Fusion subsequently combines complementary details and temporal changes into compact, potentially non-atomic views. Only Primary views are retrieved for answering, preventing redundant or outdated atoms from competing independently. Across models and evaluation settings, ROAM improves answer accuracy by up to 29.8 percentage points. Ablations show complementary benefits from different relations and consistent gains from fusion beyond role organization. Mechanism analysis further finds 15.6-point higher answer-critical source recall and an 11.5-point lower confounder-token share. ROAM remains robust across manager scales.

</details>


### [26] [MUCnoHARM@GermEval Shared Task 2026: Retrieval-based In-Context Learning for Defamatory Offences, and Where It Falls Short](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09791)
*Kristin Gnadt,Maximilian Meidinger,Matthias Aßenmacher*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates retrieval-based in-context learning strategies for detecting criminally relevant hate speech in German social media, finding that model choice matters more than retrieval strategy and that current systems are better for triage than full automation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Because hate speech and potentially criminal defamatory content are widespread online, there is a need for automatic detection systems, especially for content that could be criminally relevant under German law (§§ 185-187 StGB). Existing approaches like zero-shot and few-shot prompting show promise, but it is unclear how much retrieval-based in-context learning can improve performance for this specific legal classification task.

Method: The authors experiment with various retrieval-based in-context learning (RetICL) strategies that select example demonstrations from a dataset of social media posts related to German defamation law. They compare zero-shot prompting, few-shot prompting with random examples, retrieval-based selection of examples, and an optimized static set of demonstrations. They also test the effect of providing explicit legal knowledge in prompts and analyze multiple model architectures to assess their relative impact on performance.

Result: Few-shot prompting outperforms zero-shot prompting in detecting criminally relevant posts. However, retrieval-based in-context learning strategies yield only marginal improvements over randomly chosen demonstrations and in some cases underperform a carefully optimized static set of examples. Including concrete legal information in prompts improves results, but variations in model choice have a larger effect than retrieval strategy or prompt design. Despite these optimizations, models still overestimate the criminal relevance of content and fail to detect 26–57% of actually criminal posts.

Conclusion: Retrieval-based in-context learning provides limited benefits over simpler prompting strategies for detecting defamatory offences under German law in social media posts. Supplying legal knowledge is useful, but selecting a strong underlying model is far more important than fine-tuning retrieval or demonstration strategies. Given their tendency to over-predict criminal relevance while still missing a substantial portion of truly criminal content, current models are not reliable enough for fully automated moderation and are better suited as triage tools to assist human reviewers.

Abstract: With hate speech being ubiquitous online, automatic detection is crucial, in particular when it comes to criminally relevant social media posts. We study a variety of retrieval-based in-context learning (RetICL) strategies for detecting defamatory offences under §§ 185-187 StGB (the subject of GermEval 2026 Subtask 4). Few-shot prompting beats zero-shot, but retrieval-based approaches offer only marginal gains over random demonstrations, and even fall behind an optimised static set of demonstrations. Providing concrete legal knowledge helps, yet model choice outweighs every other system choice. Models over-predict criminal relevance while still missing 26-57% of criminally relevant posts, suiting them for triage rather than autonomous moderation.

</details>


### [27] [HyperTrace: Hypothesis-Based Preference Tracing for Online LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09835)
*Jianzhi Shen,Keyu Mao,Minghao Shao,Chuanyang Jin,Yusong Wang,Ailiang Lin,Kotaro Funakoshi,Manabu Okumura,Tianmin Shu,Muhammad Shafique*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: HyperTrace is a training-free framework that personalizes LLMs by continuously tracing users’ latent short-term and long-term preferences with natural-language hypotheses updated over interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training-free personalization methods mostly rely on raw interaction histories or retrieved memories, but they struggle to simultaneously capture stable long-term user preferences and ephemeral short-term intents. There is a need for a method that can dynamically infer and reconcile both aspects of user preference without retraining or updating model parameters.

Method: HyperTrace frames personalization as latent preference tracing. It maintains a set of interpretable natural-language hypotheses representing both short-term intent and long-term preferences. Using an LLM-based surrogate choice model, it performs an SMC-style (sequential Monte Carlo–like) reweighting of these hypotheses based on ongoing user interactions. The highest-weighted hypotheses guide the model’s responses, and the process is repeated across dialogue turns and sessions, all without parameter updates.

Result: On two benchmarks, PRISM and PersonaMem-v2, HyperTrace outperforms strong online, training-free personalization baselines. It achieves better alignment of responses to user preferences, higher accuracy in predicting user preferences, and greater consistency with a user’s profile across interactions.

Conclusion: Tracing user preferences as evolving latent hypotheses in natural language enables robust, training-free personalization of LLMs. HyperTrace demonstrates that an SMC-style hypothesis reweighting mechanism can effectively integrate short-term intents with long-term preferences, improving alignment and consistency without modifying model parameters.

Abstract: Personalized language models aim to adapt responses to individual users, whose preferences are often latent and revealed gradually through interaction. Existing training-free methods rely on stored histories or retrieved memories, but they often struggle to reconcile long- term preferences with short-term topic-specific needs. To address this issue, we propose HyperTrace, a training-free framework that formulates online personalization as latent preference tracing. HyperTrace maintains interpretable natural-language hypotheses over short-term intent and long-term preferences, and updates them through an SMC-style reweight process using an LLM-based surrogate choice model. By updating these hypotheses across turns and sessions, HyperTrace enables personalization without parameter updates. Experiments on PRISM and PersonaMem-v2 show that HyperTrace improves response alignment, preference prediction, and profile consistency over strong online baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness of tracing latent user preferences for robust personalization. Code and scripts are available in the repository: https://github.com/jiseshen/HyperTrace.

</details>


### [28] [$S^3$-Bench: Evaluating Speech Interaction Models as Scientific Voice Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09852)
*Heyang Liu,Jiayi Huang,Wenyang Xiao,Ziyang Cheng,Lixin Zhang,Zhen Liu,Miao He,Ronghua Wu,Qunshan Gu,Yanfeng Wang,Yu Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces S^3-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating scientific speech interactions in multimodal large language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal LLMs do well as general voice assistants, but their capabilities in specialized scientific domains are not well understood, especially for speech-based interaction that must handle technical terms, abbreviations, and symbolic expressions.

Method: Design a systematic evaluation framework called S^3-Bench across 10 scientific disciplines, consisting of (1) a Knowledge set for speech question answering and (2) a Dialogue set for multi-turn progressive interactions with simulated user agents. They decompose each speech interaction turn into stages: speech recognition, perception, knowledge utilization with reasoning, and response pronunciation, and use this decomposition to assess and compare existing approaches.

Result: Using S^3-Bench to run experiments on current models, they identify common challenges and performance tradeoffs across the different stages (recognition, perception, reasoning, pronunciation) and measure how existing MLLMs perform in specialized scientific speech tasks.

Conclusion: Current multimodal LLMs show notable limitations in scientific speech interactions, particularly in adapting to users over multiple turns and in providing responses that are simultaneously accurate, comprehensive, and efficient; S^3-Bench offers a structured way to diagnose and analyze these shortcomings.

Abstract: The advance of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) has fundamentally reshaped the paradigm of human-computer interaction, especially speech interaction models capable of seamless conversations. Despite remarkable performance as general voice assistants, their performance in specialized domains remains underexplored, particularly in scientific areas. Scientific interactions introduce formidable challenges, involving rare technical terminology, spoken norms of abbreviations, and the natural verbalization of symbolic special expressions. In this paper, we introduce S$^3$-Bench, a systematic evaluation framework covering 10 major disciplines, consisting of a Knowledge set for speech question-answering and a Dialogue set for multi-turn progressive interactions with simulated user agents. By decomposing a complete atomic turn into stages of speech recognition, perception, knowledge utilization with reasoning, and response pronunciation, we systematically characterize the common challenges and performance tradeoffs of existing approaches. Furthermore, experiments on multi-turn interactions reveal persistent limitations in user adaptation and the generation of accurate, comprehensive, and efficient responses.

</details>


### [29] [When Does Defendant Statement Matter? A Study of Bias and Persuasion in LLM-Simulated Jurors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09887)
*Cho-Ying Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces JuryBench, a benchmark to study how LLM-simulated jurors respond to defendants’ courtroom statements in U.S. criminal cases, revealing patterns of persuasion, bias, and background affinity similar to human juries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether and how large language models can reliably simulate jury decision-making in common-law criminal trials, and to identify the types of persuasion and biases (ideological and background-based) that emerge in such simulations.

Method: The authors construct JuryBench, a dataset of controversial U.S. criminal cases where defendants can make various plausible justificatory statements. They systematically vary defendants’ backgrounds and the emotional or rebuttal content of statements, and simulate jurors with diverse ideological profiles. They then query 20 state-of-the-art LLMs, collecting 432K verdicts and rationales, and analyze how verdict severity changes with these factors.

Result: Across models, emotional appeals often backfire (being interpreted as signs of guilt or inconsistency), background similarity between juror and defendant significantly predicts more lenient judgments, dissimilarity predicts harsher ones, and juror ideology strongly influences verdict severity. LLM behavior recapitulates many known human jury effects.

Conclusion: LLMs show jury-like patterns of persuasion and bias when simulating jurors, suggesting they may be useful for studying jury reasoning but also carry risks of reproducing and amplifying human-like biases. Therefore, any use of LLMs in this domain requires careful design, evaluation, and oversight. 

Abstract: LLMs have been used to simulate human decision-making in professional settings, yet their behaviors in common-law jury trials remain unexplored. We study when and how a defendant's courtroom statement affects LLM-simulated jurors, focusing on persuasion, ideological bias, and background-based affinity. To support the analysis, we introduce JuryBench, a benchmark containing controversial criminal cases in U.S. criminal law. In each case, a defendant can claim various plausible justifications to support acquittal or reduced liability. We fix the base case and design defendants of different backgrounds, who give courtroom statements with varying emotional appeal or rebuttal. Jurors with diverse ideological profiles across the spectrum are simulated. We examine 20 frontier LLMs, resulting in a total of 432K decisions and rationales, and quantify changes in verdict severity. Our findings show that LLM-jury simulation echoes many human-jury findings. First, emotional persuasion can be detrimental, since jurors may perceive it as evidence of guilt or inconsistency. Next, we show that background fit between jurors and defendants is a stronger and significant factor than other isolated factors, and that jurors are in general harsher toward opposite-background defendants and lenient toward same-background ones. Finally, we find that juror ideology also strongly shapes severity judgments. These findings highlight both the promise and risks of using LLMs to model jury reasoning and call for careful evaluation. The data and code are available at https://github.com/choyingw/JuryBench

</details>


### [30] [Leveraging Fine-grained Error Correction in Korean Speech Recognition for Consultation Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09889)
*Yonghyun Jun,Jimin Lee,Hwan Chang,Dongho Shin,Seolah Kim,Hwanhee Lee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Korean call-center ASR transcripts often contain errors, especially in low-resource settings, and this paper builds both a new dataset and a specialized text-only correction model to fix them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ASR systems used in call centers still produce errors, but in many real-world scenarios audio cannot be accessed because of privacy constraints, so errors must be corrected from text alone. For low-resource languages like Korean, there is a lack of annotated data and methods specifically for text-based ASR error correction, which limits performance and evaluation.

Method: The authors construct DasanCallDial, a large-scale dialogue-level Korean dataset of real call center ASR outputs paired with corrected transcripts. They then propose Detector-Gated Contextual Span Correction (DCSC), a two-stage text-only post-editing framework: an encoder-based detector marks likely erroneous tokens, and a language-model-based corrector performs span-level corrections only where needed. They further add dialogue-level context augmentation, allowing the model to use previous turns in the conversation for better disambiguation, and design the system to work well with error-sparse transcripts.

Result: Using the new dataset, the DCSC framework achieves state-of-the-art performance on Korean ASR error correction in comparison to general large language models and other baselines, especially in low-resource and text-only settings. The multi-level granularity and use of dialogue context improve both detection and correction accuracy.

Conclusion: A dedicated dialogue-level benchmark (DasanCallDial) and the proposed DCSC framework substantially improve text-only ASR error correction for Korean call center data, showing that a detector-gated span corrector with dialogue context can outperform generic LLMs in low-resource languages. This work establishes a foundation and benchmark for future research on ASR post-editing under privacy constraints.

Abstract: Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) technology is fundamental to customer service automation and large-scale transcription. However, even advanced ASR models exhibit inevitable errors in complex real-world environments such as call center conversations. When privacy restrictions preclude audio access, error correction must rely on text-based post-editing. Existing text-only approaches face significant challenges in low-resource languages, mainly due to a critical scarcity of annotated corpora and tailored correction methodologies. For Korean, this resource gap is particularly pronounced, as existing resources are predominantly designed for ASR training rather than text-based error correction. To address this, we introduce DasanCallDial, the first large-scale Korean benchmark dataset specifically curated for dialogue-level ASR error correction. Derived from genuine call center interactions, it comprises 1,974 dialogues with 115,460 utterances. Leveraging this resource, we propose Detector-Gated Contextual Span Correction (DCSC), a text-only post-editing framework for error-sparse Korean speech recognition transcripts. DCSC combines an encoder-based detector that first performs token-level error detection, followed by a language model-based corrector trained to rectify fine-grained span-level errors. Additionally, we employ dialogue-level context augmentation to enable the model to leverage discourse history for disambiguation. By employing multi-level granularity, our method achieves state-of-the-art performance, effectively overcoming the limitations of general LLMs in low-resource settings.

</details>


### [31] [Deep and shallow biases in language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09901)
*An Vo,Vy Tuong Dang,Khai-Nguyen Nguyen,Emilio Villa-Cueva,Thamar Solorio,Anh Totti Nguyen,Daeyoung Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a new metric, bias depth, to distinguish stable, model-internal answer preferences (Deep biases) from prompt-wording artifacts (Shallow biases) in large language models, and show Deep biases are much harder to change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing analyses of answer concentration in LLMs treat it all as bias and typically rely on single-prompt evaluations, failing to distinguish between true, stable model preferences and those that arise only because of a particular phrasing or context. This limits our understanding of what kinds of biases are actually encoded in the model parameters and which are superficial artifacts that could be removed by rewording the prompt. The authors want a principled way to separate these phenomena.

Method: They introduce a new metric called bias depth. First, they measure how strongly a model prefers its top answer under straightforward, direct prompting. Then they systematically reframe the scenario in alternative ways and test whether the same answer continues to be selected. If a concentrated preference is robust across reframings, it is assigned a high bias depth (Deep bias); if it disappears or flips under reframing, it has low depth (Shallow bias). They apply this to 4,442 opinion-style prompts across four LLMs, and examine how these biases are affected by pretraining, supervised fine-tuning (SFT), further fine-tuning, and prompt-based debiasing methods.

Result: Empirically, only about 25% of initially concentrated answer preferences remain stable under reframing, qualifying as Deep biases. Deep biases are more likely to originate in pretraining and are preserved through SFT, whereas Shallow biases are more influenced by prompt wording and can be more easily changed. Under both continued fine-tuning and debiasing strategies aimed at increasing response diversity, Deep biases are consistently more resistant to modification than Shallow biases.

Conclusion: Bias depth effectively distinguishes between robust, parameter-level biases and superficial prompt artifacts in LLM behavior. This separation shows that many apparent biases in single-prompt tests are shallow, while a smaller subset are deeply ingrained and harder to mitigate. Consequently, evaluations and debiasing methods should account for bias depth, focusing special attention on Deep biases that persist across reframings and training regimes.

Abstract: Large language models often repeatedly select the same answer even when many alternatives are plausible. Prior work treats this concentration as bias, but it does not distinguish stable model preferences from responses that depend on a particular prompt wording. We introduce a bias depth score that measures both how strongly a model prefers its top answer under direct prompting and whether that answer survives scenario reframing. Across 4,442 opinion prompts and four large language models, only about a quarter of the concentrated preferences survive reframing. We call these persistent cases Deep biases, and the remaining prompt-dependent cases Shallow biases. Our results show that Deep biases are more often inherited from pretraining and preserved through SFT. Under both continued fine-tuning and prompt-based debiasing for diversity, Deep biases are consistently harder to remove than Shallow biases. Bias depth therefore separates stable learned biases from prompt-wording artifacts that single-prompt metrics conflate. Code, models, and data are available at deepbias.github.io.

</details>


### [32] [Contrastive Projection: Reading Transformer Internals by Differencing Logit Lenses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09902)
*Olli Tuomi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a more reliable way to interpret transformer hidden states by reading differences between similar prompts instead of raw activations, revealing robust, model-invariant distinctions despite token-level variability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Directly interpreting transformer internal states via logit lens is unreliable at intermediate layers because outputs are dominated by generic, input-agnostic tokens, obscuring meaningful computation. The authors aim to develop a training-free, more trustworthy method to see what specific computations transformers perform and how they represent distinctions such as compound nouns, entity types, and metaphors.

Method: They subtract hidden states from closely matched prompts to cancel shared components, then project the difference through the unembedding (logit) layer—equivalent to applying a logit lens to a RepE/ActAdd steering vector. This is embedded into a training-free tracer that reads at each position, sub-layer, and head, averaging results over carefully chosen baselines. They validate the traced circuits using activation patching in Phi-2, and replicate the same conceptual distinctions across three architectures via readout and probing instead of patching. They also apply the method to analyze retrieval for real vs. fictional entities and metaphor representations as mappings between domains.

Result: The tracer identifies a specific MLP-to-attention computation chain for compound nouns in Phi-2, corroborated by activation patching. It robustly recovers analogous distinctions (e.g., between types of entities and metaphor domains) across three different model architectures, even when direct logit lens on single states is noisy. However, across five networks that differ only in random initialization, the same underlying distinction appears as largely different top tokens (overlap 0.08), demonstrating high token-level variability despite similar conceptual computations.

Conclusion: Reading differences between matched prompts' hidden states, rather than raw hidden states alone, yields a more faithful, training-free view of what transformers compute. While the surface token representation of a computation is highly network-specific, the underlying distinctions and computations are shared across architectures. This suggests interpretability methods should focus on invariant distinctions in representation space rather than on specific tokens surfaced by naive logit-lens reads.

Abstract: Reading a transformer's internal states in token space is easy to do and hard to trust: a logit lens on a single hidden state is dominated, at intermediate layers, by the generic tokens the model would predict for almost any input. We read the difference instead. Subtracting two closely matched prompts' hidden states and projecting through the unembedding cancels the shared component and surfaces what separates them, an operation equivalent to reading a RepE/ActAdd steering vector through a logit lens. Built into a training-free tracer that reads at every position, sub-layer, and head and averages over designed baselines, it traces a compound- noun MLP->attention chain in Phi-2, confirmed there by activation patching, with the same distinction recovered across three architectures by readout and probe rather than by patching; it reads what retrieval surfaces for real versus fictional entities, and reads metaphor as a set of domain-to-domain mappings rather than a single figurativity feature. A cross-seed control marks the boundary: across five networks differing only in initialization, the same distinction surfaces as almost entirely different tokens (top-10 overlap 0.08). What a computation looks like in token space is network-specific; the distinction it draws is not

</details>


### [33] [Improving Cross-Lingual Token Representations by Adding a Pinch of SALT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09953)
*Guillem Ramírez*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces SALT, a lightweight post-training method to enhance token representations of cross-lingual sentence encoders using span-level supervision, improving both token- and sentence-level performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-lingual sentence encoders are widely used for sentence-level tasks and increasingly repurposed for token-level tasks like hallucination detection and sequence tagging. However, these encoders are originally trained for sentence-level alignment, creating a mismatch when they are used for token-level applications. There is a need for methods that can better adapt sentence encoders to token-level tasks without expensive retraining.

Method: The authors propose SALT, a lightweight post-training procedure that injects span-level supervision into existing cross-lingual sentence encoders. Instead of retraining from scratch, SALT fine-tunes encoders using supervised signals defined over text spans, thereby refining the internal token representations while preserving or enhancing sentence-level semantics. The approach is applied on top of existing multilingual encoders and compared against other fine-tuning strategies and competitive baselines.

Result: On five multilingual token-level benchmarks, SALT achieves state-of-the-art or best overall performance on four of them, outperforming both alternative fine-tuning strategies and other strong encoders. Furthermore, the method not only improves token-level performance but also enhances sentence-level capabilities, as evidenced by gains on cross-lingual retrieval and classification tasks.

Conclusion: Span-level supervision is an effective and efficient way to adapt cross-lingual sentence encoders for better token representations, benefiting both token-level and sentence-level tasks. SALT offers a lightweight post-training solution that can be applied to existing models, providing strong performance improvements across a variety of multilingual benchmarks.

Abstract: Cross-lingual sentence encoders enable scalable transfer across hundreds of languages, powering applications such as translation mining and zero-shot learning in low-resource settings. Although trained for sentence-level alignment, they are increasingly also applied to token-level tasks such as hallucination detection and sequence tagging, exposing a mismatch between training and usage. We propose SALT, a lightweight post-training method that improves token representations by injecting span-level supervision into existing sentence encoders. Across five multilingual token-level benchmarks, SALT achieves the best overall results on four of them, outperforming alternative fine-tuning strategies and competitive encoders. It also improves sentence-level performance on cross-lingual retrieval and classification tasks. These results demonstrate that span-level supervision is an effective signal for improving both token and sentence representations.

</details>


### [34] [5-Dialects-BN: Unmasking the Impact of Transliteration on Bangla Dialectal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09964)
*Md Mahir Jawad,Galib Mahmud Jim,Rafid Ahmed,Mir Sazzat Hossain,Md Fahim,Md Farhad Alam Bhuiyan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build and release a multi-annotation benchmark dataset (5-Dialects-BN) for five major Bangla dialects to evaluate and improve LLMs in low-resource, dialectally diverse settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs perform poorly on low-resource languages and dialect-rich settings. For Bangla, existing resources focus almost only on Standard Bangla, leaving major regional dialects under-resourced and without benchmarks, which hinders building and evaluating dialect-aware systems.

Method: They construct a manually annotated dataset of 6,000 entries covering five Bangla dialects (Chittagong, Noakhali, Sylhet, Barisal, Rangpur). For each entry, they provide five aligned annotations: original dialectal text, Romanized transliteration, English translation, Standard Bangla translation, and a subjectivity label. Native speakers and trained annotators create and cross-validate these labels to ensure authenticity and semantic fidelity.

Result: They obtain the 5-Dialects-BN dataset: a balanced, quality-controlled, multi-annotation benchmark spanning five major Bangla dialects, aligned across scripts and languages and labeled for subjectivity, suitable for multiple downstream NLP tasks.

Conclusion: 5-Dialects-BN offers the first standardized, multi-annotation benchmark for Bangla dialects, enabling systematic evaluation and improvement of LLMs on dialectally diverse Bangla, and providing a foundation for future research in low-resource, dialect-aware NLP tasks such as dialect identification, normalization, translation, and subjectivity classification.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable progress across natural language processing (NLP) tasks, yet their capabilities degrade sharply for low-resource languages and dialectally diverse settings. Bangla, the world's sixth most spoken language, exemplifies this gap: existing resources overwhelmingly target Standard Bangla, leaving its regional dialects without the benchmarks needed to develop or evaluate dialect-aware systems. We address this gap with 5-Dialects-BN, the first multi-annotation Bangla dialect benchmark to align Romanized transliteration with dialectal text, Standard Bangla, English, and subjectivity labels across five regional varieties. The dataset comprises 6,000 manually annotated entries spanning five major dialects: Chittagong, Barisal, Noakhali, Sylhet, and Rangpur (Chittagong 1,900; Noakhali 1,500; Sylhet 1,200; Barisal 700; Rangpur 700), reflecting natural online availability. Each entry is enriched with five aligned annotations: the original dialectal text, a Romanized transliteration, an English translation, a Standard Bangla translation, and a subjectivity label (subjective vs. objective). Annotations were produced and cross-validated by native speakers and undergraduate linguistics students to ensure dialectal authenticity and semantic fidelity. The resulting resource supports a diverse suite of tasks, including dialect identification, dialect-to-standard normalization, machine translation, subjectivity classification, and parameter-efficient fine-tuning (e.g., LoRA) of multilingual LLMs. By providing a standardized, multi-annotation benchmark, 5-Dialects-BN enables principled evaluation of LLMs on dialectally diverse Bangla and lays a foundation for further research in low-resource, dialect-aware NLP.

</details>


### [35] [Towards Stress-Aware Sentence-Level Filipino G2P With Weakly-Supervised ByT5 Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09974)
*Lorenz Bernard Marqueses,Paulo Grane Gabriel Silva,Chastine Cabatay,Ericson Adler Tan,Ann Franchesca Laguna*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper tackles Filipino grapheme-to-phoneme conversion at the sentence level, incorporating stress, by fine-tuning a multilingual ByT5 G2P model on LLM-annotated sentence data, achieving very low PER/CER and showing good stress prediction except for malumi words.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Filipino G2P is simple at the word level due to shallow orthography, but becomes challenging when prosody (especially stress) is included, as this depends on sentence context and homograph disambiguation. Existing Filipino resources lack sentence-level phoneme transcriptions, making it difficult to train models that handle stress and context. The authors aim to create effective sentence-level Filipino G2P models using only limited existing resources.

Method: Start from a ByT5-based model pre-trained on multilingual word-level G2P data. Use Wiktionary and an LLM-assisted annotation pipeline to automatically add phoneme and stress information to three Filipino sentence-level datasets, creating training data. Fine-tune the ByT5 model on these new datasets for sentence-level G2P with stress. Evaluate against a manually-corrected test set, measuring phoneme error rate (PER), character error rate (CER), and stress classification accuracy across Filipino stress classes.

Result: Fine-tuning on the LLM-annotated sentence-level datasets dramatically reduces PER from about 19.74% (base model) to as low as 0.54%, with CER around 2.50% on a manually-validated test set. The model accurately predicts most major Filipino stress classes and performs well on sentence-level G2P, though performance is notably weaker on malumi words. The system also shows promise for disambiguating Filipino homographs via correct stress assignment.

Conclusion: A ByT5-based multilingual G2P model, when fine-tuned on LLM-annotated Filipino sentence-level data guided by Wiktionary, can achieve very strong performance on Filipino G2P with stress. Despite remaining challenges for specific stress classes (e.g., malumi), the approach enables accurate sentence-level phoneme prediction and demonstrates strong potential for practical applications such as homograph disambiguation in Filipino.

Abstract: Grapheme-to-phoneme conversion (G2P) refers to the task of converting a sequence of graphemes to a corresponding sequence of phonemes. While Filipino G2P is fairly straightforward due to its shallow orthography, the inclusion of prosodic features such as stress adds a layer of complexity that requires sentence-level context instead of single-word inputs. However, sentence-level data for Filipino typically do not include phoneme transcriptions, posing a challenge for training G2P models. As such, we investigate how to obtain sentence-level phoneme data for Filipino using available data and compare the resulting models with multilingual word-level G2P as well as measure how accurately they predict stress marker position for Filipino. We propose fine-tuning a ByT5-based model, pre-trained on multilingual word-level G2P data, on three sentence-level G2P datasets annotated with an LLM-assisted pipeline guided by data from Wiktionary. This approach produces models that perform well on the G2P task, achieving at best around 0.54% PER and 2.50% CER, a significant decrease compared to base model PER at around 19.74%, on a manually-corrected test set. The model is able to correctly classify most of the main stress classes in Filipino, but struggles particularly with malumi words. We show that a ByT5-based model performs well at sentence-level Filipino G2P and offers strong potential for Filipino homograph disambiguation.

</details>


### [36] [Multi-Functional Embedding Models for Funder Name Disambiguation in Scientific Publication Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09984)
*Kanyao Han,Zhiwen You,Jinseok Kim,Jana Diesner*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build and evaluate a multilingual framework to disambiguate and cluster research funder names, achieving high accuracy and revealing limitations for smaller and non-English funders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Research funding patterns are hard to study at scale because funder names are highly inconsistent across sources and languages, and there is no comprehensive, standardized disambiguation resource comparable to those for authors or institutions. This prevents accurate, large-scale analyses of who funds what research and where.

Method: They integrate the Research Organization Registry (ROR) with Web of Science and Crossref Open Funder Registry data to construct labeled training pairs between noisy funder strings and canonical ROR IDs. Using this dataset, they fine-tune three open-weight text-embedding models (from Sentence Transformer, Gemma, and Qwen3 families) with a multi-task learning setup combining Contrastive Loss and Multiple Negatives Ranking Loss. For funder names absent from ROR, they build a similarity network based on embedding distances and detect clusters to group related funders. They compare performance against general-purpose LLMs used as matchers.

Result: The fine-tuned embedding models reach over 0.90 accuracy when matching Web of Science funder strings to ROR identifiers and surpass several strong general-purpose LLM baselines (GPT-5.2, Claude-Sonnet-4.6, Gemini-2.5-Flash) by more than 0.1 in accuracy. The clustering over non-ROR funders yields coherent groups but also exposes gaps in coverage and difficulties especially for smaller or non-English funders.

Conclusion: Their approach offers a reusable, cost-efficient framework for funder name disambiguation that works across languages, model architectures, and datasets. It substantially improves matching performance over general-purpose LLMs and supports downstream analyses of research funding, while also underscoring ongoing challenges due to incomplete registries and sparse information about smaller and non-English funders.

Abstract: Understanding the historical allocation and distribution of research funding advances our knowledge of how scientific research is supported across fields, institutions, and regions. However, large-scale analyses are hindered by the lack of comprehensive funder name disambiguation solutions, as funder names often exhibit spelling variations, translations, abbreviations, and inconsistent levels of granularity. In this paper, we present a framework for developing multilingual, multi-functional funder name disambiguation models and demonstrate its application to research publications in biodiversity conservation. To construct a training dataset, we integrated the Research Organization Registry (ROR), which provides unique identifiers for research organizations, with two publication datasets: the Web of Science (WoS) and the Crossref Open Funder Registry (OFR). We used multi-task learning with Contrastive Loss and Multiple Negatives Ranking Loss to fine-tune three open-weight embedding models from the Sentence Transformer, Gemma, and Qwen3 families. The best-performing models achieved accuracy above 0.90 when matching WoS funder names to ROR identifiers, outperforming general-purpose LLMs, including GPT-5.2, Claude-Sonnet-4.6, and Gemini-2.5-Flash, by more than 0.1. For funder names not indexed in ROR, we constructed a similarity network among funder names and identified clusters within it. Finally, we analyzed the disambiguation results and highlighted challenges arising from limited knowledge of smaller funders and funders from non-English-speaking countries. This work provides a reusable framework for funder name disambiguation with potential applicability across different model architectures and datasets, featuring cost-effective training data creation and multi-task learning and disambiguation.

</details>


### [37] [VLX-VR: An Agentic-Aware Video Reasoning Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09985)
*Sheng Li,Peng Liu,Qianqian Zhang,Tiancheng Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: VLX-VR is an agentic multimodal video reasoning model that iteratively decides what evidence to gather, how to use memory, and when to stop, achieving state-of-the-art performance and stable cross-duration behavior on the MINERVA video understanding benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video understanding systems usually process a fixed video segment in a single pass, which prevents them from adaptively acquiring additional evidence when the initial observation is incomplete, ambiguous, or conflicting. This limits their ability to perform realistic, complex video reasoning that must integrate visual, audio, textual, and temporal cues over time. The paper aims to design a model that can actively and iteratively seek out relevant video evidence, use memory, and reason in a way that better matches real-world demands.

Method: The authors propose VLX-VR, an "agentic-aware" video reasoning model embedded in a Think--Memory--Observation loop. At each reasoning step, the model: (1) plans what evidence is needed next; (2) calls read_memory or write_memory to retrieve or store information; (3) incorporates the newly obtained Observation; and (4) decides whether to continue reasoning or output an answer. Training uses multimodal data consisting of videos and agent trajectories, and reinforcement learning is applied so the model can learn effective strategies for evidence acquisition, memory utilization, and deciding when to terminate reasoning.

Result: On the MINERVA video reasoning benchmark, VLX-VR achieves 78.79% overall accuracy, which is state-of-the-art among compared models. When evaluated across three original video duration groups, it attains accuracies of 76.70%, 78.73%, and 80.92%, indicating a low cross-duration variance of 2.97 pp^2 and thus stable performance across video lengths. Regarding reasoning quality, 96.20% of reasoning traces for correctly answered questions align with MINERVA's reference reasoning traces and their cited evidence, and around 75.80% of all evaluated samples satisfy both answer correctness and this evidence-grounded reasoning trace criterion.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that an agentic, loop-based video reasoning framework with explicit memory operations and learned evidence acquisition can deliver both strong quantitative performance and qualitatively consistent reasoning on a challenging video benchmark. VLX-VR shows robustness across different video durations and produces reasoning traces that largely match human-annotated references. Nonetheless, certain complex reasoning skills—such as counting, tracking state changes, inferring causality, and understanding spatial relations—remain difficult, pointing to avenues for future improvement in video reasoning models.

Abstract: Real-world video understanding requires integrating visual, audio, textual, and temporal evidence distributed across a video. Yet many pipelines use a fixed video context and single-pass inference, limiting adaptive evidence acquisition when observations are incomplete, ambiguous, or conflicting. We present VLX-VR, an agentic-aware video reasoning model trained within a video reasoning framework defined by a Think--Memory--Observation loop. At each step, VLX-VR determines the needed evidence, invokes read_memory or write_memory, incorporates the returned Observation, and decides whether to continue or produce the task output. We train VLX-VR with multimodal data, including videos and agent trajectories, using reinforcement learning to learn evidence acquisition, memory use, and termination. On MINERVA, VLX-VR achieves state-of-the-art performance among the models included in our comparison, with 78.79% accuracy. Under the original three duration groups, its accuracies are 76.70%, 78.73%, and 80.92%, with a cross-duration accuracy variance of 2.97~$\mathrm{pp}^2$. On correctly answered samples, 96.20% of VLX-VR's reasoning traces are consistent with the MINERVA reference reasoning traces and the evidence described by them, while approximately 75.80% of all evaluated samples satisfy both answer correctness and this evidence-grounded trace criterion. These results show strong performance and broadly stable behavior across durations, while counting, state changes, causal reasoning, and spatial perception remain challenging.

</details>


### [38] [Stable Answers, Unfinished Reasoning: Why Self-Consensus Is Not a Safe Early-Exit Signal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09989)
*Yunxiang Mo,Donghao Zhao,Hejia Geng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates 'self-consensus' stopping rules for reasoning models and finds that agreement among probes on a partial trajectory is not a safe or reliable signal to stop generation, due to a consensus-termination gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce inference cost for reasoning models by stopping generation early when possible, and to test whether self-consensus rules can provide a reusable, safe, and token-saving stopping criterion.

Method: The authors preregister a sweep of 3,520 self-consensus stopping rules and evaluate them on frozen trajectories from two reasoning models across three benchmarks, applying three pre-defined safety and efficiency gates. They validate the frontier of rules on a held-out split and two unseen models, and compare against a boundary-confidence control method (DEER). They also analyze the nature of agreement via probe re-wording and a hand-labeled error taxonomy.

Result: No self-consensus rule passes all three acceptance gates, while the DEER boundary-confidence method does. Even at a rule that saves 32% of tokens, about 1 in 9 early stops occur on answers that the same trajectory later abandons; broadening the agreement requirement only reduces this error rate to about 7% while eroding token savings to around 8%. Analysis shows many agreed-upon answers are unstable placeholders that the model has not committed to.

Conclusion: Agreement among probes in a partial trajectory is not a reliable stop signal because it captures persistence of the current answer under the probing procedure rather than true completion of reasoning—a consensus-termination gap. Using agreement alone as a stopping criterion is fundamentally flawed, not just too loose, because it systematically measures the wrong underlying property.

Abstract: A natural way to cut reasoning-model inference cost is to repeatedly probe a single partial trajectory for its current answer and stop once probes agree -- self-consensus. We ask whether any such rule is both safe and token-saving, and whether one can be selected once and reused. A preregistered sweep of 3,520 consensus rules, replayed on frozen trajectories from two models and three benchmarks, clears none of three acceptance gates fixed in advance; the frontier reproduces on a held-out split and on two unseen models -- while a boundary-confidence control (DEER) swept through the same pipeline clears all three. The reason lies in the signal: agreement establishes that the current answer persists under a fixed probing procedure, not that the reasoning has terminated -- a consensus-termination gap. Stopping on it commits non-terminal answers. At a rule still saving 32% of the tokens, one stop in nine fires on an answer the trajectory itself later abandons, and most of those stops cut off a correction it would otherwise have made. Widening the agreement window does not remove them: the share levels off near 7%, and by then the saving has fallen to 8%. Probe re-wording and a hand-labelled error taxonomy show the agreed answer is often a placeholder the model had not settled on. Used on its own as the stop signal, agreement fails not because it is insufficiently strict, but because it repeatedly measures the wrong object.

</details>


### [39] [SalamandraTA at WMT 2026 Terminology Shared Task: Hard Examples Are Better Teachers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09999)
*Xixian Liao,Maite Melero*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper improves terminology-aware machine translation by training only on examples where the model’s own translation contradicts the glossary, greatly boosting term accuracy without extra data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard terminology-aware MT fine-tunes on all glossary-annotated sentence pairs, but many of these are redundant because the model already uses the prescribed terms. This wastes training capacity and does not effectively teach the model to resolve conflicts between its preferences and the glossary. The authors want a more data-efficient way to enforce exact glossary term usage.

Method: They filter training data to keep only “hard” examples where the base model’s translation violates the glossary. Using a two-way synthetic data pipeline with open models, they generate such filtered, glossary-annotated data. They then include this data in the instruction-tuning mixture for SalamandraTA-7b-instruct v3.0. The system is used as-is, wrapped in a document-level inference pipeline, for the WMT26 Terminology Shared Task Track 1, relying solely on supervised fine-tuning rather than RL methods.

Result: In controlled experiments at fixed data volume, filtering to contradictory examples increases term accuracy from 78.7% to 89.9%. In the WMT26 shared task, their system attains 94.2% terminology success and 74.6 chrF++, with only 2 out of 22 submissions performing better on both metrics. On the previous year’s benchmark, it outperforms their prior GRPO-based system, despite using only standard supervised fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Focusing training on cases where the model disagrees with the glossary is a highly effective and data-efficient way to improve terminology adherence in MT. This selective supervised fine-tuning can rival or surpass more complex RL-based approaches and yields competitive performance in shared-task evaluations when integrated into a document-level inference pipeline.

Abstract: Terminology-aware translation asks for more than a correct translation: the output must use the exact terms a glossary prescribes. The standard recipe, fine-tuning on glossary-annotated translation pairs, hides an inefficiency: for most examples the glossary prescribes exactly what the model would have produced anyway, so they teach nothing about following a glossary. We therefore keep only the examples where the model's own translation contradicts the glossary. In a controlled study at fixed data volume, this selection alone raises term accuracy from 78.7% to 89.9%. The filtered data, built by a two-way synthetic pipeline on open models, is part of the instruction-tuning mixture of our public release SalamandraTA-7b-instruct v3.0, which, used exactly as released and wrapped in a document-level inference pipeline, forms the BSC submission to the WMT26 Terminology Shared Task Track 1. At the official WMT26 evaluation, our system achieves 94.2% term success at 74.6 chrF++, with only two of the twenty-two submissions outperforming it on both metrics. On last year's benchmark, it also surpasses our GRPO-based system, despite being trained solely with ordinary supervised fine-tuning.

</details>


### [40] [MedDeID enables locally governed clinical-text de-identification from real or synthetic training data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10049)
*Stig Hellemans,Tom Stroobants,Elyne Scheurwegs,Pieter Meysman,Philippe G. Jorens,Kris Laukens*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MedDeID is an on-premises framework for de-identifying clinical notes, enabling safe reuse of data through models trained on local and/or synthetic text with strong PII detection performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical notes are rich data sources but contain PII, limiting their reuse for research and AI, especially when regulations or policy prevent data from leaving a hospital. Existing de-identification tools may require sharing data externally or training on real patient text, creating privacy risks and governance barriers. There is a need for a locally governed solution that can work with minimal or no access to real clinical text by leveraging synthetic data, while still achieving high-quality PII detection across settings and languages.

Method: The authors built MedDeID, an on-premises de-identification framework that integrates: (1) local manual annotation of notes; (2) generation of synthetic clinical notes; (3) training of compact transformer models for PII detection; (4) inference and pseudonymisation of clinical text; and (5) systematic evaluation. They trained and evaluated models on Dutch hospital notes using a 300-note independently annotated and adjudicated benchmark, compared models trained on real versus synthetic notes, tested generalisation to Dutch primary-care notes and robustness to identifier-format perturbations, and instantiated the workflow in English using only synthetic text and evaluated on external synthetic benchmarks.

Result: On the Dutch hospital benchmark, a compact transformer trained on hospital data detected 98.9% of identifying text (high recall) while only redacting 0.24% of non-identifier text (low over-redaction). A model trained solely on synthetic data reached 96.1% recall. On 100 Dutch primary-care notes, the synthetic-trained model outperformed the real-data-trained model in recall (90.3% vs 87.0%) and showed greater robustness to changes in identifier format. For English, a model trained without any real clinical text detected 99.7% and 98.9% of annotated identifier characters on two independent synthetic benchmarks, evidencing good performance of the workflow when transferred to another language on synthetic data.

Conclusion: MedDeID enables privacy-preserving, locally governed de-identification of clinical notes by supporting training on either real local data, synthetic data, or a mix of both, without requiring data to leave the institution. The framework achieves high PII detection performance in Dutch hospital data, competitive performance using only synthetic data with better robustness in some settings, and can be instantiated in other languages such as English. While English clinical performance remains to be validated on real notes, the work shows that realistic de-identification systems can be built and deployed entirely on-premises with strong privacy guarantees.

Abstract: Clinical notes contain personally identifiable information (PII), restricting reuse for research and medical AI, especially when data cannot leave an institution. We developed MedDeID, an on-premises framework combining in-house annotation and synthetic-note generation with model training, inference, pseudonymisation and evaluation. On an independently annotated, adjudicated 300-note Dutch hospital benchmark, a hospital-trained compact transformer detected 98.9% of identifying text while redacting 0.24% of text outside annotated identifiers; a synthetic-only counterpart detected 96.1%. On 100 primary-care notes, the synthetic-trained model achieved higher recall than the hospital-trained model (90.3% versus 87.0%) and greater robustness to identifier-format perturbations. An English instantiation trained without real text detected 99.7% and 98.9% of annotated identifier characters on two external synthetic benchmarks. These results demonstrate transfer of the workflow to another language, but not clinical English performance. MedDeID provides a route to locally governed de-identification using real or synthetic training data.

</details>


### [41] [Direct Diversity Optimization for Diverse Successful Trajectories in Preference Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10052)
*Junwon Ko,Dong-Jae Lee,Minchan Kwon,Sunghyun Baek,Junmo Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Direct Diversity Optimization (DDO), a post-training method for LLM agents that improves both success rates and diversity of successful strategies in sequential decision tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing trajectory-level post-training on outcome labels tends to collapse multiple successful strategies at the same decision state into a single preferred path, limiting robustness and exploration. The authors want to explicitly preserve and utilize multiple distinct successful branches for better coverage of successful strategies under a limited rollout budget.

Method: They propose Direct Diversity Optimization (DDO), an offline post-training framework that: (1) uses Divergence-Tree Collection (DTC) to construct state-aligned branching structures that group different successful trajectories sharing the same decision states; and (2) trains with the Reference-Relative Target-Odds (RTO) objective, which optimizes the model to match reference-relative probability targets over multiple successful alternatives instead of pushing all mass to a single action. This directly encourages the model to assign non-trivial probability to diverse successful continuations.

Result: On three benchmarks—BabyAI, BabaIsAI, and WebShop—DDO achieves the best task success and successful strategy coverage among compared post-training baselines. It also yields the highest recovery rate when local actions are perturbed (local action replacement) and outperforms both successful-only imitation learning approaches and decoding-time diversification baselines on success and coverage metrics.

Conclusion: Explicitly optimizing for diversity among successful strategies during post-training, via DTC and the RTO objective, leads to LLM agents that not only solve tasks more reliably but also maintain broader coverage over multiple valid strategies, improving robustness to perturbations and outperforming existing post-training and decoding diversification methods.

Abstract: LLM agents for sequential decision tasks are often post-trained with trajectory-level outcome labels, but such labels provide little supervision for preserving multiple successful branches from the same decision state. We study this problem as successful strategy coverage: how broadly a model realizes distinct successful strategies under a fixed rollout budget. We present Direct Diversity Optimization (DDO), an offline post-training method that combines Divergence-Tree Collection (DTC) with the Reference-Relative Target-Odds Objective (RTO). DTC constructs state-aligned branch sets rooted at shared decision states, and RTO trains the model to match reference-relative targets over successful alternatives. DDO achieves the strongest task success and successful strategy coverage among the compared post-training methods across BabyAI, BabaIsAI, and WebShop. It also achieves the highest recovery rate after local action replacement and higher task success and coverage than successful-only imitation and decoding-time diversification controls.

</details>


### [42] [Data-Centric Post-Training for Financial Reasoning: Mining, Distillation, and Verifiable Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10113)
*Zhirayr Hayrapetyan,Andrei Kalmykov,Denis Kokosinskii,Dmitry Stanishevskii,Dmitry Zmitrovich*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a data-centric pipeline and retention-aware adaptation methods to improve financial reasoning in language models without degrading pre-existing capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing financial QA and educational texts are abundant but poorly suited to reasoning-focused post-training because they lack explicit reasoning chains, sufficient context, or verifiable answers. Naive supervised fine-tuning often hurts existing financial abilities. The authors aim to construct better training data and adaptation strategies that enhance financial reasoning while preserving prior strengths.

Method: They build complementary datasets by mining open-source reasoning traces, distilling financial instruction data, and generating knowledge-graph-guided QA pairs from educational material. After semantic deduplication, they use three small sequence classifiers to (1) filter for finance-relevant data, (2) discard under-specified questions, and (3) select tasks amenable to RL with rule-based verifiers. For model adaptation, they compare standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT), self-distilled SFT, RL (GRPO) on verifiable tasks, and post-training model merging, with a focus on mitigating capability loss.

Result: On FINESSE-Bench, ordinary SFT reduces accuracy by 3.2–4.0 percentage points relative to the base models. In contrast, self-distilled SFT improves over base models by 1.0–2.8 points. Equal-weight merging of models recovers 3.0 points over its SFT parent and ends 0.9 points above the original model. Applying GRPO on hard tasks adds 0.4 points on top of self-distilled SFT, or 3.0 points when used directly on verifiable tasks.

Conclusion: Carefully designed, retention-aware data pipelines and adaptation strategies—particularly self-distilled SFT, model merging, and targeted RL with verifiers—can enhance financial reasoning performance while avoiding the regressions commonly caused by standard SFT. This demonstrates a viable path for improving domain-specific reasoning without sacrificing existing model capabilities.

Abstract: Financial text, textbooks, and question-answer pairs are abundant, but only a small fraction is directly usable for reasoning-focused post-training. Existing QA pairs often lack explicit reasoning, sufficient context, or reliably verifiable answers, while textbooks must first be transformed into synthetic training examples.
