We present a data-centric pipeline that constructs complementary corpora by mining open-source reasoning traces, distilling financial instruction data, and generating knowledge-graph-guided question-answer pairs from financial educational material. After semantic deduplication, three lightweight sequence classifiers select finance-relevant examples, reject under-specified questions, and identify tasks suitable for reinforcement learning with compact rule-based verifiers. For model adaptation, we study supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, while self-distilled fine-tuning and post-training model merging are used to prevent the loss of financial capabilities already present in the starting model. We evaluate the adapted language models using FINESSE-Bench, reporting aggregate performance and changes relative to their starting checkpoints. Across the selected comparisons, ordinary SFT reduces FINESSE-Bench accuracy by 3.2-4.0 percentage points, whereas self-distilled SFT improves over the corresponding starting models by 1.0-2.8 points. Equal-weight merging recovers 3.0 points over its SFT parent and finishes 0.9 points above the original model; GRPO on hard tasks adds 0.4 points after self-distilled SFT or 3.0 points when applied directly to verifiable tasks. These results show that retention-aware adaptation can improve financial reasoning without the regressions observed after ordinary SFT.

</details>


### [43] [ProbPlug: A Plugin Uncertainty Network for Reliable Confidence in LLM Binary Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10122)
*Jianzong Wang,Chuhang Liu,Botao Zhao,Zuheng Kang,Xulong Zhang,Xiaoyang Qu,Junqing Peng,Zhiewei Ye,Yayun He*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces ProbPlug, a lightweight framework to estimate and calibrate confidence for LLM-based binary classification using internal token features without modifying the base model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs perform well on many classification tasks, but their confidence scores are often poorly calibrated, which is problematic in high-stakes applications requiring reliable uncertainty estimates. Existing work on confidence estimation for LLMs lacks effective, practical calibration methods for LLM-based classification, motivating a method that is reliable, easy to integrate, and broadly applicable.

Method: Freeze a pretrained LLM and extract internal token-level hidden representations during inference. Feed these features into a small self-attention-based module (ProbPlug) that aggregates token information and predicts whether the LLM’s output is correct, effectively providing a calibrated confidence score. The module is trained as a separate lightweight head and integrated into the inference pipeline without altering the base LLM.

Result: Across multiple text and multimodal binary classification tasks, ProbPlug yields more reliable confidence estimates than existing baselines, improves overall classification performance, and adds only negligible computational overhead. It also generalizes well across tasks, indicating robustness and practical deployability.

Conclusion: ProbPlug is an effective and practical add-on for LLM-based binary classification that delivers better-calibrated and more reliable confidence estimates by leveraging internal LLM representations through a self-attention module, all without modifying the base model. This makes it suitable for deployment in a range of real-world settings where trustworthy uncertainty estimates are required.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance across a broad range of classification settings, yet the reliability of their predictions remains a major obstacle to deployment in high-stakes scenarios. Although confidence estimation for LLMs has been widely studied, confidence calibration for LLM-based classification remains underexplored. We introduce ProbPlug, a lightweight confidence estimation framework for LLM-based binary classification, which predicts whether an output is correct using internal token features extracted from a frozen LLM. ProbPlug employs a self-attention module to aggregate hidden representations and can be integrated into the original inference pipeline without modifying the base model. Experiments across multiple tasks involving both text-based and multimodal large models show that ProbPlug provides more reliable confidence estimates, improves classification performance with negligible additional overhead, and exhibits strong generalization across tasks. These results indicate that ProbPlug serves as a practical solution for confidence estimation in LLM-based classification. Our code is publicly available at Github.

</details>


### [44] [Active Adaptation, Not Static Defense: Temporal Dynamics of Preventative Steering in Adversarial Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10142)
*Jing Guan,Yachao Yang,Zhaoliang Liu,Yuyao Zhang,Fanyu Meng,Junlan Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies why Preventative Steering—a defense against malicious fine-tuning of language models—works, finds it depends on ongoing adaptation rather than a fixed weight change, and proposes a new scheduling strategy that improves robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can be maliciously fine-tuned to adopt harmful personas, and existing defenses like Preventative Steering work but lack clear mechanistic understanding. The authors want to understand *how* this defense provides lasting protection and whether it can be made more robust and reliable.

Method: They analyze the temporal optimization dynamics of Preventative Steering during fine-tuning, identifying distinct adaptation phases and tracing where in parameter space the defensive updates concentrate (notably in attention output projections). They also design Intervention Delta Preservation (IDP) and IDP Continuation experiments, where they preserve or reapply the learned weight offsets, to test whether static weight changes are sufficient. Based on these insights, they introduce Progressive Intensity Scheduling (PIS), which gradually increases steering strength over time, and evaluate it on Qwen2.5 and Gemma-3 models.

Result: They find that Preventative Steering’s protection arises from an early compensatory adaptation phase and then a steady-state phase where the effect of the corrective signal decays. Defensive updates mainly route through attention output projections. IDP and IDP Continuation experiments show that simply preserving or re-injecting the weight offsets does not keep the model safe, indicating that Preventative Steering depends on continual active adaptation instead of a static defense. PIS, which ramps up steering strength over training, yields better safety robustness than a fixed-strength steering approach and simultaneously reduces harmful trait expression.

Conclusion: Preventative Steering defends against malicious fine-tuning through dynamic, ongoing adaptation rather than a single, static parameter offset, with attention output projections playing a key role. Leveraging this insight, Progressive Intensity Scheduling enhances and stabilizes the defense, providing stronger safety robustness on tested models like Qwen2.5 and Gemma-3 while limiting harmful trait expression.

Abstract: Large language models remain fragile against malicious fine-tuning, motivating training-time defenses against harmful persona drift. Preventative Steering injects undesirable-trait persona vectors during fine-tuning and removes them at evaluation time, yet the mechanism behind its lasting protection remains unclear. Analyzing its temporal optimization dynamics, we find that the defense emerges from an early compensatory adaptation phase followed by a steady-state phase where the corrective signal decays; in parameter space, attention output projections emerge as the dominant residual-write route for defensive updates. Through Intervention Delta Preservation (IDP) and IDP Continuation experiments, we further show that preserving or reinjecting the weight offset fails to maintain protection, indicating that preventative steering relies on active adaptation rather than a static defense. Motivated by this finding, we propose Progressive Intensity Scheduling (PIS), which starts with a moderate injection strength and increases it after static-strength alignment begins to decay. Across the evaluated Qwen2.5 and Gemma-3 models, PIS improves safety robustness over static-strength steering while reducing harmful trait expression.

</details>


### [45] [YallaMorph: A Benchmark for Evaluating Arabic Morphological Generation in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10153)
*Mahmoud Reda,Salam Khalifa,Reham Marzouk,Nizar Habash*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: YallaMorph is a large-scale benchmark to test how well language models can generate Arabic word forms with precise morphology, showing that this remains a difficult problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can produce fluent Arabic text but often fail at precise morphosyntactic control. Existing evaluations focus on downstream tasks and do not isolate the ability to generate specific morphological forms from given lexical roots and features. There is a need for a direct, large-scale evaluation of controlled Arabic morphological generation.

Method: The authors build YallaMorph, a large-scale benchmark for Arabic morphological generation that includes verbs, nouns, adjectives, their cliticized forms, and intentionally invalid morphological configurations. They then test both multilingual and Arabic-specialized language models on this benchmark under two settings: with diacritics and without diacritics, evaluating performance on over 600,000 benchmark instances.

Result: The evaluation shows that current models struggle with accurate Arabic morphological generation, with especially poor performance on cliticized forms, unseen forms, and morphologically rare word patterns. The difficulty persists across both diacritized and undiacritized settings.

Conclusion: Despite seeming fluency, LLMs lack robust control over Arabic morphology. YallaMorph provides a focused benchmark revealing these weaknesses and can be used to drive future improvements in Arabic morphological modeling and controlled generation.

Abstract: Arabic morphology remains challenging for large language models, since fluent generation does not guarantee accurate morphosyntactic control. Existing Arabic evaluations mainly target downstream tasks and do not directly test controlled morphological generation from explicit lexical and feature-based input. We introduce YallaMorph, a large-scale benchmark for Arabic morphological generation covering verbs, nouns, adjectives, their cliticized forms, and invalid configurations. We evaluate multilingual and Arabic-oriented LLMs under diacritized and undiacritized settings over 600K benchmark entries. Results show that Arabic morphological generation remains difficult, especially for cliticized, unseen, and morphologically rare forms.

</details>


### [46] [From Retrieval to Weights: Parametric Individualization of Small Language Models with Individual Text Corpora](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10155)
*Christoph Wigbels,Ali Abusaleh,Markus T. Jansen,Alexander Mehler,Markus J. Hofmann*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how personal web-browsing text can be consolidated into small language models to simulate individual episodic/semantic memory for multiple-choice question answering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether and how individual text corpora derived from people’s web search histories can be integrated into language models to emulate human-like episodic and semantic memory, and to enable more personalized, cognitively plausible tutoring or QA systems.

Method: The authors web-crawled search histories from 515 participants who completed 36 multiple-choice knowledge questions, then focused on a stratified sample of 150 participants. For each person, they built an Individual Text Corpus (ITC) from their history and trained a separate DoRA adapter on top of a small language model whose baseline performance was intentionally below the lower quartile of human performance. They used retrieval-augmented generation and fine-tuned the adapters on each person’s ITC, then evaluated: (1) whether the adapted model’s weights preferentially captured its own participant’s held-out text versus other people’s text, and (2) how the personalization affected performance on a generalized multiple-choice knowledge test using log-loss and bias-corrected PMI-based accuracy, with and without retrieval.

Result: Each DoRA adapter encoded its participant’s ITC into the model: the adapted SLM fit its own participant’s held-out text substantially better than other participants’ texts (effect size dz = 1.27), and this individuality effect strengthened with larger ITCs. On the knowledge test, the adapter improved log-loss alignment (a distributional measure of knowledge) but did not improve accuracy when evaluated via a bias-corrected PMI readout, indicating added knowledge but not better match to the individual’s item-level answers. Retrieval-augmented generation did not provide additional gains beyond what was already in the adapted weights.

Conclusion: Individual web-search text can be consolidated into the weights of small language models via DoRA adapters, yielding models that reflect person-specific linguistic regularities and knowledge, and improving probabilistic knowledge measures. However, this does not yet equate to accurate simulation of an individual’s answer patterns or full episodic/semantic memory. The work suggests a promising direction for individualized tutoring agents while highlighting the need for further developments to more faithfully capture individual cognitive profiles and memory dynamics.

Abstract: We approach a cognitive simulation perspective on episodic and semantic memory in multiple-choice question answering by incorporating text from individual text corpora (ITC) into retrieval-augmented generation and DoRA fine-tuning. We web-crawl the search histories of 515 participants who answered 36 multiple-choice knowledge items and analyze a stratified subsample of 150 participants. For each participant, one DoRA adapter consolidates their ITC into a small language model (SLM) whose baseline correctness falls below the participants' lowest quartile. The adapter measurably writes the ITC into the weights: it fits its own participant's held-out text better than other participants' texts (dz =1.27), an individuality effect that increases with ITC size in rank order. On the generalized knowledge test, however, the adapter adds knowledge rather than alignment with the individual: log-loss match improves, whereas match accuracy under a bias-corrected PMI readout does not, and retrieval adds nothing on top. Our results demonstrate that ITCs can be consolidated into the weights of SLMs, an encouraging basis for individualized tutoring agents, and we discuss how to move from there toward a realistic simulation of episodic and semantic memory at the individual level.

</details>


### [47] [Who Argues What? Joint Argument-Entity Detection and Classification in Political Debates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10192)
*Lucio La Cava,Stefano Francesco Monea,Sergio Greco*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper enhances argument mining for political debates by jointly tagging arguments and debate-specific named entities using a new entity-enriched dataset and a generative tagging framework, achieving large F1 gains over pipeline baselines and generalizing to essays.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing argument mining work on political debates focuses on argumentative spans and roles but ignores the debate-specific entities (people, events, organizations, etc.) that make arguments interpretable. There is no standardized layer capturing such Debate Named Entities (DNEs), nor a method that jointly models arguments and entities. This limits our ability to ask which actors and events are invoked, and to develop systems that understand debates at both argumentative and entity levels.

Method: 1) They construct DNE-ElecDeb, an entity-enriched version of USElecDeb, by annotating Debate Named Entities (DNEs) across both argumentative and non-argumentative spans, and define Debate Named Entity Recognition (DNER) as detecting these entities. 2) They propose JAET (Joint Argument and Entity Tagging), a generative framework that fine-tunes decoder-only large language models to output the original debate transcript with inline tags marking both argument spans and entity mentions. Performance is evaluated via BIO tagging on joint argument mining plus DNER, comparing against strong sequential (pipeline) baselines that perform AM then DNER or vice versa.

Result: Under BIO-tagging evaluation, JAET yields large relative F1 improvements on the joint AM+DNER task compared to the best sequential AM-DNER pipelines: +27.3% in the untyped setting and +41.9% in the typed setting on political debates. Similar or even larger relative gains are observed on a distinct Persuasive Essays dataset (+26.6% untyped, +52.7% typed), indicating strong cross-domain generalization and showing that the benefits of joint modeling cannot be matched by simply composing two separate modules.

Conclusion: By creating an entity-augmented debate dataset and a joint generative tagging framework, the paper shows that unifying argumentative structure and debate-specific entities in a single model substantially improves performance over pipeline approaches and generalizes beyond political debates. This unified representation supports richer, more interpretable analysis of debates by capturing both how arguments are structured and which actors and events they invoke.

Abstract: Political debates are often analyzed through Argument Mining (AM) to investigate the key arguments that drive them. However, political arguments are rarely interpretable from argumentative spans alone, as claims and premises generally depend on the entities (e.g., people, events, locations, parties) they mention. Existing AM resources and methods typically annotate argumentative spans and roles, but do not provide a paired debate-entity layer for asking which Debate Named Entities (DNE), e.g., actors and events, are invoked within debates. In this work, we address these data and methodological gaps by (i) introducing DNE-ElecDeb, an entity-enriched version of the USElecDeb dataset that adds DNEs in both argumentative and non-argumentative spans and defines Debate Named Entity Recognition (DNER) as the task of detecting DNEs, and (ii) proposing Joint Argument and Entity Tagging (JAET), a generative framework that fine-tunes decoder-only LLMs to insert inline argument and entity tags into debate turns while preserving the original transcript. Under BIO-tagging evaluation, JAET improves relative F1 on the joint AM+DNER task by +27.3%, resp. +41.9%, under the untyped, resp. typed setting over the strongest sequential AM-DNER pipelines, demonstrating that such gains cannot be recovered by composing two independent modules. Notably, similar margins replicate on Persuasive Essays (+26.6%, resp. +52.7%), showing effective generalization to domains orthogonal to political debates. By unifying argumentative and entity-level representations within a single view, our contributions pave the way for richer political debates understanding.

</details>


### [48] [Politics of Feelings: Emotional Expression and Legislative Effectiveness in the U.S. Congress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10198)
*Segun Aroyehun*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Study uses 1.7M U.S. congressional speeches (1973–2024) and a transformer-based classifier to measure 8 emotions, linking emotional expression patterns to legislators’ effectiveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although emotions are central to political communication, most work only describes when and how emotions are expressed, not whether they are systematically linked to concrete legislative outcomes such as effectiveness in passing bills or shaping policy. The authors aim to bridge this gap by connecting fine-grained measures of discrete emotions in legislative speeches to measurable performance within democratic institutions.

Method: The authors collect over 1.7 million speeches from the U.S. Congress spanning 1973–2024. They apply a transformer-based emotion classification model to each speech to estimate eight discrete emotions: anger, fear, disgust, sadness, joy, enthusiasm, pride, and hope. They then conduct observational analyses of how emotion levels vary over time, across policy topics, and by legislator characteristics (such as ideology). Finally, they statistically relate multiple emotion metrics—discrete emotions, emotional valence, diversity, and intensity—to measures of legislative effectiveness.

Result: (1) Emotional language in congressional speeches has increased over time. (2) Emotional profiles differ systematically by policy domain and by legislators’ ideological positions. (3) Specific emotions have distinct associations with legislative effectiveness: enthusiasm and pride correlate positively with higher effectiveness, while anger correlates negatively. (4) Aggregate features of emotional expression matter: higher emotional valence and diversity are positively related to legislative effectiveness, whereas higher emotional intensity is negatively related.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that computationally inferred discrete emotions from legislative speeches are meaningfully related to legislators’ performance and behavior. Emotional expression is not merely stylistic; different emotions and overall emotional profiles correspond to differences in legislative effectiveness. This demonstrates the utility of large-scale, transformer-based emotion analysis for understanding communication, interaction, and role performance in democratic institutions.

Abstract: Emotions are a pervasive feature of political communication, yet existing research has focused primarily on describing patterns of emotional expression rather than examining whether they are associated with consequential legislative outcomes. We address this gap by investigating the expression and correlates of discrete emotions in more than 1.7 million speeches delivered in the U.S. Congress between 1973 and 2024. Using a transformer-based emotion classifier, we measure eight discrete emotions: anger, fear, disgust, sadness, joy, enthusiasm, pride, and hope. We examine how these emotions vary over time, across policy topics, legislator characteristics, and their relationship with legislative effectiveness. We find that congressional speeches are becoming emotionally expressive over time. Emotional expression also varies systematically across policy domains and ideological positioning of legislators. Notably, the relationship between emotional expression and legislative effectiveness depends on the specific emotions expressed: enthusiasm and pride are positively associated with effectiveness, whereas anger exhibits a negative association. Emotional valence and emotional diversity are positively associated with legislative effectiveness, while emotional intensity is negatively associated with legislative effectiveness. These findings demonstrate that computationally derived measures of discrete emotions can provide insight into affective dimensions of legislative speeches and facilitate our understanding of how legislators communicate, interact, and perform within democratic institutions.

</details>


### [49] [Quantifying Logical Consistency in Transformers via Query-Key Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17017)
*Eduard Tulchinskii,Anastasia Voznyuk,Laida Kushnareva,Andrei Andriiainen,Irina Piontkovskaya,Evgeny Burnaev,Serguei Barannikov*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They introduce a lightweight way to evaluate LLM logical reasoning quality using internal attention query-key scores from selected heads, distinguishing valid from invalid inferences in one forward pass.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs still struggle with multi-step logical reasoning. Existing improvements like Chain-of-Thought help generate step-by-step solutions but lack a built-in way to judge whether the intermediate reasoning steps are logically coherent. Current evaluation and analysis techniques (e.g., ablations) can be expensive and unscalable. The authors want a scalable, inexpensive method to assess logical coherence using the model’s internal mechanics.

Method: They analyze query-key (QK) alignments within transformer attention heads and define a "QK-score" from specific, carefully chosen heads during a single forward pass. This score acts as a proxy for logical coherence. Instead of perturbing or ablating the model, they read out latent representations already present in attention to determine whether an inference step is valid or invalid. They then test this method on multiple logical reasoning benchmarks and a variety of LLM sizes (1.5B–70B parameters).

Result: The proposed QK-score derived from attention heads can reliably distinguish valid from invalid inferences, revealing latent structure associated with logical reasoning. Compared to traditional ablation-based approaches, their method scales better and is more robust in the presence of distractors and for tasks that require deeper reasoning. This holds across several benchmarks and across models from 1.5B to 70B parameters.

Conclusion: Internal attention patterns, specifically query-key alignments in certain heads, contain linearly readable signals about logical validity. A single-pass QK-score offers a practical, scalable way to evaluate and probe LLM logical reasoning, providing robustness to distractors and deeper reasoning depth without expensive interventions such as ablations.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive performance in various natural language processing tasks, yet their ability to perform multi-step logical reasoning remains an open challenge. Although Chain-of-Thought prompting has improved logical reasoning by enabling models to generate intermediate steps, it lacks mechanisms to assess the coherence of these logical transitions. In this paper, we propose a novel, lightweight evaluation strategy for logical reasoning that uses query-key alignments inside transformer attention heads. By computing a single forward pass and extracting a "QK-score" from carefully chosen heads, our method reveals latent representations that reliably separate valid from invalid inferences, offering a scalable alternative to traditional ablation-based techniques. We also provide an empirical validation on multiple logical reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating improved robustness of our evaluation method against distractors and increased reasoning depth. The experiments were conducted on a diverse set of models, ranging from 1.5B to 70B parameters.

</details>


### [50] [Through the Looking Glass: Directly Reading and Writing Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10210)
*Mark Oskin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how many internal components of a transformer meaningfully contribute to predicting a token, finding that only a small, sparse subset is truly decisive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and quantify how predictions in transformer models are distributed across internal components (units, channels, heads), and whether predictions rely on many components or just a small critical subset, for better interpretability and controllability.

Method: They attribute each component's signed contribution to the output logit for a token, analyze contribution distributions across 18 transformer models, and trace which components a prediction depends on and reads from using only existing parameters and activations, without extra training or fitting. They also experiment with targeted edits by installing new units and attention heads to verify causal control.

Result: They find that although thousands to hundreds of thousands of components contribute to a prediction, positive and negative contributions largely cancel, and a small subset (dozens or fewer components) is sufficient and often necessary for a prediction. This sufficient set covers only 1–3% of model parameters, a fraction that does not grow with model size. They further show that components can be named on both write and read sides, that sorting residual components by upstream source reveals grammatical categories, and that installing new units/heads enables precise, low-cost behavioral edits.

Conclusion: Transformer predictions are effectively driven by a sparse, identifiable subset of components whose influence can be quantified and manipulated. This sparsity is stable across model scales and enables targeted, low-rank-like edits that alter model behavior with minimal impact on overall performance, improving mechanistic interpretability and control of transformer models.

Abstract: How many of a transformer's components decide a token? Counted by the absolute value of each unit's and channel's contribution to the logit, one prediction rests on thousands to hundreds of thousands of them. But contributions are signed, and across eighteen models the mass pushing away from the predicted token is a median of seven times the mass carrying it. Divide by the net and the count is dozens: on the baseline, 53 components carry ninety percent of a prediction, 13 it cannot survive losing, and 8 suffice to produce it alone. Across twelve models trained elsewhere, 124M to 7B parameters, the sufficient set runs from two components to sixteen, and what a prediction draws on, followed all the way back, is one to three percent of the model, a share that does not grow with size. Three quarters of a layer's update is a fixed linear map of the state it received.
  Everything is read from the model's own parameters and activations, with nothing trained or fitted, and it names a component on both sides: what it writes, from the predictions it drives, reaching close to half of every model; what it reads, from its weights in the frame of its own layer, at 58.9 percent above chance over its eight strongest inputs. Sorting the remainder by upstream source yields grammatical categories the embedding cannot see.
  A name can be acted on. An association the model does not hold installs into one spare unit, key and value read from the weights, for a quarter of a percent of held-out loss, a fortieth of what a rank-one update costs. An installed attention head and a unit two layers above it make an edit fire only where a token occurred earlier in the context, and a unit the model trained for itself is driven from two layers upstream, 86 percent of the effect passing through it. An order-preserving activation puts a unit's inputs at the instrument's ceiling, at the price of a two-part install.

</details>


### [51] [From Plausible to Actionable: A Position on LLM Self-Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15957)
*Elize Herrewijnen,Benedetta Muscato,Gizem Gezici,Fosca Giannotti*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Opinion paper examining large language model (LLM) self-explanations, arguing they are often plausible but not necessarily faithful, and proposing ways to evaluate and leverage them for actionable decision support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can produce coherent natural language explanations of their own outputs, which are being used as a form of explainable AI. However, it is unclear whether these self-explanations accurately represent the models’ true internal reasoning processes. Existing XAI evaluation practices may not be suitable for this new setting, creating a need to rethink how we assess and use LLM-generated explanations.

Method: Conceptual and critical analysis rather than empirical experimentation. The authors review conventional XAI notions of plausibility and faithfulness, analyze how these apply to LLM self-explanations, point out limitations of standard evaluation protocols, and then formulate practical guidelines and an extended framework that also incorporates actionability as a key criterion.

Result: The paper clarifies that LLM self-explanations can be simultaneously (a) very plausible to humans, (b) only weakly or questionably faithful to the model’s actual internal processes, yet (c) still highly useful for guiding human decisions and interactions. It delineates where current evaluation methods fall short and articulates criteria and guidelines for systematically assessing plausibility, faithfulness, and actionability of LLM rationalizations.

Conclusion: Self-explanations from LLMs should not be treated as transparent windows into model reasoning, but as rationalizations whose faithfulness must be carefully scrutinized. Nonetheless, these explanations can be productively harnessed if we explicitly evaluate and design for plausibility, faithfulness, and especially actionability. The field of XAI should broaden its evaluation focus to include how well such explanations support informed decision-making for different stakeholders.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate natural language explanations that rationalize their own decisions, a phenomenon commonly referred to as self-explanations. Such explanations have emerged as a promising direction for explainable artificial intelligence (XAI), particularly for interpreting LLM behavior. However, while self-explanations often appear plausible, whether they faithfully reflect a model's underlying reasoning process remains an open question. In this opinion paper, we argue that self-explanations can be highly plausible, questionably faithful, and yet highly actionable. From a traditional XAI perspective, we identify the limitations of standard evaluation protocols for LLM-generated self-explanations and propose practical guidelines for assessing their plausibility and faithfulness.Moreover, we argue that evaluation should extend beyond these criteria to actionability, highlighting applications of LLM rationalization capabilities that support informed decision-making and appropriate action across diverse stakeholders.

</details>


### [52] [$Φ$-Bench: Can Large Language Models Engineer the Infrastructure That Powers Them?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10226)
*Leilei Ding,Shumin Wang,Yuting Huang,Fanqi Wan,Yinmin Zhang,Qi Han,Yiming Xu,Feiyuan Zhang,Xiaomeng Chu,Guoliang You,Wuyang Zhang,Daxin Jiang,Yanyong Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Φ-Bench is a benchmark to evaluate how well LLMs can design and optimize the infrastructure that runs LLMs, from small kernels to full systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of LLMs in systems and code focus on narrow, short, or pre-specified tasks (single kernels, fixed operators, or given optimization goals). They do not capture open-ended, long-horizon engineering work needed to build and optimize full LLM infrastructure. The authors want a benchmark that reflects realistic research and engineering challenges in LLM systems.

Method: They build Φ-Bench from real-world, frontier research optimization problems and existing code repositories across the LLM infrastructure stack. The benchmark includes tasks with different difficulty and scope, from localized kernel/function completion through implementation of larger components to end-to-end system optimization. They then run extensive experiments with state-of-the-art LLMs on these tasks.

Result: Experiments show where current LLMs succeed and where they fail in complex infrastructure engineering tasks, across different layers and complexities, giving a profile of their current abilities and weaknesses.

Conclusion: Φ-Bench exposes that while LLMs already have nontrivial capability for infrastructure engineering, they still struggle with open-ended, long-horizon system design and optimization, and the benchmark helps clarify the remaining challenges for achieving more autonomous AI-driven optimization of LLM infrastructure.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in reasoning and code generation, raising the prospect that they could assist in developing and optimizing the very infrastructure that powers them. However, existing benchmarks mainly focus on isolated kernels, predefined operators, or pre-specified optimization targets, and therefore fail to evaluate the ability of LLMs to perform open-ended, long-horizon LLM infrastructure engineering. To address this gap, we present $Φ$-Bench, a benchmark for systematically evaluating LLMs on engineering the LLM infrastructure stack. Derived from optimization problems studied in frontier research and grounded in real-world code repositories, $Φ$-Bench provides broad coverage of the LLM infrastructure stack and spans tasks of varying complexity, ranging from localized kernel-level function completion to long-horizon implementation and end-to-end system optimization. Extensive experiments on frontier LLMs reveal their current capabilities and limitations in engineering complex LLM infrastructure, offering insights into the challenges that remain on the path toward autonomous optimization of future AI infrastructure.

</details>


### [53] [The Answer Path and the Grounding Instruction in LLM Question Answering over Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10237)
*Arquimedes Canedo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper empirically studies which design choices in graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) actually affect QA performance, finding that including the correct answer path and using grounding instructions matter, while syntax, triple order, and subgraph size largely do not.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph RAG pipelines for knowledge-graph question answering involve many arbitrary or under-justified design choices (what triples to retrieve, how to format them, in what order, and what instructions to give the LLM). Prior work often attributes gains or failures to these design aspects without carefully isolating their actual effects. The authors aim to disentangle and rigorously measure the impact of these choices across multiple LLMs and datasets, and to identify which parts of the pipeline really matter.

Method: They systematically vary four components of a graph RAG prompt—(1) which triples are included (especially whether the gold answer path is present), (2) the natural-language grounding instruction, (3) the syntax used to express triples, and (4) the order of triples—across six large language models and two KGQA benchmarks. Instead of using a learned retriever, they construct subgraphs from gold SPARQL queries, letting them precisely control recall vs. precision of the context. They then run controlled ablations: swapping non-answer-path triples with unrelated ones at fixed budget, removing answer-path triples, toggling grounding instructions, and changing syntax/order/subgraph size, and measure answer F1 scores.

Result: Including the correct answer-path triples is crucial: replacing off-path triples with unrelated ones at the same context size barely changes performance (+0.003 F1), but removing the answer path causes a large drop, indicating recall of the answer chain is what matters, not fine-grained context precision. Grounding instructions are also highly influential: with an empty context, instructing the model to answer only from provided facts collapses F1 from 0.299 to 0.035 (8.63× drop). This shows that applying a strict grounding instruction in one condition but not in the baseline can falsely suggest that graph context hurts performance at depth. The authors identify such a spurious effect in their own earlier analysis and retract that claim. Conversely, triple syntax, ordering, and subgraph size have no measurable impact at multi-hop depth within their tested ranges.

Conclusion: For graph-based RAG in multi-hop KGQA, the two design choices that matter are (i) ensuring the retrieved subgraph actually contains the answer path (high recall on the reasoning chain) and (ii) how the grounding instruction constrains model behavior. Precision in which extra triples are chosen, the textual syntax of triples, their order, and moderate changes in subgraph size appear largely irrelevant in the tested setting. Evaluations that apply grounding instructions asymmetrically across conditions can produce misleading conclusions about the value of graph context. Future work should focus on robust retrieval of answer paths and on fair, format-robust evaluation of grounding vs. free-form answering, with the comparison between strict grounding and correct context left as an open question.

Abstract: A graph retrieval-augmented generation pipeline chooses which triples to put in the prompt, a syntax to write them in, an order to write them in, and a sentence telling the model what to do with them. We vary all four over six large language models and two knowledge-graph question answering benchmarks. Two of the four choices move the answer and the other two are flat. The first is whether the answer path, the triples needed to reach the answer, is in the prompt at all. Holding the number of triples fixed and replacing every triple that is not on the chain with material from an unrelated entity changes answer accuracy by +0.003 F1, while removing the chain costs most of what the graph was worth. Retrieval budget belongs on recall, and precision in the range we can test buys nothing. There is no retriever here: subgraphs come from gold SPARQL, so precision describes the context we build, not a system setting. The second is the grounding instruction. With no facts in the prompt, telling a model to answer using only the provided facts drops F1 from 0.299 to 0.035, a factor of 8.63. That figure describes an evaluation with an empty context arm rather than a working pipeline, and an experiment that applies the instruction to its context arm but not to its no-context baseline manufactures a spurious finding that graph context hurts at depth. We found one in our own results and retract it. Syntax, triple order and subgraph size produce no effect we can measure at multi-hop depth. The comparison that would price the grounding instruction against correct context is not measurable with a format-sensitive scorer, because the instruction determines the response format; we report it as an open contrast rather than a number.

</details>


### [54] [Two-Token Features and Small-Large Ensembles for VLM Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10244)
*Eli Schwartz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A system for detecting hallucinations in character-level vision-language models by combining a fine-tuned small VLM token classifier with a large zero-shot VLM judge, using synthetic data and OCR, achieves top-10 performance across multiple languages in SHROOM-Visions 2026.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve detection of hallucinated content generated by vision-language models at a fine-grained (character) level, and to perform well in the SHROOM-Visions 2026 shared task on hallucination detection across multiple languages.

Method: Fine-tune a 4B-parameter VLM as a per-token classifier that uses a two-token feature extracted from its hidden states, and combine (ensemble) its predictions with those of a much larger (~400B) zero-shot VLM judge at inference. Both models use OCR outputs for grounding on visible text in images. Synthetic hallucination data is generated by the large model to diversify training, and validation experiments choose the best internal feature layer, training set composition, and OCR grounding strategy.

Result: On the shared task's hidden test set, the system achieves mean Cor 0.487 and Cor-lbl 0.387. It ranks 6th of 28 systems for English, 6th of 21 for French, 8th of 21 for Italian, and 7th of 22 for Chinese on the primary Cor-lbl metric, indicating competitive top-10 performance across languages.

Conclusion: An ensemble of a fine-tuned small VLM token classifier with a large zero-shot VLM judge, supported by OCR-based grounding and synthetic hallucination data, is effective for character-level hallucination detection in multimodal systems and yields competitive multilingual results in a standardized benchmark.

Abstract: We present our system for the SHROOM-Visions 2026 shared task on character-level VLM hallucination detection. A small ($4$B-parameter) VLM is fine-tuned as a per-token classifier reading a two-token feature from its own hidden states, and is ensembled with a $\sim$400B zero-shot VLM judge at prediction time. Both components see off-the-shelf OCR of any visible in-image text. We use synthetic hallucination data generated by the large model as a source of ensemble diversity, and use validation to select feature layer, training data and OCR grounding. Our official entry reaches mean Cor $0.487$ / Cor-lbl $0.387$ on the hidden test set, placing $6$th/$28$ (EN), $6$th/$21$ (FR), $8$th/$21$ (IT) and $7$th/$22$ (ZH) on the task's primary Cor-lbl metric.

</details>


### [55] [DiSCo: A Distribution-First Steering and Cultural Prior Evaluation Framework for Measuring Cultural Preference Bias in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10253)
*Bhuvan Arora,Devesh Saraogi,Sravya Varada,Dhruv Kumar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes DiSCo, a new framework to measure large language models’ default cultural preferences and how steerable they are toward different cultures, showing strong bias toward UK/US and limits of prompt-based cultural personalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current cultural benchmarks assume a single correct answer, which fails when multiple culturally grounded responses are valid and obscures which cultures an LLM defaults to. They also mix up a model’s inherent cultural priors with its ability to adapt when given cultural context. There is a need for tools that expose these default cultural tendencies and rigorously test whether prompting can meaningfully shift them, especially as LLMs are deployed globally and may systematically favor some cultures over others.

Method: Introduce DiSCo, a distribution-first, forced-choice evaluation framework with a four-level context gradient (C0–C3) to separate default cultural priors from context-driven adaptation. Build DiSCo-Bench, a 304-item benchmark derived from BLEnD, covering 12 cultures. Evaluate six instruction-tuned LLMs by measuring how their choice distributions over culturally grounded options change across increasing levels of cultural context in the prompts.

Result: The evaluated LLMs’ default cultural priors are highly skewed: UK and US options account for about 35% of all selections even though they represent only 2 of the 12 cultures tested. When cultural steering is attempted via prompts, the gap between high-resource and low-resource cultures increases rather than decreases. Adding explicit cultural facts into prompts causes only minimal changes in the selection distributions.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit concentrated cultural preference priors heavily favoring UK/US, and these biases persist despite attempts at prompt-based steering. In fact, prompting tends to reinforce disparities between high- and low-resource cultures, and explicit cultural information in prompts barely disrupts existing distributions. Therefore, cultural preference bias in LLMs cannot be effectively addressed by prompt-based personalization alone, underscoring the need for deeper changes in data, training, or architectures to achieve more equitable cultural behavior.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in globally used assistants, yet their default choices in culturally grounded everyday situations can systematically favour some cultures over others, affecting localisation, user trust, and equitable behaviour. Existing cultural benchmarks evaluate accuracy against a single "correct" answer, making it difficult to characterise an LLM's cultural preference prior when multiple culturally grounded responses are all valid; they also conflate default preferences with context-driven adaptation. We propose DiSCo, a distribution-first forced-choice evaluation framework that isolates default cultural priors and tests steerability via a four-level context gradient (C0--C3). Using DiSCo-Bench (304 items) derived from BLEnD spanning 12 cultures, we evaluate six diverse instruction-tuned LLMs. Default priors are heavily concentrated, with UK and US together absorbing approximately 35\% of all selections despite representing only 2 of 12 cultures. Most critically, prompt-based steering consistently widens the selection gap between high- and low-resource cultures, and injecting explicit cultural facts produces negligible distributional disruption, confirming that cultural preference bias cannot be resolved through prompt-based personalisation alone.

</details>


### [56] [KVShareArena: KV-Cache Reuse Across Contexts and Model Checkpoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10266)
*Xi Shi,Qian Lou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces KVShareArena, a benchmark for evaluating and comparing methods that repair and reuse KV caches across varied prompts and model checkpoints in LLM serving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KV-cache reuse only works when reused text is an exact prefix of the new prompt, which breaks down for retrieval-augmented generation and multi-agent systems where context is recomposed each time. Several communities have proposed incompatible repair methods evaluated on different setups, and current benchmarks ignore more realistic, non-prefix reuse and cross-checkpoint issues.

Method: The authors design KVShareArena, a benchmark that constructs realistic workloads involving retrieved chunks and multi-agent reports, and systematically varies prompt contexts and model checkpoints. They define a unified metric: the fraction of the quality gap recovered between a no-cache baseline and full recomputation, while also accounting for compute, memory, per-request latency, and one-time cache construction cost. They evaluate multiple families of cache-repair and cache-compression methods, including simple position correction, partial re-encoding, and training-based adapters, across three different model boards.

Result: They find that simple position correction (fixing token positions without recomputation) works well when queries depend on a single source, but fails when a question needs multiple sources simultaneously. In such multi-source settings, only methods that incur extra cost (partial re-encoding or trained adapters) recover about half to two-thirds of the performance gap; naive reuse of unrepaired caches can even underperform not using caches at all. Cache-compression techniques that look harmless on single prompts degrade markedly compared with position correction when applied to newly written agent reports. These trends are consistent across three model families, and cross-checkpoint cache reuse shows that training-free methods are robust, whereas adapters trained on one checkpoint’s caches lose effectiveness.

Conclusion: KVShareArena reveals that realistic KV-cache reuse across changing prompts and checkpoints is much harder than exact-prefix scenarios suggest. Simple, training-free position correction is a strong baseline for single-source contexts, but multi-source questions require more expensive repair methods, and some naive or compression-based approaches can be actively harmful. The benchmark, accompanying harness, frozen query sets, and cost-accounting tools provide a standardized way to compare cache-reuse techniques, enabling more grounded progress in efficient LLM serving.

Abstract: LLM serving systems already reuse KV caches, but only when the reused text sits at the very start of the prompt. Two growing workloads break this condition: a retrieval-augmented generation server assembles a different set of retrieved chunks for every query, and a multi-agent coordinator reads reports written by other agents. Reused inside a new prompt, a cache carries the wrong positions and never attended to the other sources. The cache may also have been written by a different checkpoint of the same model family, which changes the stored values. Repair methods for such caches have appeared in three separate communities, each measured on its own terms, and existing benchmarks test only exact-prefix reuse, where nothing is lost. KVShareArena benchmarks KV-cache reuse across prompt contexts and model checkpoints on retrieved chunks and agent reports. It scores every method by the fraction of the gap it recovers between no cache and full recomputation, and charges compute, memory, and per-request latency with the cache in hand, reporting the one-time cost of building a cache separately. We find that correcting positions, which needs no recomputation, is enough until a question needs several sources at once. There, only methods that pay, by re-encoding part of the cache or by training, recover half to two thirds of the gap; unrepaired caches can be worse than no cache. Cache-compression methods that are harmless on a single prompt fall significantly behind position correction on freshly written agent reports. These patterns hold across three model boards. When a different checkpoint wrote the cache, training-free methods are barely affected, while an adapter trained on one checkpoint's caches loses quality. Harness, frozen querysets, and cost accounting ship as a pip package with an automated submission workflow and a public leaderboard.

</details>


### [57] [GANDR: Claim Auditing for Verifiable Legal Answer Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10293)
*Chen Qian,Yimeng Wang,Yu Chen,Lingfei Wu,Andreas Stathopoulos*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces GANDR, a two-agent system for fine-grained verification of legal-domain LLM answers, achieving state-of-the-art strict citation-correctness on a shared legal benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In legal and other high-stakes settings, users must be able to trust that every claim made by an LLM is correctly supported by the cited sources. Existing grounded-generation methods typically score answers holistically, allowing correct final conclusions to be built on fabricated, weak, or loosely matched citations. This mismatch between apparent quality and actual evidence undermines reliability and safety. The authors aim to close this gap by designing both a system and an evaluation protocol that operate at the per-claim level and enforce strict citation grounding.

Method: The authors propose GANDR (Grounded ANswer DRafter), a two-agent pipeline. A Drafter model produces answers in a structured legal-reasoning format, explicitly breaking reasoning into claims with associated citations. A separate Critic model, with access only to what a human verifier would see (retrieved passages and the drafted answer), audits each individual claim against its cited source. For each claim, the Critic generates an audit trace and a verdict, enforcing a protocol-anchored commit rule that requires every citation to resolve to a specific passage returned by the retriever. The system is evaluated on a 185-item legal benchmark where competing systems share the same backbone model, retrieval setup, and citation instructions to isolate the contribution of the protocol and configuration. Additional ablations explore the impact of removing the protocol-anchored commit rule and of varying backbones and drafting strategies.

Result: On the 185-item legal benchmark, GANDR achieves 70.8% strict accuracy, outperforming the strongest baseline by 11.3 percentage points with statistical significance (p<0.01) under a strict criterion that every citation must map to a retriever-returned passage. Removing the protocol-anchored commit rule causes a 22.7-point drop in strict accuracy, underscoring its importance. Across three additional backbone models, GANDR maintains a positive strict-accuracy lead of +3.2 to +6.5 points over baselines. Analysis attributes these gains mainly to the Drafter configuration and the protocol-anchored commit mechanism, rather than superficial rewriting. When compared with two law-trained human annotators, the Critic’s audit flags under-supported claims with F1=0.84 as a binary detector, although agreement on more granular four-way verdict labels is weak, indicating that those labels should be treated as advisory rather than definitive.

Conclusion: Per-claim verification with a dedicated auditing agent and a strict, protocol-anchored commitment rule substantially improves the factual grounding and citation reliability of legal-domain LLM answers. GANDR’s structured drafting plus Critic auditing achieves state-of-the-art strict accuracy under controlled conditions, demonstrating that better protocols—rather than different backbones or simple rewriting—drive the gains. The system’s audit mechanism is effective at detecting under-supported claims, though fine-grained verdict categories remain noisy. Overall, the work shows that building and evaluating grounded generation at the claim level is both feasible and beneficial for high-stakes applications, and offers a concrete blueprint for more trustworthy citation-based reasoning systems.

Abstract: In high-stakes domains such as legal practice, a language-model answer is only useful to the extent that a reader can verify each claim against the source the system cites. Current grounded-generation pipelines score the answer as a whole, so a correct conclusion can rest on fabricated or loosely matched citations and still score well. Closing this gap requires both a system built for per-claim verification and an evaluation that measures it. We introduce GANDR (Grounded ANswer DRafter), a two-agent system in which a Drafter writes an answer in a structured legal-reasoning format and a separate Critic, with the same view as a human verifier, audits each claim against its cited source and emits a per-claim audit trace on every round. We pair it with a strict correctness criterion requiring every citation to resolve to a passage the retriever returned. On a 185-item legal benchmark where all six systems share one backbone, one retrieval surface, and one citation instruction, GANDR ranks first on every primary metric, reaching 70.8% strict accuracy and leading the strongest baseline by 11.3 points (p<0.01). Reverting the protocol-anchored commit rule lowers strict accuracy by 22.7 points, and the strict lead stays positive on three further backbones, at +3.2 to +6.5 points. This lead traces to the Drafter configuration and the protocol-anchored commit, not to rewriting. Against two law-trained annotators the audit flags under-supported claims at F1 0.84 as a binary detector, while its four-way verdict labels agree only weakly and are advisory. Code is available upon request.

</details>


### [58] [The Semantic Bottleneck: Leveraging Semantic Representations for Non-Invasive Speech Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10296)
*Gilad D. Landau,Dulhan Jayalath,Oiwi Parker Jones*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They present Brain2Semantics2Text, a non-invasive brain-to-text method that decodes sentence-level meaning via a semantic embedding bottleneck, achieving better sentence reconstruction than prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-invasive neural recordings like MEG have low SNR, making it hard to reconstruct detailed phonemes or words. Neuroscience suggests that semantic representations are more distributed and temporally coarse, so decoding high-level meaning instead of low-level acoustics may be more feasible and robust for brain-to-text applications.

Method: They use MEG to record sentence-level brain responses, then train a model that maps these MEG signals into a semantic embedding space (a semantic manifold). A separate model then inverts these predicted semantic embeddings back into natural language text. The approach avoids word-level alignment by using a sentence-level semantic bottleneck and includes implementation details and strategies to achieve a reliable neural-to-semantic mapping.

Result: Their Brain2Semantics2Text framework can reconstruct high-level sentence meaning from MEG without relying on word-level alignment and shows improved sentence-level performance compared with previous non-invasive brain-to-text methods.

Conclusion: Targeting distributed, slower-evolving semantic representations and introducing a semantic embedding bottleneck enables more accurate and robust non-invasive decoding of text from brain signals compared with approaches that focus on fine-grained acoustic or lexical reconstruction.

Abstract: Non-invasive speech decoding remains constrained by the low signal-to-noise ratio of neural recordings, which makes fine-grained reconstruction of phonemes or individual words difficult. Motivated by neuroscientific evidence that high-level semantic representations are distributed across cortical regions and evolve over slower temporal scales, we hypothesize that semantic content may provide a more suitable target for non-invasive decoding than low-level acoustic or lexical features. We introduce Brain2Semantics2Text, a method that reconstructs text through an intermediate semantic embedding space. Our model maps sentence-level MEG responses into a semantic manifold and then inverts the predicted embeddings into natural language. This semantic bottleneck enables recovery of high-level meaning without word-level alignment. We describe the core principles of the approach, its implementation, and the strategies used to mitigate the challenges of learning a reliable neural-to-semantic mapping. Finally, we compare against prior non-invasive Brain2Text methods and show improved sentence-level results.

</details>


### [59] [RiLM: Parameter-Efficient Language Modeling via Geodesic Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10305)
*Fang Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes Riemannian Language Models that remove the output layer by interpreting decoding as geometry on a manifold, achieving much better perplexity than comparable small LMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small language models (<1M parameters) are important for edge devices, domain-specific tasks, and reproducible research, but they are heavily bottlenecked by the large output projection matrix, which consumes a large fraction of parameters and capacity. The authors aim to design an architecture that uses parameters more efficiently, especially for output prediction, while staying in the small-model regime.

Method: They introduce Riemannian Language Models (RiLM), where the hidden state is interpreted as a point on a Riemannian manifold, and next-token probabilities are derived from squared geodesic distances between the current state and token embeddings. The same embedding space is used for both input and output, eliminating the separate output matrix. They instantiate two versions: Flat RiLM (manifold is Euclidean R^d) and HypRiLM (manifold is the Poincaré ball model of hyperbolic space). Both use a shared MLP composition map to update states, with roughly 290k parameters, embedding dimension d = 128, and vocabulary size |V| = 2000. They also analyze and fix training issues specific to hyperbolic recurrence via Möbius stabilization.

Result: On WikiText-2, HypRiLM achieves a validation perplexity of 54.2 ± 0.2, outperforming Flat RiLM (87.6 ± 0.6) and several carefully matched/tied baselines (LSTM, Transformer, SSM), which fall between 113 and 147 perplexity. HypRiLM is about 2x better than the best recurrent baseline (SSM at 113.0 ± 3.8). Additional experiments on Penn Treebank and a 10k-vocabulary setting show that the geodesic-decoding approach scales to other corpora and larger vocabularies, with hyperbolic curvature giving selective benefits. They empirically study boundary collapse in naive hyperbolic recurrence and show that Möbius stabilization restores stable training.

Conclusion: Decoding language via Riemannian geometry—using geodesic distance on a manifold instead of a large linear output layer—allows sub-million-parameter language models to use capacity more efficiently and achieve significantly better perplexity than conventional tied small LMs. Hyperbolic geometry can further improve performance but must be carefully stabilized. The approach is validated only in controlled small-model settings and is not claimed to be competitive with large state-of-the-art LMs, but it indicates that geometric decoding is a promising direction for compact language modeling.

Abstract: Language models under one million parameters matter for edge deployment, domain adaptation, and reproducible research, yet a two-layer LSTM or Transformer at embedding width d = 128 still spends roughly one third of its capacity on the output matrix W_out in R^(d x |V|). We propose Riemannian Language Models (RiLM), which remove that layer entirely: context unfolds as a trajectory on a Riemannian manifold, and next-token probabilities arise from squared geodesic distance between the current state and vocabulary embeddings. The same embedding map serves input and output -- decoding is geometry. We instantiate the framework on flat R^d (Flat RiLM) and the Poincare ball H^d (HypRiLM) with a shared MLP composition map phi (~290k parameters, d = 128, |V| = 2000). Across five seeds on WikiText-2, HypRiLM reaches 54.2 +/- 0.2 validation perplexity versus 87.6 +/- 0.6 for Flat RiLM; tied and matched LSTM, Transformer, and SSM controls remain at 113-147 PPL on WT-2 -- HypRiLM leads by roughly 2x over the strongest tied recurrent baseline (SSM, 113.0 +/- 3.8). Penn Treebank and a 10k-vocabulary stress test confirm that geodesic decoding transfers across corpora and larger |V|, while hyperbolic curvature helps selectively. We also characterize boundary collapse in naive hyperbolic recurrence and show how Mobius stabilization restores trainability. Claims are scoped to controlled small-model comparisons, not full-vocabulary state of the art.

</details>


### [60] [On-Policy Distillation for Vision-Language Model Adaptation, an Effective Paradigm on Low-Quality Multimodal Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10321)
*Hongyuan Zhang,Xianda Guo,Yanlun Peng,Qianlong Yang,Yubin Guo,Pinhan Fu,Mulin Chen,Xiaozhen Qiao,Ping Luo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces OnPoKD, an on-policy knowledge distillation framework that dynamically constructs distillation targets for adapting vision-language models, leading to better generalization across classes and datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vision-language knowledge distillation methods rely on fixed teacher predictions as training targets, applied uniformly to all samples. This is unreliable when there are class or domain shifts, because the teacher’s predictions can be noisy or biased in such settings and different samples have different reliability. The authors want a way for the distillation process to adapt its targets per sample and over training, so that the student learns representations that transfer better across distributions rather than just fitting the original training data.

Method: They propose OnPoKD, an on-policy distillation framework that treats target construction as a policy learning problem. A lightweight controller (policy) takes as input reliability and disagreement cues from the teacher model, the student model, and a zero-shot prior. For each training sample, the controller outputs bounded actions that determine how to combine (1) teacher supervision, (2) zero-shot prior guidance, and (3) hard-label anchoring into an adaptive distillation target. The controller is trained on-policy: its decisions are evaluated on validation data, and its parameters are updated based on feedback that reflects transfer performance, not just training fit. The controller is used only during training, and the underlying vision-language architecture and inference cost remain unchanged at test time.

Result: OnPoKD is applied to vision-language model adaptation tasks, including Base-to-novel generalization and Cross-dataset transfer benchmarks. Across these settings, it consistently outperforms strong existing vision-language distillation baselines, indicating that dynamic, policy-based target construction improves the robustness and transferability of the distilled student models.

Conclusion: Treating distillation target construction as a dynamic, learnable policy rather than a fixed recipe leads to more reliable and transferable vision-language model adaptation. OnPoKD demonstrates that an on-policy controller can effectively modulate the contributions of teacher predictions, zero-shot priors, and hard labels on a per-sample basis, improving generalization under class and domain shifts without changing inference-time architecture or cost.

Abstract: Knowledge distillation offers an efficient route to transfer a task-adapted vision-language teacher to a compact student. The training target in current vision-language distillation methods is typically constructed from the teacher prediction and applied uniformly to all training samples, making it unreliable under class and domain shifts. In this paper, we argue that distillation target construction should be treated as a dynamic training decision rather than a fixed recipe. To this end, we propose OnPoKD, an on-policy distillation framework for vision-language model adaptation. To the best of our knowledge, OnPoKD is the first framework that applies on-policy distillation to vision-language model adaptation by learning target construction as a policy decision. OnPoKD learns a lightweight controller that constructs sample-wise adaptive targets using reliability and disagreement cues from the teacher model, student model, and zero-shot prior. Instead of relying on a fixed teacher prediction, the controller dynamically balances teacher supervision, zero-shot prior guidance, and hard-label anchoring through bounded policy actions, allowing the distillation target to adapt to varying sample reliability and training stages. The policy controller is updated with validation feedback, encouraging target construction to optimize transferability rather than merely fitting the training distribution. Since the controller is only used during training, OnPoKD can be seamlessly integrated into existing vision-language distillation pipelines while preserving the original inference architecture and test-time cost. Extensive experiments on Base-to-novel generalization and Cross-dataset transfer benchmarks show that OnPoKD consistently improves over strong vision-language distillation baselines.

</details>


### [61] [Rosetta at AlexandriaX-2026: LoRA-Adapted NileChat for Context-Aware Dialectal Arabic Dialogue Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10395)
*Nada Esmaeil,Fathima Rena,Sibi Subhash,Osama Elgendy,Mina Naguib,Salma Omar,Muhammad Arif*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Rosetta fine-tunes a LoRA adapter on a 3B-parameter NileChat model for context-aware English-to-Dialectal Arabic dialogue translation, testing both constrained and unconstrained data settings in a shared task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To participate in the AlexandriaX shared task on context-aware English-to-Dialectal Arabic dialogue translation and explore how conditioning on dialect and dialogue context, as well as external pretraining on related corpora, affects translation quality across multiple Arabic dialects.

Method: They fine-tune a LoRA adapter on the NileChat-3B model using structured system and user prompts that specify the target dialect and provide dialogue context. For the unconstrained track, they further pretrain the adapter on external dialectal corpora MADAR and PADIC. They then evaluate performance in both constrained and unconstrained shared-task tracks using spBLEU and compare dialect-wise impacts of external pretraining.

Result: Rosetta achieved 4th place in the constrained track with a spBLEU of 26.10 and 5th place in the unconstrained track with a spBLEU of 25.09. External pretraining on MADAR and PADIC benefited only 2 of 13 dialects and slightly reduced the overall spBLEU score, indicating limited gains and potential negative transfer for many dialects.

Conclusion: Context- and dialect-conditioned prompting with a LoRA adapter on NileChat-3B is competitive for English-to-Dialectal Arabic dialogue translation, but naive external pretraining on additional dialect corpora can induce negative transfer, improving only a subset of dialects while degrading average performance.

Abstract: This paper describes the Rosetta system for Subtask 1 (Context-Aware English-to-Dialectal Arabic Dialogue Translation) of the AlexandriaX shared task, participating in both constrained and unconstrained tracks. The approach fine-tunes a LoRA adapter on NileChat-3B using structured system/user prompts that condition generation on dialect and dialogue context. For the unconstrained track, the adapter is additionally pretrained on MADAR and PADIC. Rosetta ranked 4th in the constrained track (spBLEU 26.10) and 5th in the unconstrained track (spBLEU 25.09). The experimental results demonstrate that external pretraining helps only two of thirteen dialects while slightly hurting overall performance, suggesting negative transfer.

</details>


### [62] [Can Foundation Models Moderate Online Content? Evaluating Instruction- vs. Example-Driven Policy Operationalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10410)
*Ayan Majumdar,Shounak Paul,Pushpdeep Singh,Ines Abdelaziz,Sayeh Jarollahi,Seungeon Lee,Krishna P. Gummadi,Ingmar Weber,Abhisek Dash*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates whether vision-language foundation models can reliably perform online content moderation and compares instruction-driven vs example-driven guidance using a new benchmark from Bluesky posts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Content moderation policies are increasingly complex, making consistent and scalable enforcement difficult. Existing deployed systems underperform, and it is unclear if and how powerful foundation models can be best used to operationalize such policies reliably.

Method: The authors build ModerationBench, a benchmark of 4,000 real Bluesky posts manually labeled for moderation outcomes. They then systematically evaluate two paradigms for guiding vision-language models: instruction-driven (using explicit policy descriptions and reasoning) and example-driven (using labeled precedents), and compare their performance to Bluesky’s existing moderation system.

Result: Both instruction-driven and example-driven VLM guidance paradigms achieve similar and substantially better performance than Bluesky’s deployed moderation system, with the foundation model approaches nearly tripling the F1 score on random posts (0.60 vs 0.22).

Conclusion: Vision-language foundation models, when properly guided, can significantly improve the reliability and scalability of online content moderation policy enforcement, and both instruction- and example-driven approaches are viable paths for effective policy operationalization.

Abstract: The growing complexity of content moderation policies presents a critical challenge for their consistent operationalization. While foundation models possess the basic capabilities needed to confront this challenge, whether they can reliably moderate online content remains an unanswered question. In this paper, we systematically compare two competing paradigms for Vision-Language Model (VLM) guidance: an instruction-driven approach where models reason from policy precepts, and an example-driven approach where they generalize from prior precedents. We ground this investigation in ModerationBench, a new benchmark of 4,000 manually annotated, in-the-wild posts from the Bluesky platform. Our experiments reveal that foundation models can substantially outperform Bluesky's deployed moderation system, nearly tripling its $F_1$ score (0.60 vs. 0.22) on Random Posts in the benchmark, with both instruction- and example-driven paradigms achieving comparable peak effectiveness. Our findings thus chart a path toward reliable and adaptable policy operationalization at scale.

</details>


### [63] [Do speech foundation models really learn words?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10434)
*Robin Huo,Ewan Dunbar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies whether self-supervised speech foundation models learn word-level representations that are independent of phonetic form, and shows that later layers of HuBERT and wav2vec 2.0 do encode word identity beyond surface phoneme sequences, improving word discovery tasks via a simple disentanglement approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although self-supervised speech models like HuBERT and wav2vec 2.0 achieve strong performance on downstream tasks, most analyses focus on phoneme and word discrimination without clarifying whether the models truly represent words as abstract units or just encode detailed phonetic form. There is a need to understand whether these models learn form-independent word representations that capture identity and higher-order linguistic information, which is important for their use in speech-aware language models and linguistic analysis.

Method: The authors apply a residualization technique to remove phoneme-level information from the internal representations of self-supervised speech models (HuBERT and wav2vec 2.0). By statistically partialling out phonetic content, they analyze the remaining representation to see whether it still encodes word identity. They focus on later layers of the models and evaluate the fidelity of these form-independent word representations, particularly in the context of word discovery tasks.

Result: After removing phoneme-level information, the later-layer representations in HuBERT and wav2vec 2.0 still encode word identity with reasonable fidelity, indicating the presence of word-level abstractions that are not solely dependent on local phonetic content. Moreover, using these disentangled representations improves performance on word discovery tasks, suggesting that the residualization approach enhances higher-order linguistic information.

Conclusion: Self-supervised speech foundation models do not merely encode detailed phonetic forms; in their later layers, they develop form-independent word representations that capture word identity and are useful for higher-order linguistic tasks. A simple residualization-based disentanglement method can expose and enhance these word-level abstractions, benefiting applications such as unsupervised word discovery and potentially informing the design of speech-aware language models.

Abstract: Self-supervised speech foundation models are now used in a wide array of downstream applications, including traditional speech recognition and as the basis for tokens in speech-aware language models. Attempts to understand their usefulness have largely focused on probing their representations' ability to discriminate phonemes and words. However, discriminative ability for words need not imply specialized representation of words per se.
