Good discrimination of words may be explained by good encoding of word form (phonemes) rather than form-independent word representations encoding identity or syntactic/semantic properties. By partialling out phoneme information using residualization, we show that, in later layers, HuBERT and wav2vec 2.0 do in general learn representations which encode words with reasonable fidelity independently of local phonetic content. We show that this simple approach to disentanglement can enhance higher-order linguistic information in word discovery tasks.

</details>


### [64] [Building Multilingual Bridges: Data Mixing as the Pillar of Generalization for In-Language Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10445)
*Mehrnaz Mofakhami,Ananya Sahu,Alejandro R. Salamanca,Daniel D'souza,Alexandre Berard,Thomas Euyang,Marzieh Fadaee,Julia Kreutzer*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper improves reasoning language models so they can think and respond in the same language as the user’s prompt (L2 reasoning) across many languages, not just English.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reasoning LMs largely process and reason in English internally, even when prompted in other languages, which harms accessibility, can distort user intent, and ignores knowledge best expressed in other languages. The authors want models that genuinely reason in the user’s language to make AI more inclusive and effective globally.

Method: They take a data-centric approach to supervised fine-tuning (SFT), systematically exploring how to mix and schedule multilingual reasoning and non-reasoning data to promote reasoning in many languages. They build a 3.35B-parameter model (Tiny Aya L2-Thinker) with a strong English reasoning backbone, broadened language coverage, and abundant multilingual non-reasoning data, without requiring explicit reasoning supervision for every language.

Result: The Tiny Aya L2-Thinker model attains over 93% L2 reasoning rate across 60 languages on six benchmarks covering math, commonsense reasoning, instruction following, open-ended generation, and cultural reasoning, while maintaining strong overall performance. They empirically show that good L2 reasoning in unseen languages emerges from the right data mixture rather than per-language reasoning labels.

Conclusion: Reasoning can be treated as a largely language-agnostic ability that transfers across typologically diverse languages if the model has a solid English reasoning core and is fine-tuned with broad, well-mixed multilingual data. High-quality L2 reasoning does not require explicit reasoning supervision in every target language. They release model weights and data to enable further work on inclusive, in-language reasoning systems.

Abstract: Reasoning language models have made substantial advances on a variety of complex tasks, yet their capabilities remain overwhelmingly English-centric: models primarily reason in English regardless of the language they are prompted in. This is inaccessible for non-English-speaking users, risks losing the intent of the original question, and forgoes knowledge more readily expressed in the target language. In this work, we advance L2 reasoning, the ability of a model to reason consistently in the language of the user's prompt, thus building an in-language bridge between the prompt and the answer. We approach this problem from a data-centric angle, investigating how to optimize data composition and scheduling in SFT for reasoning generalization. Building Tiny Aya L2-Thinker at 3.35B scale, we achieve an L2 reasoning rate above 93% across 60 languages on 6 benchmarks spanning math, commonsense reasoning, instruction following, open-ended generation, and cultural reasoning while keeping performance strong. We show the path to generalizing L2 reasoning to held-out languages goes through broader language coverage, readily available multilingual non-reasoning data, and a sufficient English reasoning backbone. These findings indicate that reasoning is a language-agnostic behavior that can be transferred across typologically diverse languages through careful data mixing and without requiring reasoning supervision in every target language. We release our model weights and multilingual reasoning data to support further research on accessible, in-language reasoning.

</details>


### [65] [IBIB: A Protocol for Measuring Enterprise AI Systems by Serving Route, Not Model Identifier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10494)
*Blake Stenstrom,Charangan Vasantharajan,Brian Sathianathan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes IB2, a protocol for realistically and reliably evaluating deployed AI systems rather than just model IDs, integrating capability binding, reliability-aware scoring, and blind adjudication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks evaluate advertised model identifiers and ignore real serving configurations (routes, tools, harnesses), causing measurement error and misleading claims about capability and reliability of deployed enterprise systems.

Method: The authors design the IB2 protocol with three components: (1) a gold-blind capability-binding preflight that checks whether a serving route can satisfy an evaluation contract before tasks are sent; (2) a scoring rule that includes reliability by penalizing failures while excluding unsupported capabilities; and (3) a structurally score-blind adjudication process. They instantiate IB2 with a sealed benchmark of 128 locked tasks and 987 assertions across multiple work modalities.

Result: Applying IB2 to eleven systems shows that (a) capability availability can vary across runs even with identical model weights; (b) benchmark discrimination is uneven, with much of the spread driven by governed database and multi-tab join tasks, so ranks are unstable; (c) serving-arm implementation details (access mode, harness generation, tool parsers) materially affect measured precision; and (d) including or excluding failed responses from denominators can change system ranking conclusions.

Conclusion: Reliable enterprise evaluation must treat the deployed system (routes, tools, harness) as the unit of analysis, not just model identifiers; evaluation protocols should bind capability, include reliability in scores, use interval-based groupings rather than fine ranks where discrimination is limited, and keep test data sealed so the procedure, not the corpus, is the main artifact.

Abstract: Enterprises deploy systems, not checkpoints. Usable capability depends jointly on weights, serving route, precision, output contract, and harness, yet all 18 audited benchmarks score advertised model identifiers. We treat this as measurement error and give a protocol that makes it reportable. It has three parts. A gold-blind capability-binding preflight verifies that a route can execute the evaluation contract before any task reaches it; a reliability-inclusive first-pass scoring rule keeps failure in the score while keeping unsupported capability out; and adjudication is structurally score-blind. We call the protocol IB2 and release its algorithms, classification tables, request contract, and manifest schemas. Its reference instantiation, 128 locked tasks and 987 assertions over document, spreadsheet, chart, tool and database work, stays sealed: the procedure is the artifact, not the corpus. Across eleven systems, four results. Capability availability is measurable: two complete single-route runs on identical weights later failed distinct predicates of the finalized binding gate, while a third passed that gate before a fresh run. The advertised identifier exposed neither limit. Discrimination is not uniform: four of seven suites saturate under a six-system band, with the spread almost entirely from governed database work and multi-tab joins, so we report interval-backed resolution groups, not ranks; two of the nominal five-label output's four cuts fail multiplicity adjustment. Serving-arm choice moved one declared revision and precision from 77.38 to 82.54, paired interval [0.11,10.60], though the arms differ in access mode, harness generation, and the serving tool-call parser, and harness generation is a property of our evaluator, not any endpoint. Excluding failed responses from denominators changes the point ordering, so reliability inclusion changes a conclusion, not its wording.

</details>


### [66] [IdeaAMBIG: Benchmarking Implementation-Critical Gaps in Research-Idea Specifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10539)
*Yiling Ma,Yilun Zhao,Sihong Wu,Manasi Patwardhan,Arman Cohan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces IdeaAMBIG, a benchmark to evaluate how well LLMs can detect and clarify missing details in research method descriptions, showing that localizing defects is much harder than suggesting clarifications once defects are known.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many research papers present novel and coherent ideas, but their method descriptions lack enough detail for faithful implementation. This undermines reproducibility and makes it difficult for humans and coding agents to reimplement the intended methods without guesswork. There is a need to systematically measure how well models can recognize when a method description is codification-ready and identify and repair missing or ambiguous details.

Method: The authors define "codification readiness" as whether a research-method specification contains enough information for a competent implementer to recreate the intended method without unsupported assumptions. They build evidence-grounded method specifications and associated resolutions from papers, public codebases, issue threads, and reproduction artifacts. Using these, they create IdeaAMBIG, a benchmark of 660 instances comprising 163 real-world gaps mined from reproducibility reports and GitHub issues, and 497 synthetic gaps injected into otherwise codification-ready references. The benchmark is designed to test three capabilities in LLMs: (1) assessing codification readiness (is the spec complete enough?), (2) localizing defects (where exactly is the missing or ambiguous information?), and (3) generating clarification actions (what clarification should be requested or added). Defect localization tasks provide only the original specification, while clarification tasks additionally annotate the defect location. Thirteen LLMs are evaluated on these tasks, and an oracle study is conducted where gold resolutions are supplied to see the impact on downstream codification readiness.

Result: On the IdeaAMBIG benchmark, the best-performing LLM reaches only 9.6% Macro Defect Recovery Rate on real-world instances, indicating that accurately finding and characterizing real missing/ambiguous method details is very challenging. However, when the defect is given (in the clarification setting), the same model achieves an 80.6% Macro Clarification Action Success Rate, showing that models are relatively strong at suggesting clarifications once they know where the gap is. In an oracle experiment where models are provided with the gold resolution to the defects, the rate of codification-ready specifications increases sharply from 14% to 98%. Across all evaluated models, the main performance bottleneck is defect localization rather than the clarification step.

Conclusion: IdeaAMBIG highlights a significant gap between LLMs' ability to produce clarifications and their ability to detect where clarifications are needed in method descriptions. While models can effectively generate corrective or clarifying actions when guided to known defects, they struggle to autonomously identify those defects, especially in real-world ambiguous research descriptions. The benchmark provides a structured way to study and improve codification readiness assessment, defect localization, and clarification, pointing to defect localization as the primary bottleneck for building systems that can faithfully implement research ideas.

Abstract: A research idea may be novel, coherent, and scientifically plausible, yet its proposed method may remain insufficiently specified for faithful implementation. We study the codification readiness of implementation-facing research-method specifications, defined by whether they provide sufficient methodological information for a competent implementer or coding agent to construct the intended method without unsupported assumptions. We construct evidence-grounded specifications and their supported resolutions from papers, codebases, issue threads, and reproduction artifacts. We introduce IdeaAMBIG, a benchmark of 660 evidence-grounded instances: 163 real-world gaps from reproducibility reports and GitHub issues, and 497 controlled synthetic gaps injected into codification-ready references. IdeaAMBIG evaluates three capabilities: codification-readiness assessment, defect localization, and clarification action generation. Defect localization receives only the specification, whereas clarification additionally receives the annotated defect. Across 13 LLMs, the best model achieves 9.6% Macro Defect Recovery Rate on real-world instances but 80.6% Macro Clarification Action Success Rate when given the defect. In an oracle study, supplying the gold resolution raises the downstream codification-ready rate from 14% to 98%. Across all evaluated models, defect localization is the main bottleneck, with stronger clarification given the defect.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [67] [OpenDiscoveryTrace: Process Traces for Evaluating AI Scientist Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09203)
*Aayam Bansal,Keertan Balaji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce OpenDiscoveryTrace, a dataset of full reasoning trajectories from AI scientific agents, enabling process-level evaluation instead of only judging final outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks only evaluate final outputs of AI scientists, making it impossible to audit methodology, understand failure modes, or separate systematic reasoning from lucky guesses.

Method: They collect 558 complete trajectories from AI agents solving 124 scientific tasks across several domains, logging a rich 9-field trace at each step, covering seven different models and some live-retrieval variants. They also perform pilot analysis with LLM-based judges and define five benchmark tasks with standard ML baselines.

Result: Process traces reveal major behavioral differences between models that look similar under output-only metrics; for instance, frontier models have similar success rates but very different error rates and error types. They also provide baseline performance on new benchmark tasks.

Conclusion: OpenDiscoveryTrace enables rigorous, process-level study and auditing of AI scientific agents, uncovering nuanced behavioral differences and supporting research in evaluation and governance via an open, standardized dataset and tooling.

Abstract: Existing benchmarks for autonomous AI scientists evaluate only final outputs---generated code, hypotheses, or papers---yet discard the reasoning process by which those outputs were obtained. This makes it impossible to audit scientific methodology, diagnose failure modes, or distinguish systematic reasoning from fortunate guessing. We present \textbf{OpenDiscoveryTrace}, a public dataset of 558 complete AI scientific agent trajectories that captures how models reason, not just what they produce. Each trajectory records a structured 9-field-per-step trace---including thoughts, tool calls, observations, errors, revision triggers, and self-reported confidence---as models execute 124 scientific tasks spanning drug discovery, materials science, genomics, and scientific literature analysis. The dataset covers seven models: three frontier models (GPT-5.4, Claude Opus 4.6, and Gemini 3.1 Pro; 124 trajectories each, fully balanced across domains and difficulty levels) and four open-weight models (Qwen2.5-7B, Mistral-7B-v0.3, Phi-3.5-mini, and Qwen2.5-1.5B; 30 each), plus 60 live-retrieval variant trajectories. Pilot analysis on 363 LLM-judged trajectories reveals that process traces expose behavioral differences invisible to output-only evaluation: all three frontier models achieve comparable success rates (84--89%), yet Claude Opus 4.6 produces 30$\times$ more errors than GPT-5.4 (2.5 vs. 0.08 per trajectory, $p < 0.0001$, Cliff's $δ= 0.613$), with qualitatively different error profiles---66.7% tool misuse for Claude versus 83.6% reasoning errors for GPT-5.4. We define five benchmark tasks with baselines from logistic regression, random forests, LSTMs, and Transformer models. The dataset, trace schema, agent harness, and benchmark definitions are publicly available under CC BY 4.0 to support research on process-level evaluation, scientific agent auditing, and AI governance.

</details>


### [68] [Adaptive Entangled Game Modules in Artificial General Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09226)
*Haochen Li,Xinshuai Guo,Jingdong Ouyang,Wei Zhang,Leilei Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a probability-wave framework to model collective behavior of adaptive agents, providing evidence for a brain entanglement hypothesis via stock market data and suggesting this as a basis for more brain-like AGI architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To better explain and predict collective human decision-making than neoclassical finance allows, to find indirect empirical support for the Liu-Chen-Ao hypothesis of nonlocal entangled nerve fibers in the brain, and to design more compact, brain-inspired AGI systems that overcome limitations of conventional ANN-based AI.

Method: The authors formulate a generalized behavioral intelligence (GBI) nonlocal probability-wave equation that yields eigenmodes representing collective behavioral patterns. They then fit this framework to Chinese intraday stock market trading data, decomposing observed decision patterns into adaptive entangled game modes, news/event-adaptive modes with dual equilibria, and independent rational-agent modes, and compare explanatory power with neoclassical models.

Result: Adaptive entangled game modes account for 82–94% (89% overall) of observed decision patterns in the Chinese intraday stock market data. News- and environment-adaptive behaviors with dual equilibria and abrupt reference point shifts represent 2–12%, while purely independent rational-agent-like modes appear in less than 5% of cases. This pattern contrasts strongly with neoclassical finance predictions and aligns with the proposed nonlocal entangled-brain mechanism.

Conclusion: Collective trading behavior is dominated by adaptive entangled game modes, providing empirical support for the LCA hypothesis of nonlocal entangled brain fibers and suggesting that observable market behavior reflects underlying entangled intelligence processes in the brain. The authors argue that AGI architectures should incorporate probability-wave-based entangled-brain modules alongside ANN-based components to build more compact, efficient, robust, and human-like processing units, especially for embodied intelligence and robotics.

Abstract: We introduce a probability-wave framework for modeling the collective behavior of interacting adaptive agents, deriving testable eigenmodes through a generalized behavioral intelligence (GBI) nonlocal probability-wave equation. This framework captures a broad range of human intelligence behaviors with analytical mechanisms and offers an indirect method to examine the Liu-Chen-Ao (LCA) hypothesis of nonlocal entangled nerve fibers in the brain through collective trader behaviors. Our empirical analysis of Chinese intraday stock market data demonstrates that adaptive entangled game modes explain 82-94% (89% overall) of observed decision patterns, a sharp contrast to the predictions of neoclassical finance based on independent rational agents. Moreover, 2-12% of behaviors show adaption to intraday news, events, and environments, characterized by dual equilibrium states and abrupt reference point shifts, while purely independent modes occur in less than 5% of cases. These findings empirically support the LCA hypothesis, as observable trading behaviors reflect underlying brain mechanisms and internal intelligence decision-making in behavioral psychology. Our results highlight the necessity of incorporating adaptive entangled game modules into artificial general intelligence (AGI) architectures, addressing the limitations of conventional artificial neural network (ANN)-based AI, which relies on trillions of opaque parameters. By integrating ANN-based AI with probability-wave-based entangled-brain simulations, machine learning can enrich AGI foundation models (FMs) and facilitate the development of human-like processing units (HPUs) that leverage brain-inspired mechanisms. Such HPUs may ultimately create more compact, efficient, and robust AGI systems, particularly for embodied intelligence and robotics.

</details>


### [69] [Subagents vs Agent Skills: Executing Reusable Knowledge for Long-Horizon Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09233)
*Wasu Top Piriyakulkij,Rachel Lawrence,Alicia Curth,Sushrut Karmalkar,Niranjani Prasad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to better use reusable skill libraries in language-model agents by running skills as separate subagents with fresh contexts instead of just loading their instructions into a single large context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As language-model agents tackle longer, more complex tasks, simply stuffing many skill instructions into one shared context leads to degraded reasoning and brittle behavior. There is a need to understand alternative ways to structure and invoke reusable knowledge so agents can reliably solve long-horizon tasks.

Method: The authors compare two architectures for using reusable skills: (1) standard agent-skill execution, where skill instructions are injected into the main agent’s context, and (2) subagent execution, where each skill package is invoked as an independent subagent with its own fresh context window. They analyze performance under conditions where skills expose explicit input-output contracts and encode procedural instructions, and they study the cost in terms of additional communication tokens between the main agent and subagents.

Result: They find that invoking skill packages as subagents with separate context windows leads to better performance than traditional agent-skill execution, provided the skills have well-defined input-output contracts and sufficient procedural detail. However, this comes at the cost of extra token overhead to coordinate between the main agent and its subagents.

Conclusion: The effectiveness of reusable knowledge in language-model agents depends not only on what the skills contain but also on how they are structured and executed. Using skill packages as subagents can improve long-horizon reasoning when skills are well specified, though designers must account for increased communication overhead between agents.

Abstract: How can language model agents effectively leverage libraries of reusable knowledge to solve long-horizon tasks? Recent work has increasingly focused on agent skills: reusable capabilities represented as skill packages, i.e., multi-file bundles containing instructions, scripts, and other resources that help agents perform specific tasks. Agent skills are typically executed by loading their skill instructions into an agent's context and relying on the agent to follow them. As task horizons grow, however, this approach becomes increasingly brittle, because reasoning quality degrades as more information accumulates in the context window. We investigate an alternative approach in which skill packages are instead invoked as subagents. Rather than loading skill instructions into the main context, subagent execution spawns fresh context windows dedicated to solving individual subtasks. We show that subagent execution outperforms agent-skill execution when skill packages expose clear input-output contracts and their instructions encode the procedural knowledge needed to fulfill those contracts. The tradeoff is additional communication overhead, as extra tokens are required to coordinate between the main agent and its subagents. Our results show that the benefit of reusable knowledge depends not only on its content, but also on how it is organized and invoked.

</details>


### [70] [Gradland: On Phenomenal Experience, Differentiated Across Many Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09306)
*David Balduzzi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes that the local differential structure of physical interactions (gradients/Jacobians) underlies phenomenal experience, and tests this in a toy world of neural networks using two new Jacobian-structure measures, showing they map onto multiple qualitative features of experience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To offer a principled, mathematically precise bridge between physical dynamics and phenomenal consciousness by focusing on first-order interaction structure, and to explain diverse qualitative aspects of experience (duration, vividness, texture, learning, etc.) within a unified framework.

Method: Construct an idealized world, Gradland, populated by neural networks whose dynamics are differentiable and fully known; define two quantitative measures of Jacobian structure—effective rank and cohesion (derived from Kirchhoff complexity); apply these metrics to a range of toy scenarios and analyze how variations in gradient/Jacobian structure correspond to differences in the putative phenomenal character of states.

Result: The two structural measures systematically vary in ways that correspond to intuitive differences in experiential properties across examples: they explain why some states would sustain experience over time, why some contents are vivid vs. obscure, how texture-like experiences might arise, what early undifferentiated experiences could be like, how distinct vs. confused ideas differ structurally, what subjective learning might correspond to, and they assign a functional role to rich, dense experiences.

Conclusion: In the Gradland setting, first-order interaction structure (gradients/Jacobians), as captured by effective rank and cohesion, provides a coherent explanatory basis for multiple features of phenomenal experience, supporting the hypothesis that such differential structure is central to consciousness and suggesting that richer, denser Jacobian structures have functional significance for cognitive systems.

Abstract: This paper investigates the hypothesis that the first-order structure of physical interactions, i.e. gradients or Jacobians, characterizes the structure of phenomenal experience. It does so in an idealized world inhabited by neural networks, Gradland, where the physics are known and the functions are (mostly) differentiable. The paper introduces two measures of Jacobian structure: effective rank and cohesion, based on Kirchhoff complexity. Applying the measures to a series of worked examples shows the hypothesis accounts for: (1) the duration of experience, that it can prolong over hundreds of milliseconds; (2) the difference between what is experienced vividly and obscurely; (3) the experience of texture; (4) the blooming buzzing confusion presumably experienced by newborns; (5) the difference between ideas that are held distinctly in mind and ideas that are confused; (6) what learning is like; and finally (7) the paper explains the function of rich, dense experience.

</details>


### [71] [An Autonomous GeoAI Agent for Arctic Eco-Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09374)
*Samira Alkaee Taleghan,Younghyun Koo,Farnoush Banaei-Kashani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a GeoAI-based, human-in-the-loop, multi-agent routing framework for Arctic maritime navigation that jointly optimizes operational, environmental, and community-related criteria to support safer and more socially responsible route planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With climate-driven changes in Arctic sea ice, seasonal accessibility for maritime navigation is increasing, bringing both economic opportunities and heightened operational, environmental, and community risks. Existing routing approaches mostly optimize for time, fuel, and navigational risk and give limited attention to ecological sensitivity and impacts on local communities. There is a need for a transparent, multi-criteria system that can incorporate ecological and community factors while leaving final value-laden trade-offs to human decision-makers.

Method: The authors develop a human-in-the-loop, multi-agent GeoAI system for Arctic eco-navigation. Specialized agents are designed for different tasks: (1) gathering and preparing heterogeneous geospatial datasets (sea ice, operational factors, ecological and community data such as Essential Fish Habitat and seal critical habitat), (2) generating multi-objective routes that trade off safety, efficiency, ecological exposure, and community impacts, and (3) performing skyline-based decision support to present non-dominated route options for human evaluation. The framework integrates these agents into a unified routing system where humans guide and approve consequential trade-offs.

Result: The system can generate route alternatives that explicitly balance operational efficiency, navigational safety, and exposure to ecologically sensitive areas and communities. By incorporating ecological criteria (e.g., Essential Fish Habitat and seal critical habitat) and community-related factors, it identifies routes that may be slightly less efficient but substantially reduce ecological and social impacts. The open-source project page and code demonstrate the feasibility and practicality of the approach for real Arctic navigation scenarios.

Conclusion: Incorporating a multi-agent GeoAI, human-in-the-loop framework into Arctic maritime route planning enables more holistic, transparent, and socially responsible navigation decisions. By making ecological and community impacts first-class routing criteria, while leaving normative trade-offs to human decision-makers, the system advances eco-navigation practices and offers an extensible, open-source tool for stakeholders concerned with safe and sustainable Arctic shipping.

Abstract: Arctic maritime navigation is becoming increasingly important as changing sea-ice conditions expand seasonal accessibility while simultaneously introducing substantial operational, environmental, and community risks. Arctic route planning is inherently a multi-criteria problem: routes that improve vessel safety or efficiency may increase exposure to sea ice, sensitive ecosystems, or nearby communities. Existing routing methods prioritize travel time, fuel use, and navigational risk, often overlooking ecological and community impacts. We introduce a human-in-the-loop, multi-agent GeoAI system for Arctic eco-navigation that integrates operational, physical, ecological, and community-related criteria within a unified routing framework. Multiple specialized agents coordinate geospatial data acquisition and preparation, multi-objective route generation, and skyline-based decision support. The ecological criteria explicitly account for exposure to sensitive areas, including Essential Fish Habitat and seal critical habitat. By considering these ecosystem impacts and potential community burdens while keeping consequential value judgments under human control, the framework supports safer, more transparent, and socially responsible Arctic navigation. Project page and code are publicly available. https://samiraat.github.io/Arctic-Eco-Navigation-Agent/, https://github.com/samiraat/Arctic-Eco-Navigation-Agent

</details>


### [72] [The Menu Is an Execution Prior: State-Path Tool Menus for Online Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09395)
*Bo Yan,Weikai Lin,Song Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose State-Path Tool Menu, a learned method to select and order a small subset (menu) of tools for language-model agents, explicitly modeling multi-step execution paths from the current state to the goal, which greatly improves tool-use success rates without changing the agent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tool-using language-model agents must choose from thousands of tools, but the short list (menu) they see is usually ranked only by relevance to the user request. This tends to surface just the final target tool and miss or misorder prerequisite tools that generate its inputs, which hurts performance on multi-step tasks. There is a need to construct tool menus that reflect executable sequences from the current state to the desired outcome, rather than just semantic similarity.

Method: They formalize the notion of a state path: a pre-execution route from the observable request state through intermediate tool outputs to the desired outcome. They then design State-Path Tool Menu, which learns to treat the tool menu as an execution prior over such routes. The framework uses an encoder to model: (1) which tools are executable from the current state, (2) how outputs of some tools can satisfy inputs of others, and (3) which tool orderings are frequent in training execution paths. A retriever selects an "entry" tool that can execute immediately, the missing-input producer tools, and the final action tool; then a reranker orders tools so that producers appear before consumers. This produces a short, ordered tool menu exposed to an otherwise-unchanged agent.

Result: On the ToolBench benchmark, using their State-Path Tool Menu raises online task success from 0.737 to 0.898. It outperforms multiple strong baselines including standard retrieval, reranking, tool generation, and routing approaches, all without modifying the underlying agent. With only 32 tools, their menu covers more complete tool chains than an official hand-designed list with 128 tools. The performance improvements hold across different executor families and model sizes, indicating robustness and generality.

Conclusion: Modeling the tool menu as an execution prior over learned state paths enables better selection and ordering of tools for language-model agents. By explicitly encoding executability, input-output compatibility, and common tool sequences, the State-Path Tool Menu significantly improves multi-step tool-use success without needing changes to the agent itself, and does so efficiently with fewer tools than existing curated lists.

Abstract: Language models act through tools, yet practical agents face libraries containing thousands of interfaces. We introduce the tool menu as the short, ordered subset of available tools shown to an agent before execution. The agent can call only tools in this menu. Multi-step tasks require the final action and the prerequisite tools that create its inputs in a usable order. Current constructors rank tools by request relevance, which can surface the final action while omitting or delaying less obvious producers. We introduce the state path, a pre-execution route from the observable request state to the desired outcome, and propose State-Path Tool Menu to learn it. Our framework treats the menu as an execution prior over these routes. Its encoder represents which tools can run from the current state, how their outputs satisfy later inputs, and which orders recur in training paths. A retriever covers an executable entry, the missing-input producers, and the final action. A reranker then places producers before consumers. On ToolBench, our menu raises online success from 0.737 to 0.898 and outperforms retrieval, reranking, generation, and routing baselines without changing the agent. The State-Path menu also covers more complete chains with 32 tools than the official list covers with 128, and its success gain persists across executor families with different model capacities. Our code is at https://github.com/Met2348/State-Path.

</details>


### [73] [Decision-Focused Active Learning for Scale-Aware Critical-Materials Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09413)
*Niranjan Srinivas,Debajyoti Ray,Elias Nakouzi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Active learning and Bayesian decision-theoretic policies are evaluated and proposed to design experiments for selective precipitation in critical element recovery, aiming to reduce experiments and downstream risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling up recovery processes for critical materials requires efficiently connecting lab experiments to industrial requirements, costs, and scale effects. Traditional experimental design (e.g., space-filling) may be inefficient, and there is a need for methods that exploit prior results and explicitly consider downstream process decisions and economic risks.

Method: The authors analyze historical records from the CICERO autonomous selective precipitation workflow for recycled NdFeB and SmCo magnets and produced water. They conduct conditional retrospective benchmarks comparing active learning policies to nonadaptive space-filling designs, focusing on enrichment, purity, and yield. They introduce a Bayesian decision-theoretic approach that selects experimental batches based on expected reduction in downstream Bayes risk, including exploratory simulations of a hybrid policy that filters candidates before detailed evaluation. They also reconstruct a two-stage adaptive policy and propose a pre-registered prospective test with standardized loss, logging, and economic inputs.

Result: In retrospective analyses, active learning policies reach the best recorded enrichment in 16–24 wells versus 48 for nonadaptive designs, with two adaptive alternatives tied at 16 wells. Conditional analyses reveal tradeoffs between purity and nominal yield for SmCo magnets and show differing enrichment routes for NdFeB Round 1; rankings for produced water depend on unverified assumptions. Exploratory simulations suggest that a hybrid Bayes-risk-based policy that filters candidate experiments yields lower estimated downstream loss than the currently implemented joint search policy, though differences versus a synthetic two-stage policy are small relative to uncertainty.

Conclusion: Adaptive, active learning-based experimental design can substantially reduce the number of experiments needed to reach high-performing recovery conditions compared with nonadaptive space-filling. Framing experiment selection around expected reduction in downstream Bayes risk provides a principled way to connect lab experiments with process decisions and economic objectives. However, the advantages of different adaptive strategies may be modest given estimation uncertainty, and robust evaluation will require a pre-registered, standardized prospective test with clarified measurements, economic assumptions, and validation at target scale.

Abstract: Choosing a recovery process for scale-up requires connecting laboratory results with product requirements, process costs, and scale effects. We analyze records from Pacific Northwest National Laboratory's Computer Intelligence for Critical Element Recovery and Optimization (CICERO) workflow for autonomous selective precipitation. Active learning uses prior results to choose experiments. In a conditional retrospective benchmark with fitted models and recycled neodymium-iron-boron (NdFeB) magnet records, active learning finds the best recorded result with fewer experiments than nonadaptive space filling. Enrichment is the selected rare-earth-to-iron ratio relative to that in the feed. Adaptive policies reach the recorded enrichment maximum by 16 to 24 wells (individual experiments), versus 48. Our two-stage reconstruction ties two adaptive alternatives at 16 wells. Conditional analyses of recycled samarium-cobalt (SmCo) magnets show a Round 2 tradeoff between purity and nominal yield, the recovery fraction calculated from an assumed starting amount - NdFeB Round 1 routes differ in enrichment. Rankings for produced water from oil and gas extraction depend on phase and dilution assumptions requiring confirmation.
  We propose choosing batches by their expected reduction in downstream Bayes risk: the minimum expected loss among available process decisions under current beliefs. In exploratory simulations, a hybrid that filters candidates has lower estimated loss than the implemented joint search across routes and conditions. Differences involving the synthetic two-stage policy are small relative to estimation uncertainty. We outline a pre-registered prospective test under a shared loss and logging standard, requiring clarified measurements and records, a defined process decision and relevant outputs, credible economic inputs, and validation at the intended scale.

</details>


### [74] [Valerant: An Automatic Navigable Game Map Generator via Action-Conditioned World Model Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09418)
*Yiran Qiao,Feng Wang,Jing Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Valerant, a training-free framework that turns pretrained action-conditioned world models into World Action Models (WAMs) for 3D game-map exploration and construction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World Action Models have driven progress in embodied AI, but their general-purpose use in games—especially 3D games—remains underexplored. Existing game approaches largely operate in 2D observation space and rely on external policies and rewards, without building persistent 3D geometry. Unlike robotics, games lack an external, persistent 3D world, so the virtual environment itself must be instantiated. There is a need for a method that can autonomously construct persistent, navigable 3D game spaces with minimal manual effort.

Method: The authors propose Valerant, a training-free framework that converts a pretrained action-conditioned video/world model into a full WAM suitable for 3D games. Valerant couples three components: (1) predictive visual rollouts from the world model, (2) SLAM-based spatial reconstruction to build a persistent 3D map from these rollouts, and (3) exploration-driven action selection that uses the evolving map to choose new actions. Starting from a single image, Valerant iteratively predicts future frames, reconstructs geometry, and chooses actions to explore and expand the game map.

Result: Valerant is able to progressively transform a single input image into a persistent, navigable 3D game map by leveraging world-model rollouts and SLAM. It demonstrates that an off-the-shelf action-conditioned world model can be repurposed—without additional training—into a system capable of 3D map exploration and construction for games.

Conclusion: The framework shows that WAM-based interaction can be extended beyond 2D visual simulations into 3D game environments, where both perception and environment instantiation are required. Valerant offers a promising, training-free direction to reduce manual effort in 3D game map design by autonomously exploring and constructing persistent 3D geometry from minimal input.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) couple predictive world modeling with action generation, allowing anticipated future states to guide agent behavior. Although WAMs are rapidly advancing embodied AI, general-purpose counterparts remain largely unexplored in games. Existing game-oriented approaches often combine action-conditioned world models with external policies and reward functions to realize WAM-like decision-making, yet they operate mainly in 2D visual observation space and do not instantiate persistent 3D geometry. Extending this paradigm to 3D games introduces a distinct challenge. In autonomous driving and robotics, the physical environment exists independently of the model, providing a persistent 3D world in which selected actions can be executed. Games have no such external substrate; the virtual world itself must be instantiated. Most playable games require a persistent and navigable space, while 3D games additionally require explicit geometry that supports movement and interaction. Action-conditioned video rollouts provide visual observations but not this spatial representation. We present \textsc{Valerant}, a training-free framework that transforms a pretrained action-conditioned world model into a WAM for exploring and constructing 3D game maps. By coupling predictive visual rollouts with SLAM-based spatial reconstruction and exploration-driven action selection, \textsc{Valerant} progressively transforms a single image into a persistent 3D game map. This framework extends WAM-based interaction beyond 2D visual simulation and offers a new approach to reducing manual effort in 3D game-map creation.

</details>


### [75] [XAI-Arena: Can LLMs Assess the Quality of XAI Explanations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09428)
*Yanfei Hu Fleischhauer,Alona Zharova,Nadja Klein,Stefan Feuerriegel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes XAI-Arena, a framework that uses large language models (LLMs) as judges to systematically evaluate the quality of explainable AI (XAI) explanations in a scalable and reproducible way.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of XAI explanations heavily rely on subjective human judgments, which are costly, hard to scale, and difficult to reproduce and compare across studies. There is a need for an automated, standardized, and multidimensional way to assess explanation quality that can still align with human preferences and needs.

Method: The authors design XAI-Arena, an LLM-as-a-judge evaluation framework. They define multiple dimensions of explanation quality (e.g., simplicity, clarity, task adequacy, trust calibration, actionability, transparency, faithfulness, and overall interpretability) and prompt LLMs to compare and rate XAI explanations along these dimensions. They apply this framework to benchmark different XAI methods across multiple datasets, ML models, and stakeholder personas, and then validate the LLM-based ratings against human ratings.

Result: The framework can distinguish systematic differences in the quality of XAI explanations across methods, datasets, models, and personas. LLM-generated ratings show a strong positive correlation with human ratings (Spearman's rho = .693, p < .001), suggesting that LLMs can approximate human judgments of explanation quality reasonably well.

Conclusion: LLMs can be effectively used as scalable and reproducible judges for evaluating XAI explanations. XAI-Arena enables multidimensional, stakeholder-sensitive benchmarking of XAI methods and offers a practical alternative or complement to traditional human-subject evaluations for comparative assessment of explanation quality.

Abstract: Evaluating the quality of explanations produced by explainable AI (XAI) methods remains challenging because existing approaches often rely on subjective human judgment, limiting reproducibility, scalability, and comparability between studies. We examine whether LLMs can serve as a reproducible and scalable mechanism to make comparative assessments of the quality of XAI explanations. We introduce XAI-Arena, an LLM-as-a-judge framework for scalable, reproducible, multidimensional, and stakeholder-sensitive evaluation of XAI explanation quality. XAI-Arena then allows us to compare XAI explanations along various dimensions, namely, perceived simplicity, clarity, task adequacy, trust calibration, actionability, transparency, faithfulness, and overall interpretability. We then benchmark XAI explanation methods across various datasets, machine learning models, and stakeholder personas. Human validation shows a strong positive association between LLM-generated and human ratings (Spearman's rho=.693, p<.001). Together, LLM-based evaluations can capture systematic differences in XAI explanation quality and provide a scalable and reproducible framework for comparative assessment of XAI explanations.

</details>


### [76] [Do Agents Know When They Succeed? Calibrating Agent Confidence from Internal Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09448)
*Priyanka Mary Mammen,Emil Joswin,Srujananjali Medicherla*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose methods to estimate confidence/reliability of multi-turn agentic AI systems using internal model representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic systems are increasingly used in safety-critical settings, but their complex, multi-step workflows make it hard to estimate how likely their actions are to succeed; existing calibration methods based on surface text or logprobs are insufficient.

Method: They study whether internal residual-stream representations can predict eventual task success. They introduce: (1) Latent Trajectory Dynamics (LTD), which summarizes how internal representations evolve over the interaction trajectory; and (2) Action Representation Probe (ARP), which uses representations at action-decision points to predict success. They test across multiple interactive coding benchmarks (Bash, SQL, Python) and several model families.

Result: Across all three benchmarks and three model families, both LTD and ARP outperform baselines that rely on surface-level text features or sequence-based calibration, providing better prediction of task success/failure.

Conclusion: Internal model representations carry strong, exploitable signals about whether an agentic interaction will succeed. LTD and ARP can be used as low-cost, zero-overhead reliability monitors without changing prompts or requiring multiple rollouts.

Abstract: As agentic systems getting adopted rapidly in safety critical applications, it is vital to measure the confidence associated with the agentic actions. In comparison to the traditional machine learning systems, agentic workflows have complex failure modes with planning, tool invocation and dynamic environment interactions. In this paper, we investigate whether model's internal representations provide stronger signals of eventual task success in multi-turn agentic setups. We introduce two complementary methods: Latent Trajectory Dynamics (LTD), which summarizes changes in residual-stream representations across an an interaction trajectory, and the Action Representation Probe (ARP), which predicts success from representations formed at action decisions. Across three interactive benchmarks (Bash, SQL, Python) and three model families (Qwen14B, Qwen7B, DeepSeek6.7B), our methods consistently outperform surface level generation and sequence-based calibration baselines providing a zero-overhead reliability monitor that requires neither prompt alterations nor multi-sample rollouts.

</details>


### [77] [ContractEval: Query-Conditioned Execution Matching for Procedural Instruction Conformance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09458)
*Praphul Singh,Shanu Kumar,Akshat Agarwal,Ganesh Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CONTRACTEVAL, a framework to explicitly model and check procedural obligations for LLM agents, enabling fine-grained detection of structural failures that standard output-only or trace-aware evaluations miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents begin executing multi-step procedures rather than just answering questions, they can fail in subtle ways where the final output looks reasonable but key checks, branches, or invariants are skipped. Existing evaluation methods either look only at final outputs or at raw traces, and neither directly encodes which procedural obligations were supposed to be active for a given query, making it hard to detect and localize such structural failures.

Method: The authors define a representation of procedural instructions as "query-active obligations"—explicit contracts describing required checks, branches, dependencies, invariants, and output conditions. They build CONTRACTEVAL to match these obligations against evidence from model outputs or execution traces, classifying mismatches into specific conformance failure types such as omissions, wrong branches, ordering errors, extra actions, invariant violations, and output-contract breaches. They evaluate the framework on a controlled set of audited procedural contracts, using both gold-standard expected/observed graphs and LLM-backed extraction of traces.

Result: In experiments with injected structural failures, conventional output-only and trace-aware LLM judges miss many of these failures. With access to gold expected and observed graphs, CONTRACTEVAL detects and localizes all structural failures. When relying on LLM-based extraction to build the observed graphs, much of this diagnostic signal is preserved, though performance is sensitive to calibration and extraction quality.

Conclusion: CONTRACTEVAL makes procedural conformance explicit and auditable by turning implicit instructions into a set of obligations that can be systematically checked against outputs or traces. While it does not provide a formal compliance guarantee—especially when dependent on LLM-based extraction—it substantially improves the ability to detect and localize structural errors that are invisible under standard final-answer-focused evaluation.

Abstract: As LLM agents move from answering questions to carrying out procedures, failures can be unwarranted rather than visibly wrong: the final response looks acceptable even though the system skipped the check, branch, dependency, or invariant that made the answer justified. Output-only evaluation sees the answer, and trace-aware judging sees activity, but neither identifies which obligations were active for the query. We introduce CONTRACTEVAL, a diagnostic framework for making those active obligations explicit. It represents procedural instructions as query-active obligations and matches them against response or trace evidence, turning omissions, wrong branches, ordering errors, extra actions, invariant breaches, and output-contract violations into distinct conformance failures. On a controlled suite of audited procedural contracts, output-only and trace-aware LLM judges miss many injected structural failures; under gold expected and observed graphs, ContractEval detects and localizes all of them. LLM-backed extraction preserves much of this signal but remains calibration-sensitive. ContractEval is therefore not a compliance guarantee; it makes procedural conformance auditable rather than implicit in final-answer quality.

</details>


### [78] [Multi-Agent Agentic Graph Learning via Structural Signatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09565)
*Liang Qu,Jianxin Li,Hua Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MAAGL, a multi-agent framework for agentic graph learning that assigns specialized LLM agents to graph communities and uses permutation-invariant structural signatures plus relevance-filtered semantic evidence to enable efficient, collaborative graph reasoning, outperforming prior AGL methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic graph learning methods typically use a single reasoning policy (either one agent or role-based agents sharing a policy) over the entire graph, which is suboptimal for graphs with heterogeneous structures and semantics. Moreover, they verbalize graph structures into natural language, losing permutation invariance and causing sensitivity to node ordering, and suffer from rapidly growing context windows when sampling large neighborhoods. The authors aim to design an AGL framework that better adapts to heterogeneous graph regions, preserves graph permutation invariance, and controls context size while enabling effective multi-agent collaboration.

Method: The authors introduce MAAGL, a multi-agent agentic graph learning framework. They first partition the graph into communities and assign an independent LLM-based agent to each community, enabling region-specific specialization and distinct memory. Structural and semantic evidence are represented separately: structural evidence is compressed into a permutation-invariant, fixed-size structural signature that is dynamically updated along the agent’s trajectory, while semantic evidence is selected as the top-k most relevant nodes. Agents maintain historical trajectories indexed by similar structural signatures; by comparing current signatures to this history, an agent estimates its confidence in its reasoning. When confidence is low or signatures indicate ambiguity, agents initiate a debate-style collaboration across communities, sharing summarized evidence and arguments to refine predictions.

Result: Through extensive experiments on four benchmark datasets for graph reasoning, MAAGL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art agentic graph learning baselines. The method achieves better predictive accuracy and more efficient use of context, demonstrating advantages particularly on graphs with heterogeneous structural and semantic patterns. Ablation studies show the contributions of graph partitioning, structural signatures, semantic filtering, and debate-style collaboration to overall performance.

Conclusion: MAAGL shows that community-level specialization and collaboration among multiple LLM agents can significantly improve agentic graph learning on heterogeneous graphs. By separating structural and semantic evidence, enforcing permutation-invariant structural signatures, and controlling semantic context via relevance-based filtering, MAAGL addresses core limitations of prior AGL methods. The framework is effective and scalable across multiple benchmarks, suggesting a promising direction for combining graph structure with multi-agent LLM reasoning in complex graph reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Agentic graph learning (AGL) has recently achieved promising results on graph reasoning tasks, where an agent powered by a large language model (LLM) sequentially samples the graph as evidence to support its final prediction. Existing methods either employ a single agent or orchestrate multiple role-based agents to reason and learn over the entire graph, but both essentially rely on a shared reasoning policy across different graph regions, which can be suboptimal for graphs with heterogeneous structural and semantic patterns. Inspired by the progress of multi-agent collaboration on complex reasoning tasks, a natural remedy is to let multiple agents own different memory and collaborate; however, applying this paradigm to graphs directly faces two challenges. First, existing AGL methods typically verbalize graph structures into natural-language descriptions for LLM agents, making the reasoning process sensitive to the ordering of structural information and thereby breaking the permutation-invariant nature of graphs. Second, incorporating increasingly large sampled neighborhoods leads to rapidly growing contexts. To address these challenges, this paper introduces a multi-agent agentic graph learning (i.e., MAAGL) framework. MAAGL partitions the graph into communities and assigns an independent agent to each community for region-specific specialization. MAAGL represents structural and semantic evidence separately. Structural evidence is summarized by a dynamically updated structural signature that is permutation-invariant and fixed in size, while semantic evidence is filtered to the top-k nodes ranked by relevance. Based on historical trajectories with similar signatures, agents estimate their confidence and trigger debate-style collaboration when needed. Extensive experiments on four benchmark datasets show that MAAGL outperforms SOTA AGL methods.

</details>


### [79] [CityPlanner: A Sandbox Agent for Executable Urban Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09578)
*Wentao Zhang,Jingyuan Wang,Zetong Zhou,Yifan Yang,Wenrui Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CityPlanner is a sandbox-agent framework that uses a unified file-based environment and atomic-task reinforcement learning to solve complex, real-world urban planning optimization problems more effectively than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban planning involves complex spatial optimization with large decision spaces and real-world constraints like cost and service quality. Existing optimization and RL methods usually target fixed problem formulations, rely on task-specific designs, and handle constraints in ad hoc ways, limiting generality and scalability. There is a need for a more unified, executable, and learning-friendly framework that can adapt across different planning tasks while remaining practical for real-world use.

Method: The authors propose CityPlanner, a sandbox-agent framework built around UrbanSandbox, a unified file-based environment. In this environment, agents can read task specifications, propose planning solutions, execute evaluators, and iteratively refine their decisions based on executable feedback. To make long-horizon planning more tractable, they introduce atomic-task reinforcement learning, which decomposes the planning process into two subtasks: BuildPlan (initial construction of a plan) and ImprovePlan (iterative refinement guided by feedback). They train agents on these atomic tasks and evaluate them on a real-world urban planning benchmark, comparing against heuristic methods, task-specific RL systems, and general LLM-agent baselines. They also perform ablation studies to isolate the effect of each component.

Result: On a real-world urban planning benchmark, CityPlanner consistently outperforms heuristic baselines, task-specific reinforcement learning methods, and generic LLM-agent approaches in terms of objective metrics such as cost and service quality. Ablation experiments show that each component—UrbanSandbox, atomic-task RL, and iterative deployment—contributes meaningfully to the performance gains.

Conclusion: A unified sandbox-agent framework with executable, file-based environments and atomic-task reinforcement learning can significantly improve performance on complex urban planning tasks compared to existing heuristic, specialized RL, and generic LLM-agent methods. Structuring long planning trajectories into BuildPlan and ImprovePlan stages, combined with iterative deployment within UrbanSandbox, yields a practical and generalizable approach for real-world spatial optimization in urban planning.

Abstract: Urban planning is a real-world spatial optimization problem that requires selecting feasible actions from large candidate spaces under practical objectives such as cost and service quality. Existing optimization and reinforcement learning methods are effective for fixed formulations, but often depend on task-specific representations and constraint handling. We propose \emph{CityPlanner}, a sandbox-agent framework for executable urban planning. CityPlanner introduces \emph{UrbanSandbox}, a unified file-based environment where agents inspect task files, generate plans, run evaluators, and revise decisions based on executable feedback. To make learning tractable, we further propose atomic-task reinforcement learning, which decomposes long sandbox trajectories into \emph{BuildPlan} for initial construction and \emph{ImprovePlan} for feedback-based refinement. Experiments on a real-world benchmark show that CityPlanner consistently outperforms heuristic, task-specific RL, and general LLM-agent baselines. Ablations verify the contributions of UrbanSandbox, atomic-task RL, and iterative deployment. We release the code and dataset at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/co-agent-C1C8

</details>


### [80] [A Function-Space Approach to the Statistical Mechanics of Learning Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09589)
*Yizhou Zhang,Weichen Wu,Lun Du,Zhengjie Miao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper builds a statistical‑mechanics framework directly in function space to describe how deep networks learn, showing that learning dynamics are governed by a learning operator M, and that integrating out parameter fluctuations yields a fluctuation potential Φ_fluc(M;B) that favors alignment of learning dynamics with low-curvature, smooth function directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the immense dimensionality and nonlinear dynamics of neural network parameters, their learning behavior often appears simple and regular at a macroscopic level. Existing analyses largely work in parameter space, which can obscure the emergent structure in the space of functions implemented by the network. The authors want a principled, statistical‑mechanical description of learning that lives directly in function space, to explain why and how stable, low‑complexity behavior emerges during training.

Method: The authors treat parameter configurations as microscopic states and the induced functions—and their dynamical operators—as macroscopic variables. For mean‑squared error, they show that the exact error dynamics are governed by the learning operator M = J J*, with J the Jacobian in function space. They then write the conditional stochastic dynamics with a dynamical Boltzmann weight, combine this with the parameter‑space density of states characterized locally by a statistical operator B (related to curvature), and integrate over local parameter fluctuations. This yields a fluctuation‑induced contribution to an effective potential: Φ_fluc(M;B) = (σ_ξ^2 / 2) log det(M^{-1} + B) + const. They analyze this potential spectrally and specialize it to ReLU‑type networks, where B can be expressed as σ_ξ^2 L* K L with L encoding coarse‑grained second‑order structure and K an anisotropy operator.

Result: They derive an explicit fluctuation potential Φ_fluc(M;B) that depends on the spectra and relative orientation of the learning operator M and the statistical operator B. At fixed eigenvalues, Φ_fluc is stationary under rotations when M and B commute, and is minimized when large eigenvalues of M are paired with small eigenvalues of B. Misalignment between the eigenspaces of M and B produces a restoring contribution that pushes the system toward better alignment. For ReLU-type function spaces under mild stability conditions, they obtain B = σ_ξ^2 L* K L, showing that directions with small B correspond (up to bounded anisotropy in K) to function directions with low structural curvature.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates that function space is a natural macroscopic level to describe the collective organization of learning in deep networks. The fluctuation potential favors rapid error relaxation along directions that are both dynamically favorable (large M eigenvalues) and structurally smooth (small B, i.e., low curvature). This explains a bias toward learning smoother, data‑adaptive functions and provides a principled way to link microscopic parameter dynamics to emergent, stable behavior in function space.

Abstract: Deep neural networks exhibit regular macroscopic behavior despite highly nonlinear dynamics in vast parameter spaces. We develop a statistical-mechanical description of learning directly in function space, treating parameter configurations as microscopic realizations and functions with their dynamical operators as macroscopic variables. For mean-squared loss, the exact error dynamics are governed by the learning operator \(M=JJ^\ast\). Combining the dynamical Boltzmann weight of the conditional stochastic dynamics with the parameter-space density of states, whose local curvature defines a statistical operator \(B\), and integrating over local fluctuations yields
  $$ Φ_{\mathrm{fluc}}(M;B)=\frac{σ_ξ^2}{2}\log\det(M^{-1}+B)+\mathrm{const}. $$
  At fixed spectrum, this term is rotationally stationary when \([M,B]=0\), is minimized by pairing large eigenvalues of \(M\) with small eigenvalues of \(B\), and generates a local restoring contribution against rotational mismatch. For ReLU-type function spaces under mild stable statistical conditions, \(B=σ_ξ^2L^\ast\mathcal K L\), where \(L\) measures coarse-grained second-order structure. Thus the low-\(B\) sector corresponds, up to bounded anisotropy of \(\mathcal K\), to low structural curvature, implying a preference for faster relaxation along smooth, data-adaptive directions. These results identify function space as a natural macroscopic level for studying stable collective organization in learning.

</details>


### [81] [From State Synchronization to Cognitive Self-Evolution: An Operational Architecture for Cognitive Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09625)
*Haoran Gao,An Li,Zhen Li,Jun Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a structured, four-layer architecture for Cognitive Digital Twins that creates a self-evolving closed loop from physical systems to task execution and learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital Twins are evolving from simple state mirroring to intelligent, task-oriented systems, but current work on Cognitive Digital Twins is fragmented and focuses on isolated techniques rather than a holistic architecture for integrating cognition into DTs. There is a need for a systematic framework that shows how cognition, knowledge, and task decision-making can be organized and interact within DT systems.

Method: The authors design a four-layer CDT architecture (physical layer, digital-twin layer, cognitive layer, task layer) and define how information and feedback flow among them to form a self-evolving closed-loop operation. They formalize two key operation modes—user-request-driven cognition and self-driven cognition—and conceptually analyze enabling mechanisms such as semantic communication, knowledge querying, task orchestration, and closed-loop synchronization. A lightweight simulation is conducted to demonstrate the feasibility of closed-loop task execution with limited semantic information and the performance gains from accumulated task experience.

Result: The architectural model shows that CDT systems can: (1) synchronize physical states to digital representations; (2) build task-specific cognitive models using knowledge, memory, and attention; (3) generate task-level decisions under constraints; and (4) iteratively improve via operational feedback that refines cognitive experience and updates digital representations. The simulation indicates that the proposed closed-loop framework can accomplish tasks reliably with reduced semantic information and that task performance improves as experience accumulates.

Conclusion: The proposed four-layer CDT architecture provides a systematic, closed-loop framework for integrating cognition into Digital Twin systems, supporting both user-driven and self-driven operation modes. It clarifies key mechanisms and challenges for semantic communication, knowledge management, task orchestration, and synchronization, offering a structured foundation for designing and implementing future Cognitive Digital Twin systems.

Abstract: As Digital Twin (DT) systems evolve beyond state synchronization toward task-oriented and knowledge-driven operation, Cognitive Digital Twins (CDTs) have emerged as an extension that incorporates cognitive capabilities into twin operation. Existing CDT studies often focus on specific enabling techniques, such as learning modules, knowledge graphs, and large language models, while providing limited insight into how cognition can be systematically integrated into DT architectures. To address this issue, this paper proposes a four-layer CDT architecture consisting of the physical layer, digital-twin layer, cognitive layer, and task layer. The proposed architecture establishes a self-evolving closed operational loop spanning these four layers, in which physical states are synchronized into digital representations, cognition constructs task-specific cognitive models through knowledge, memory, and attention, and task-level decisions are generated under practical constraints. Operational feedback further refines cognitive experience and updates relationships and annotations in the digital representation, enabling subsequent task interpretation, initiation, and reasoning to evolve with system operation. Based on this framework, two representative operation modes are characterized: user-request-driven cognition and self-driven cognition. We further discuss key enabling mechanisms and deployment challenges associated with semantic communication, knowledge querying, task orchestration, and closed-loop synchronization. A lightweight simulation study illustrates reliable closed-loop task feasibility under limited semantic information and improved operational efficiency through accumulated task experience. The proposed framework provides a structured foundation for the design and development of future CDT systems.

</details>


### [82] [Seven Sources of Physical AI Capability Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09627)
*Gang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a taxonomy of seven distinct sources by which AI systems acquire capabilities, focusing on how capabilities are formed rather than what the system looks like or does.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing taxonomies of AI and robotics—organized by morphology, architecture, learning algorithm, task, or domain—do not directly explain what factors actually give rise to a system’s capabilities. This gap makes it hard to compare, transfer, or govern capabilities across very different systems that nonetheless achieve similar behaviors. The authors aim to provide a unifying conceptual framework that classifies AI systems by the sources of their capability formation, enabling clearer explanations and more systematic analysis.

Method: The authors introduce the concept of a "capability-formation source" (a factor that materially contributes to the formation of capabilities, distinct from components or implementation steps). They then use reconstructive induction with theoretical saturation: (1) assembling a research matrix and tracing it to primary studies; (2) deduplicating literature and defining coding rules; (3) running three rounds of maximum-difference and negative-case sampling, stress-testing the candidate sources against challenging cases such as curriculum learning, self-supervised and open-ended learning, active inference, planning/search, neuro-symbolic systems, digital twins, generative physical world models, and morphology-control co-design. They iteratively refine and test whether new evidence requires adding new sources or revising definitions.

Result: They identify seven non-exclusive capability-formation sources: Recorded-Experience (RE), Predictive-Modeling (PM), Evaluative-Interaction (EI), Surrogate-Environment (SE), Mechanism-Grounded (MG), Embodied-Coupling (EC), and Evolution-Driven (ED) Formation. Applying their methodology up to a cutoff date (September 4, 2026), they find that all 49 examined evidence records can be explained as combinations of these seven sources. None of the challenge rounds (R1–R3) required introducing an eighth source or materially changing the core definitions or boundary rules of the seven identified sources.

Conclusion: Within the defined scope and time-bounded literature, the seven-source framework achieves theoretical saturation: every studied case of capability formation can be accounted for by some combination of the seven sources, though the authors do not claim future completeness. This framework disentangles similarity in observed capabilities from similarity in how those capabilities are formed, and is proposed as a tool for analyzing explanation, transfer, replication, system dependencies, governance evidence, and broader economic or strategic implications of AI capabilities.

Abstract: Capabilities relevant to Physical AI can arise from materially different formation histories, yet existing taxonomies organized by morphology, architecture, learning algorithm, task, or domain do not directly answer what gives rise to a capability. We define a capability-formation source as a factor materially contributing to capability formation, distinct from components or construction steps. We identify seven non-exclusive sources: Recorded-Experience (RE), Predictive-Modeling (PM), Evaluative-Interaction (EI), Surrogate-Environment (SE), Mechanism-Grounded (MG), Embodied-Coupling (EC), and Evolution-Driven (ED) Formation. Using reconstructive induction with theoretical saturation, we traced a research matrix to primary studies, deduplicated the literature, set coding rules, and conducted three rounds of maximum-difference and negative-case sampling. Challenges included curriculum and self-supervised learning, active inference, open-ended and developmental learning, planning and search, neuro-symbolic architectures, digital twins, generative physical world models, and morphology-control co-design. Within the scope and criteria fixed as of September 4, 2026, all 49 evidence records were explainable by the seven sources individually or in combination. No R1-R3 challenge produced an irreducible eighth source, and R3 required no new core definition or substantive boundary rule. We therefore claim theoretical saturation within the stated scope, not logical completeness or exhaustive future coverage. The framework distinguishes similarity in observed capability from similarity in how it was formed, supporting analysis of explanation, transfer, replication, dependencies, governance evidence, and geoeconomic foundations.

</details>


### [83] [RobustSGPO: Search-Space Control for Agent Harness Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09646)
*Zibo Zhao,Jijun Shi,Mo Zhou,Zhongyuan Wang,Shifu Bie,Yunfei Zhang,Xuanting Zhou,Xiangyu Wu,Bin Liu,Ruiming Tang,Wenwu Ou,Kun Gai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes RobustSGPO, a more structured and robust semantic-gradient-based prompt optimization method for LLM agents, showing improved task completion and output quality in an AgentX brainstorming workflow under token budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semantic-gradient-based prompt optimization (SGPO) improves LLM agent performance using execution feedback, but existing approaches have underspecified and potentially brittle local update rules—particularly regarding what to edit in the prompt, how to edit it, and how to manage search trajectories and retention. This gap limits robustness, transfer across task families, and control over the optimization search space and permissions.

Method: The authors introduce RobustSGPO, which (1) explicitly specifies the type and scope of prompt edits requested, (2) constructs and validates patches to prompts, and (3) manages search via either continuing from the current best prompt (incumbent) or from retained snapshots. They systematically explore design choices including permission scheduling (e.g., periodic 1→2→3 permission levels vs fixed max permission), cumulative control mechanisms, and strategies for task-family transfer (e.g., category-based vs random retention) within the AgentX brainstorming workflow. The evaluation uses 120 tasks, 95 runs, and 7,350 candidate prompt attempts under a 20M-token budget.

Result: Periodic 1→2→3 permission scheduling outperforms a fixed maximum-permission setting by 0.28 points on a test-score metric. RobustSGPO raises completion rates on 30 held-out tasks from 60.0% to 80.0% and improves test quality scores from 3.77 to 4.14 within the same 20M-token budget. Category-based retention better preserves performance on the original task distribution after a shift, whereas random retention achieves higher performance on the new destination tasks.
