Search-space control via executable edits and alternate starting points yields higher output quality but introduces a measurable computational/retention overhead.

Conclusion: Structuring SGPO into RobustSGPO—with explicit edit specification, patch construction/validation, and managed search via snapshots—substantially improves robustness, completion, and quality for LLM agent prompt optimization under realistic token budgets. Scheduling of permissions and carefully designed retention strategies allow better trade-offs between source-task stability and destination-task performance, while search-space control provides quality gains despite additional overhead.

Abstract: Semantic-gradient-based prompt optimization (SGPO) improves agent harnesses using execution feedback, but its local update rule leaves the choice of edit scope and operation unresolved. We introduce RobustSGPO, which specifies the requested edit, constructs and checks the patch, and continues search from either the incumbent or retained snapshots. We evaluate permission scheduling, cumulative controls, and task-family transfer in the AgentX brainstorming workflow using 120 tasks, 95 runs, and 7,350 candidate attempts. Periodic $1\to2\to3$ scheduling exceeds fixed maximum permission by 0.28 test-score points. RobustSGPO increases completion on 30 held-out tasks from 60.0% to 80.0% and improves test quality from 3.77 to 4.14 under a 20-million-token budget. Category retention reduces source-task degradation after a shift, whereas random retention reaches a higher destination endpoint. Search-space control benefits quality through executable edits and alternative starting points, with measurable retention overhead.

</details>


### [84] [Black-Box Red Teaming of Agentic AI: A Taxonomy-Driven Framework for Automated Risk Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09647)
*Divyanshu Kumar,Nitin Aravind Birur,Tanay Baswa,Sahil Agarwal,Prashanth Harshangi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a black-box framework to systematically and automatically evaluate security and safety risks of agentic AI systems in multi-step, tool-using scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations mostly focus on single-turn chat models and overlook the expanded security surface and risks of deployed agentic systems that autonomously use tools and handle untrusted inputs. There is a need for systematic, scalable, and realistic testing that exposes multi-step vulnerabilities without requiring internal access to the systems.

Method: The authors design a black-box, risk-aware evaluation framework based on a seven-domain taxonomy that maps observable agent behaviors to risk categories. They develop SAGE-RT, an automated red-teaming system that generates about 120 adversarial scenarios per risk domain, and use LLM-based judges along with human validation to assess agent behavior. They apply this framework to two agent architectures (CrewAI and AutoGen) instantiated with four different base models.

Result: When evaluated with this framework, the tested agent systems exhibit high levels of vulnerability: on average, 56.25% of scenarios show governance risks, multi-agent setups expose around 65% privacy risk, and up to 85% of cases reveal agent behavior vulnerabilities, demonstrating that even state-of-the-art architectures are far from robust.

Conclusion: The black-box evaluation framework can reliably uncover critical architectural vulnerabilities in agentic systems without requiring privileged access or system internals. This shows both that current agent systems pose significant safety and security risks and that the proposed method offers a scalable path to auditing and hardening agent deployments in realistic, multi-step settings.

Abstract: Agentic systems are rapidly moving to production, where they read untrusted inputs, call tools with real permissions, and act autonomously, expanding the security surface beyond chat-only models. Yet standard evaluations remain single-turn and fail to capture multi-step agent vulnerabilities. We present a systematic black-box framework for risk-aware agent evaluation requiring only basic system descriptions. Our approach introduces: (1) a seven-domain taxonomy mapping observable behaviors to risk categories, (2) fully automated SAGE-RT red teaming producing 120 adversarial scenarios per domain, and (3) human-validated evaluation using LLM judges. Empirical validation across two agent architectures (CrewAI and AutoGen) with four base models reveals alarming patterns: 56.25\% average governance risk, 65\% privacy risk in multi-agent configurations, and agent behavior vulnerabilities reaching 85\%. Our black-box approach effectively identifies critical architectural vulnerabilities without privileged access, providing a scalable path toward safer agent deployments.

</details>


### [85] [RESCUE-BENCH: Towards Relation-Aware Multi-Party Emotional Support Conversation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09657)
*Haichuan Hu,Yang Xiao,Mingni Tang,Jiawen Duan,Quanjun Zhang,Congqing He,Hao Zhang,Jiashuo Wang,Johan F. Hoorn,Wenjie Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes and benchmarks a new task: relation-aware emotional support conversations, showing that current LLMs struggle with modeling interpersonal relationship dynamics in multi-party emotional support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most prior emotional support dialogue systems focus on dyadic (one-on-one) interactions and treat emotions as individual states, neglecting how interpersonal relationships and their evolution shape support needs and appropriate responses in multi-party settings (e.g., couples, families). The authors aim to fill this gap by creating a benchmark that explicitly tests whether LLMs can understand and leverage relationship dynamics to provide better emotional support.

Method: The authors define a new task setting called relation-aware emotional support conversation, centered on understanding and using evolving interpersonal relations in conversations. They build RESCUE, a benchmark constructed from real couple and family interviews: 191 conversation samples, 7,079 annotated turns, and about 1,065 minutes of video. They provide rich socio-emotional and support-related annotations and formalize six evaluation tasks that capture two core capabilities: (1) Relational Understanding and (2) Relation-Sensitive Support (e.g., predicting relation patterns, viewpoints, and support strategies). They then evaluate ten large language models on these tasks to assess their capabilities and limitations.

Result: Across the six defined tasks, current LLMs do relatively well on tasks that depend mainly on local emotional cues or explicit intervention hints. However, they show notable weaknesses on relation-intensive tasks that require deeper modeling of interpersonal dynamics, such as predicting how relationships evolve (relation pattern prediction), inferring different participants’ viewpoints, and choosing support strategies that are sensitive to these relational dynamics.

Conclusion: The study concludes that current LLMs are limited in their ability to model and reason about interpersonal relationship dynamics in emotional support conversations, particularly in multi-party settings. While they can handle local emotional cues, they struggle with tasks that require an integrated understanding of evolving relationships and tailored, relation-sensitive support strategies. The RESCUE benchmark is proposed as a resource to drive progress on these more complex aspects of emotional support dialogue systems.

Abstract: Existing emotional support conversation systems mainly focus on one-on-one seeker-supporter interactions and individual emotional states, leaving interpersonal relations in multi-party scenarios underexplored. In this work, we introduce relation-aware emotional support conversation, a new task that evaluates whether LLMs can capture and utilize the evolving dynamics of relationships to offer more effective emotional support. We construct RESCUE (Relation-aware Emotional Support Conversation Understanding and Evaluation Benchmark) from real couple and family interview conversations, containing 191 samples, 7,079 annotated turns, and 1,064.8 minutes of video. Based on rich annotations of socio-emotional and support-related dynamics, RESCUE defines six tasks that evaluate two core capabilities required for relation-aware emotional support: Relational Understanding and Relation-Sensitive Support. Experiments with ten LLMs show that current models perform relatively well on tasks relying on local emotional or intervention cues, but struggle with relation-intensive tasks such as relation pattern prediction, viewpoint prediction, and support strategy prediction. These findings reveal the limitations of current LLMs in modeling interpersonal relations and making relation-sensitive support decisions.

</details>


### [86] [PRAGMA: Evaluating Personalized Guidance with Memory Alignment in Lifelong Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09664)
*Hyojeong Yu,Hyukhun Koh,Minsung Kim,Yunah Jang,Kyomin Jung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PRAGMA is a benchmark designed to evaluate how well language models provide personalized guidance over long-term, evolving conversations, going beyond simple factual recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used as long-term personal assistants, using entire interaction histories is computationally expensive and unreliable: models struggle to consistently find and use the most relevant information in long contexts. Existing memory evaluations mainly test retrieval and factual recall, but real users often seek complex, personalized guidance—recommendations, planning, and decision support—based on evolving preferences and experiences, including correcting incorrect user assumptions. There is a gap in benchmarks that capture these richer, longitudinal, guidance-oriented behaviors.

Method: The authors construct PRAGMA, a benchmark consisting of curated, longitudinal conversation histories between users and assistants. Each history is annotated with evidence and associated with guidance scenarios that require integrating information across multiple past conversations, accounting for evolving user context and misconceptions. They then run experiments comparing different retrieval systems, memory systems, and long-context models on these tasks, measuring both their ability to retrieve the right evidence and to use it effectively for personalized guidance.

Result: Across a range of retrieval methods, memory architectures, and long-context models, performance is poor: systems frequently fail to recover the most relevant conversational evidence and, even when evidence is available, they often underuse or misuse it when generating personalized guidance. This shows a significant performance gap relative to the demands of realistic, long-term guidance scenarios.

Conclusion: Current conversational memory and long-context approaches are inadequate for robust, personalized guidance in long-term interactions. Simply extending context length or optimizing for factual recall is not enough. The paper argues for new memory architectures and reasoning mechanisms that better support conversational retrieval and memory-grounded reasoning, enabling models to track evolving user states and provide higher-quality, context-aware guidance over time.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as personalized assistants that interact with users over extended periods of time. As conversations grow longer, relying on full interaction histories becomes increasingly inefficient and unreliable: long contexts introduce substantial computational overhead, making it difficult for models to consistently identify and utilize the most relevant information for the current request. These challenges have motivated memory systems that structure and retrieve user-specific information. In realistic interactions, users often seek practical guidance such as recommendations, planning, and decision support. Unlike factual recall tasks, personalized guidance requires models to integrate information across multiple past conversations and reason about changing user preferences and experiences. However, existing conversational memory evaluations mainly focus on retrieval and factual recall. To study this challenge, we introduce PRAGMA, a benchmark for evaluating personalized guidance in long-term conversations. PRGAMA contains curated longitudinal conversation histories, evidence annotations, and guidance scenarios grounded in evolving user contexts and incorrect user assumptions. Experiments across retrieval systems, memory systems, and long-context models reveal that current systems struggle both to recover the appropriate conversational evidence and to effectively use it for personalized guidance. Our results highlight the need for memory architectures that support robust conversational retrieval and memory-grounded reasoning beyond evidence recall.

</details>


### [87] [Safe to Stop? Risk-Constrained Stopping for Sequential Clinical Diagnosis Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09678)
*Yuexin Wu,Vasile Rus*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose Cros, a method to decide when an autonomous clinical diagnostic agent should stop testing, diagnose, or defer, with finite-sample risk guarantees, and show it can reduce errors, tests, and costs vs naive stopping, though current results are exploratory, not a safety guarantee.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous clinical diagnosis agents need reliable rules not just for what to test next but also for when to stop and either diagnose or defer, under explicit risk constraints. Existing benchmarks mostly assume fixed-length or unconstrained interactions and report only final accuracy, implicitly trusting internal stopping heuristics without guarantees. There is a need for a principled stopping layer that can be calibrated with finite-sample guarantees on selective error and coverage, suitable for safety-critical domains like medicine.

Method: They introduce Cros, a risk-constrained stopping framework that: (1) learns a state-wise ranking of diagnostic error risk across the sequential decision process; (2) designs stopping/defer policies using only disjoint development splits to avoid overfitting; and (3) applies learning-to-test (LTT) style exact statistical tests to entire sequential policies, guaranteeing bounds on selective error and minimum autonomous coverage in finite samples. The method requires that the set of candidate policies, the testing rule, and any randomization be fixed before using calibration labels, thereby enabling valid exact testing of policy risk under selective prediction settings.

Result: On a 1,834-episode MIMIC-derived abdominal pain dataset, the full error ranker achieves an AUROC of 0.853 for predicting state-wise errors, outperforming baselines based on maximum class probability (0.715) and the backbone model's native stop score (0.552). On a previously inspected 367-episode evaluation split, a Cros-based policy (using frozen weights) achieves 16.9% selective diagnostic error at 78.8% coverage, with average cost 5.57 and 0.68 tests per episode, compared to the backbone's native stopping which yields 30.8% error at 100% coverage, cost 8.14, and 1.53 tests. They also observe that forcing agents to always continue testing can increase error non-monotonically (28.3% with HPI only vs. 34.3% after full workup). However, a uniform-weight mixture ablation is cheaper on this specific viewed split despite not satisfying the prespecified development margins, and Cros meets its joint error/coverage criterion in only 6 of 20 development resplits.

Conclusion: Cros appears to be a promising approach for adding a statistically principled, risk-constrained stopping layer to sequential clinical diagnostic agents, improving error rates and test/cost efficiency compared to native stopping heuristics, and revealing that more testing does not always help. Yet, because of prior use of evaluation labels during development and mixed performance across resplits, current results should be treated as exploratory evidence of feasibility and for auditing, not as a validated safety guarantee. More rigorous, label-blind development and confirmatory testing are required for deployment-level assurances.

Abstract: Clinical diagnosis agents must decide not only what test to request next, but also when to diagnose or defer. Existing agent benchmarks largely evaluate accuracy after fixed or unconstrained interaction, leaving autonomous stopping reliability implicit. We present Cros, a risk-constrained stopping layer combining state-wise error ranking, policy design on disjoint development splits, and LTT-style exact tests of selective diagnostic error and minimum autonomous coverage for complete sequential policies. Its finite-sample guarantee requires the candidate family, testing rule, and any randomization to be frozen before calibration labels are accessed. On a 1,834-episode MIMIC-derived abdominal-pain benchmark, the full ranker achieves exploratory state-error AUROC 0.853, compared with 0.715 for maximum class probability and 0.552 for the backbone's native stop score. On the previously viewed 367-episode evaluation split, analytically averaging over the frozen Cros weights yields 16.9% selective error at 78.8% coverage, cost 5.57, and 0.68 tests, versus 30.8% error at 100% coverage, cost 8.14, and 1.53 tests under native stopping. Forced continuation is non-monotone: error is 28.3% with HPI alone and 34.3% after full workup. However, the uniform-weight mixture ablation is cheaper on this viewed split despite missing the locked development margins, and Cros nominally satisfies the joint criterion in only 6 of 20 development resplits. Because evaluation labels were inspected during earlier development, these findings provide exploratory feasibility and audit evidence, not a confirmatory safety certificate.

</details>


### [88] [Decision Shifts, Lost Label Functionality, and an Inconclusive Grounding Audit in Correctness-Gated Multi-Teacher Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09702)
*Xiaofei Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates correctness-gated multi-teacher distillation and finds that while correctness-weighted distillation improves some aggregate metrics and reduces unsafe actions, it causes severe failures in label coverage and does not demonstrate better rationale grounding over simpler hard filtering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether weighting teacher distillation by correctness can jointly improve decision accuracy, safety (unsafe-action rate), and rationale grounding in student models, beyond what is achievable with simpler approaches like hard filtering or standard unfiltered distillation.

Method: Run a fixed experimental setup with eight training ‘arms’ sharing the same data, student model size, and optimization budget. Seven arms use multi-teacher distillation from a fixed pool of three teacher responses; one is source-label supervised fine-tuning (SFT). One key arm applies correctness-weighted distillation, gating and weighting teacher signals based on correctness. Performance is evaluated across three random seeds on 267 held-out examples using accuracy, five-label macro-F1, conditional unsafe-action rate, and a small human audit of rationale grounding/groundedness on a subset of outputs.

Result: The correctness-weighted arm substantially improved headline metrics over unfiltered distillation: +0.166 accuracy, +0.1323 macro-F1, and -0.4979 unsafe-action rate in observed intervals. However, source-label SFT still achieved the best macro-F1 overall. The correctness-weighted arm exhibited pathological behavior: zero recall on the ‘Refuted’ label for all seeds and, in two seeds, assigning ‘NotEnoughInfo’ to every claim example. A small human audit found no clear grounding benefits: weighted vs unfiltered had 0/20 vs 1/20 evidence-supported positives, and both produced positives with unsupported content (20/20 vs 19/20). Methodological limitations (non-paired sampling, overlapping sources, timing of the audit) make the audit inconclusive regarding system-level grounding differences. A simple hard-filtering baseline already reached strong metrics (0.660 accuracy, 0.530 macro-F1, 0.135 unsafe rate).

Conclusion: Correctness-gated, correctness-weighted multi-teacher distillation can shift aggregate metrics in seemingly positive ways but may redistribute decisions in a harmful way, causing loss of functionality on some labels and failing to demonstrate better rationale grounding. In this fixed setup, it offers no clear incremental benefit over hard filtering and may obscure important failure modes, underscoring the need for more nuanced evaluation of grounding and label coverage rather than relying on aggregate scores alone.

Abstract: Candidate decision correctness and rationale grounding are different objectives. We examine correctness-gated multi-teacher distillation in a fixed experiment. Eight arms share 4,330 sources, a 63.9M-parameter student, 12,990 optimization rows, 406 updates, evidence inputs, and a decoder; seven teacher-based arms use one fixed three-response pool. Three seeds are evaluated on 267 held-out examples. Relative to unfiltered distillation, the correctness-weighted arm differed in accuracy by +0.1660 (95% observed-matrix interval [0.0670, 0.2455]), five-label macro-F1 by +0.1323 ([0.0916, 0.1731]), and task-defined conditional unsafe-action rate by -0.4979 ([-0.5926, -0.3686]). These shifts do not imply uniformly better behavior. Source-label SFT had the highest mean macro-F1 (0.586). The weighted arm had zero Refuted recall in every seed, and two seeds assigned NotEnoughInfo to all 167 claim examples. In an availability-amended audit at one reference seed, weighted and unfiltered outputs had 0/20 versus 1/20 evidence-supported positives and 20/20 versus 19/20 positives containing unsupported material. Samples were non-paired, source overlap was not serialized, and the amendment followed automatic summarization but preceded annotation. The audit therefore cannot estimate a common-source grounding effect and is inconclusive about system-level improvement or harm. Hard filtering already achieved 0.660 accuracy, 0.530 macro-F1, and 0.135 conditional unsafe rate. The implemented weighted arm showed no demonstrated incremental decision benefit over hard filtering. This fixed-matrix failure analysis shows decision redistribution with lost label functionality; the available human audit does not establish a grounding gain.

</details>


### [89] [Which Tokens Should SFT Actually Learn? A Token-Trimming Perspective on Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09707)
*Yaning Jia,Chunhui Zhang,Wenxuan Xu,Xingjian Diao,Xiaoyuan Wang,Soroush Vosoughi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TrimSFT is a token-level reweighting method for supervised fine-tuning of LLMs on math reasoning that focuses learning on tokens with intermediate logit gaps, improving performance over standard SFT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard supervised fine-tuning uses uniform cross-entropy loss on all tokens, but in mathematical reasoning, not all tokens are equally informative. Uniform loss can overtrain already confident tokens and place excessive pressure on low-confidence or poorly modeled tokens, leading to inefficient and suboptimal training dynamics. The authors want a principled way to focus learning on tokens that provide the most useful training signal without requiring extra models or passes.

Method: They introduce Trimmed Logit-Gap SFT (TrimSFT), a token-level loss reweighting scheme based on the logit gap between the correct token and its highest-scoring competitor. Tokens with very large logit gaps (already mastered) and tokens with very small or negative gaps (too uncertain) are down-weighted, while those with intermediate gaps are emphasized. Concretely, they apply a Gaussian weight over the logit gap, centered at margin m with bandwidth τ, to scale the standard SFT loss, avoiding any reference model or extra forward pass. They also examine half-trim variants that remove supervision only on one side of the gap distribution and compare against monotonic reweighting schemes.

Result: On six base models from the Llama, Qwen, and DeepMath families and five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, TrimSFT consistently outperforms standard SFT. It attains the best average performance on five of six models and yields gains up to +26.9 points over SFT on MATH500. Empirically, the bandwidth τ is more critical than the precise margin m, and half-trim variants show inferior performance–robustness trade-offs compared to full trimming on both extremes. Token-level logit-gap distribution analyses indicate that TrimSFT leads to a more balanced confidence profile than uniform SFT or monotonic reweighting methods.

Conclusion: Focusing SFT learning on tokens with intermediate confidence, rather than treating all tokens equally, produces better training dynamics and performance in mathematical reasoning tasks. TrimSFT offers a simple, efficient way to implement this idea via Gaussian reweighting over logit gaps, without extra computation or reference models, and empirically improves a range of LLMs. The findings support trimming supervision at both extremes (overconfident and highly uncertain tokens) as a generally beneficial strategy for reasoning-oriented fine-tuning.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) applies a uniform cross-entropy loss to all target tokens, even though different tokens provide unequal learning signals for mathematical reasoning. This uniform treatment can over-sharpen already mastered tokens while amplifying learning pressure on uncertain, low-confidence tokens, leading to suboptimal training dynamics. We propose Trimmed Logit-Gap SFT (TrimSFT), a simple token-level reweighting method that scales the SFT loss according to the logit gap between the gold token and its strongest competitor. TrimSFT trims supervision away from both extremes: tokens already mastered (large logit gap) and tokens weakly supported by the current model (small or negative logit gap), concentrating learning within an intermediate logit-gap region between them. We instantiate this principle with a Gaussian weight centered at margin m with bandwidth τ, requiring no reference model or additional forward pass. We evaluate TrimSFT on six base models from the Llama, Qwen, and DeepMath families across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks. TrimSFT consistently improves over standard SFT, achieving the best average performance on five out of six models, with gains of up to +26.9 points over SFT on MATH500. Further analyses show that the bandwidth τ matters more than the exact margin location, and that half-trim variants that remove supervision pressure from only one side yield inferior trade-offs. A token-level logit-gap distribution analysis suggests that TrimSFT reshapes model confidence in a more balanced way than uniform SFT or monotonic reweighting methods. These results suggest that reasoning SFT can benefit from trimming both extremes rather than treating all tokens uniformly.

</details>


### [90] [Can Artificial Intelligence Support Healthcare and Mental Health Through Early Cyberbullying Detection ? The Impact of Emotion-Aware AI on Proactive Online Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09735)
*Hamed Jelodar,Amir Firouzi,Yen-Wu Lo,Maryam Tanha,Sajjad Dadkhah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CareGuard is an NLP-based early-warning framework that detects cyberbullying-related content to support mental health and online safety, optimizing both accuracy and computational efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cyberbullying and harmful online interactions negatively impact mental health, public well-being, and healthcare systems, creating a need for early-warning tools that can support proactive intervention and monitoring in healthcare and safety-critical contexts.

Method: The paper proposes CareGuard, which combines zero-shot semantic labeling with fine-tuned transformer-based language models such as BERT, DistilBERT, and RoBERTa for context-aware classification of cyberbullying categories. It incorporates an emotion-aware filtering mechanism and cosine similarity-based semantic screening to pre-filter content and prioritize semantically relevant and emotionally salient instances, thereby reducing unnecessary computation.

Result: On benchmark datasets, CareGuard achieves strong performance in detecting cyberbullying-related content while significantly improving computational efficiency through its filtering and screening mechanisms.

Conclusion: CareGuard can serve as a scalable, efficient early-warning system for cyberbullying detection suitable for integration into healthcare systems, mental health monitoring pipelines, and online safety platforms, balancing high detection accuracy with reduced computational cost.

Abstract: Healthcare systems, mental health, and public well-being are increasingly affected by cyberbullying and harmful online interactions. This paper presents CareGuard, an early-warning framework designed to support healthcare-driven mental health protection and proactive online safety through the detection of cyberbullying-related content using advanced natural language processing techniques. CareGuard integrates zero-shot semantic labeling with fine-tuned transformer-based models, including BERT, DistilBERT, and RoBERTa, to enable robust and context-aware classification across sensitive cyberbullying categories. To improve efficiency and reduce unnecessary computation in healthcare-oriented monitoring settings, the framework incorporates an emotion-aware filtering mechanism alongside cosine similarity-based semantic screening, allowing the system to focus on semantically relevant and emotionally salient content. Experimental results on benchmark datasets demonstrate that CareGuard effectively balances detection accuracy and computational efficiency, highlighting its potential for scalable deployment in healthcare systems, mental health monitoring, and online safety applications.

</details>


### [91] [LexAgentHallu: A Hierarchical Benchmark for Profiling Hallucinations in Legal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09754)
*Yujin Zhou,Mingxuan Zheng,Chuxue Cao,Huang Yidan,Jiale Chen,Yike Guo,Sirui Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces LexAgentHallu, a benchmark to diagnose and quantify how legal LLM agents hallucinate across multi-step tool-using trajectories, revealing nuanced failure patterns invisible to standard one-shot QA metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations for legal LLMs mainly focus on single-turn question answering and outcome-level accuracy, which fail to capture the process-level errors that emerge when models act as multi-step, tool-using legal agents. Moreover, current hallucination benchmarks are not tailored to law and thus lack fine-grained, legally meaningful diagnostics of where and how agentic hallucinations occur. There is a need for a benchmark that can characterize the extent and structure of hallucinations specific to legal reasoning and tool use over an agent’s full trajectory.

Method: The authors build LexAgentHallu via a four-stage expert-in-the-loop pipeline, creating 3414 instances spanning 17 legal categories and 6 task types. Each instance is labeled according to a dual-layer hallucination taxonomy with 7 high-level categories and 27 fine-grained subclasses that encompass both substantive legal reasoning errors and procedural or agent-control failures. They then design fine-grained evaluation metrics that can measure the degree of hallucination and pinpoint where, along a multi-step agent execution path, each type of failure occurs. Using these resources, they systematically evaluate 18 proprietary and open-source legal agents.

Result: Using LexAgentHallu to test 18 agents, the authors find a Right-Answer-Wrong-Reason phenomenon, where agents may reach correct final outcomes but through flawed intermediate reasoning or tool use. They also observe that hallucination subclasses tend to cluster into coherent patterns rather than appearing randomly, yielding distinctive profiles tied to the agentic framework, legal task type, and legal category. These structured patterns are not visible when only outcome-level accuracy is considered.

Conclusion: LexAgentHallu provides a legally specialized, trajectory-level benchmark and metric suite that exposes the structure and extent of agentic hallucinations in legal LLM agents. By revealing nuanced failure profiles such as clustered hallucination types and Right-Answer-Wrong-Reason behavior, the benchmark demonstrates strong diagnostic power beyond traditional outcome-based legal evaluations and can guide development and comparison of future legal AI agents.

Abstract: As large language models are increasingly deployed as tool-augmented legal agents, they introduce agentic hallucinations where tool-call and reasoning errors cascade into fabricated holdings and miscited authority. However, existing legal benchmarks evaluate only single-turn QA with outcome-level metrics, while agentic hallucination benchmarks lack legal-specific diagnostic capability. Neither answers to what extent and how a legal agent hallucinates along its trajectory. To address these limitations, we introduce LexAgentHallu, a legal agentic hallucination benchmark designed to evaluate to what extent and how legal agents fail along multi-step trajectories. Built through a four-stage expert-in-the-loop pipeline, LexAgentHallu contains 3414 instances across 17 legal categories and 6 task types. Each instance is annotated under a dual-layer hallucination taxonomy of 7 high-level categories and 27 fine-grained subclasses, covering both substantive errors and agent-procedural failures. We further design fine-grained metrics that quantify to what extent and localize how each failure occurs along an agent's execution path. Our evaluation across 18 proprietary and open-source agents uncovers a Right-Answer-Wrong-Reason effect and reveals that hallucination subclasses cluster rather than scatter, forming distinct agentic framework, legal task, and category profiles. These findings, invisible to outcome-level evaluation, validate the diagnostic power of LexAgentHallu for evaluating agentic hallucination in law.

</details>


### [92] [Procedural Memory Under Change: Reuse and Interference in Controlled Web Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09774)
*Yanze Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates when mismatched procedural memory in language agents actually causes behavioral errors and finds a tested region where mismatch is surprisingly benign.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Procedural memories in language-based agents allow reuse of successful task-solving routines, but they may become outdated as environments change. It is unclear under what conditions such mismatched memories lead to incorrect behavior, which is a safety and reliability concern for memory-augmented agents. The paper aims to characterize when mismatches between stored procedures and current tasks do or do not produce harmful interference.

Method: The authors first examine a retrospective, human-assisted adaptation case in BrowserGym TimeWarp’s WebShop V1–V6 development, where interface-specific code was updated but the high-level procedure was reportedly unchanged, noting this is not a clean autonomous memory evaluation. They then run controlled experiments on synthetic shopping decisions using a frozen procedural memory versus no-memory baselines. An initial pilot found one task where two memory conditions chose a more expensive item while a no-memory condition chose the minimum, but further probing failed to reveal consistent patterns like row-order effects or identity-binding failures. Subsequently, they systematically test four specific types of mismatch—changed quantities, altered evidence representation, local vs global optimization conflict, and distributed promotion evidence—across 32 experimental cells. Each cell uses a single temperature-0 generation with the same local qwen3:8b model configuration and no adaptive retry, and they search for predefined behavioral ‘interference signatures’.

Result: In the retrospective TimeWarp/WebShop case, the interface-level changes did not demonstrably alter the claimed high-level procedure, so it does not yield a clear test of autonomous memory interference. In the controlled synthetic shopping experiments, the initially observed case where memory conditions picked a more expensive item did not turn into a robust or generalizable interference pattern under further analysis. Across the 32 systematically designed mismatch conditions, none of the predefined diagnostic interference signatures appeared on tasks where the task evidence was explicit and sufficient, even though the stored procedure was deliberately mismatched to the current interface or evidence format.

Conclusion: The experiments delineate a specific ‘non-interference’ region: when current-task evidence is explicit and sufficient, a mismatched procedural memory can coexist with correct behavior, failing to produce the expected memory-induced errors. This suggests that procedural mismatch is not automatically hazardous in such settings, but the findings are limited in scope and do not constitute a general safety guarantee or explain the underlying mechanism. The paper leaves open the key question of which additional conditions or task properties convert applicability mismatch into observable, memory-driven error, highlighting the need for further systematic exploration of when procedural memory becomes behaviorally disruptive.

Abstract: Procedural memory lets language agents reuse successful routines, but reuse presumes that a stored routine remains applicable. We study what happens when that presumption is deliberately violated. The study combines a retrospective, human-assisted interface-adaptation case from BrowserGym TimeWarp with controlled frozen-memory comparisons on synthetic shopping decisions. During the documented WebShop V1-V6 development path, interface-specific code was adapted while the separately stored high-level procedure was not reported to change; this phase does not constitute an autonomous memory-agent evaluation. In the controlled phase, an early pilot produced one task on which two memory conditions selected a more expensive item while the no-memory condition selected the reference minimum. Follow-up probes did not establish a recurring row-order or identity-binding pattern. We then tested four forms of mismatch: changed quantities, a different evidence representation, a conflict between local and global optimization, and distributed promotion evidence, across 32 formal cells. Each cell used one temperature-0 generation with the same local qwen3:8b configuration and no adaptive retry. Across these pairs, none of the predefined diagnostic interference signatures appeared on the tasks for which they were defined when current-task evidence was explicit and sufficient. The result identifies a tested region of non-interference: a procedural memory can be mismatched without becoming behaviorally disruptive. It does not establish general safety or a mechanism. The remaining question is which additional conditions turn applicability mismatch into observable, memory-caused error.

</details>


### [93] [Proof-Carrying Cognition: Closing the Verification Gap with Reality-Settled Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09776)
*Eshwar Reddy M,Sourav Karmakar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how the quality of verifiers limits progress in RL-trained reasoning LLMs, provides theory and empirical evidence for verifier failure under optimization pressure, proposes a proof-carrying cognition paradigm to close the verification gap, and introduces a benchmark metric called Soundness-under-Pressure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent advances in reasoning-focused language models rely heavily on reinforcement learning over reasoning traces, but these gains are mostly confined to domains where we have cheap, reliable verifiers (e.g., code execution, formal math). For open-ended reasoning, we lack scalable and trustworthy reward signals; weak verifiers get exploited as optimization pressure increases, leading to reward hacking and overoptimization. The paper is motivated by understanding and quantifying this verification gap, explaining how verifier quality trades off with test-time compute, and designing methods and benchmarks to make reasoning training robust in less formal domains.

Method: The paper combines: (1) A theoretical analysis using a joint-Gaussian best-of-N selection model that relates verifier-gold correlation to compute–capability tradeoffs and quantifies penalties from unsound verifiers; they extend this with a copula-based model to better match real LLM judge behavior. (2) Empirical demonstrations on program-synthesis tasks with executable ground truth, including pre-registered, scaled replications. They compare frozen and reality-anchored (settled) verifiers, measure Soundness-under-Pressure as N increases, and examine hacking gaps and label efficiency. They also run GRPO-style RL experiments to trace overoptimization curves under frozen vs periodically refit reward models. (3) A conceptual and algorithmic paradigm, proof-carrying cognition, where each reasoning step is a probabilistic claim whose quality is priced by a self-built world model trained only on held-out reality and evaluated with proper scoring rules. (4) Benchmark design specifying Soundness-under-Pressure as a central metric for a reality-settled reasoning benchmark.

Result: The theoretical model shows that verifier-gold correlation acts as an exact exchange rate between test-time sampling (best-of-N) and capability, with unsound verifiers suffering a polynomial penalty in N^(1/rho^2). The copula-based extension fits actual LLM-judge soundness to within about 4% median error. Empirically, in program synthesis with executable ground truth, unsound verifiers lose Soundness-under-Pressure as optimization strength (N) increases, while sound verifiers continue to improve. Reality-anchored settlement significantly reduces or eliminates the hacking gap and yields log-linear scaling of soundness with the number of settled labels, with on-policy settlement being roughly 10x more label-efficient than random labeling. Real LLM judges display decreasing soundness under best-of-N for weaker judges and better robustness for stronger ones, and selection alone can create sizable hacking gaps from honest samples. In GRPO training, a frozen reward model leads to severe reward overoptimization and collapse of executed reward, whereas periodically refitting the model on a small stream of settled labels retains much more true performance (about sixfold higher executed reward).

Conclusion: Verifier quality is the central bottleneck for scaling reasoning via RL in language models; unsound or static verifiers are systematically exploited under optimization pressure, leading to reward hacking and overoptimization. The paper concludes that reality-anchored, continually-settled verifiers are necessary, and that soundness must be measured explicitly under increasing optimization pressure. It advocates for proof-carrying cognition as a general framework for building such systems, where reasoning steps are backed by probabilistic evidence evaluated against held-out reality with proper scoring rules. Finally, it proposes Soundness-under-Pressure as a key benchmark metric to guide future research toward robust, trustworthy reasoning systems outside of formally verifiable domains.

Abstract: Frontier gains in language-model reasoning come from reinforcement learning on reasoning traces and are concentrated in domains with a cheap, sound verifier. We argue the field's binding constraint is the verification gap: no scalable, incorruptible reward for reasoning outside formal domains. We make four contributions. (1) Theory: in a joint-Gaussian model of best-of-N selection, verifier-gold correlation rho is the exact exchange rate between test-time compute and capability, and an unsound verifier pays a polynomial penalty N^(1/rho^2); a margin-free copula form predicts realized soundness of real LLM judges to 4% median error. (2) Demonstration: in program-synthesis testbeds with executable ground truth, including a pre-registered scaled replication, unsound verifiers lose Soundness-under-Pressure as optimization grows (0.94 to 0.32 at N=4096) while a sound verifier improves monotonically; reality-anchored settlement beats a frozen verifier under i.i.d. and adversarial pressure, driving the hacking gap from ~0.27 to ~0; soundness scales log-linearly with settled labels, with on-policy settlement ~10x more label-efficient than random labeling. With real LLM judges and unit-test execution as gold, a weak judge loses soundness under best-of-N (p<0.001), a stronger judge is more robust, and selection alone manufactures +0.53 hacking gaps from honest samples. Under real GRPO training, a frozen reward model traces the full overoptimization curve (executed reward collapses 90%) while the same model refit on a 10% settlement stream preserves 6x the executed reward. (3) Paradigm: proof-carrying cognition, where reasoning steps are typed probabilistic claims priced by a self-built world model trained only on held-out reality and settled by proper scoring rules. (4) Benchmark: we specify Soundness-under-Pressure as the headline metric for a reality-settled reasoning benchmark.

</details>


### [94] [UnitBoost: Managing Compound LLM Systems with a Merge Operator, Not a Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09815)
*Xing Zhang,Guanghui Wang,Yanwei Cui,Mengdie Flora Wang,Peiyang He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes UnitBoost, a non-generative manager for compound LLM systems that assembles outputs using a deterministic meta-operator instead of a higher-level LLM, yielding more interpretable control and better performance on several benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing compound LLM systems typically rely on a higher-level LLM manager to coordinate worker models: reading their outputs, composing the final answer, scheduling further calls, and deciding when to stop. This makes the system expressive but also opaque, order-sensitive, and difficult to reason about or guarantee performance for. The authors question whether this managerial role truly requires a generative LLM and seek a more transparent, controllable alternative with theoretical guarantees.

Method: The authors introduce UnitBoost, which replaces the generative manager with a defined meta-level operator. First, a task-specific unit map converts worker outputs into slot-value proposals. Then, a constrained argmax over an admission score selects the best value per slot, assembling a composite output while tracking provenance. Any slots that remain unfilled or have insufficient support are placed into an explicit residual set for subsequent rounds of reasoning. This procedure is order-invariant, supports coupling constraints between units, and enables analysis of when additional rounds can be productive. They compare UnitBoost against generative managers and oracle single-candidate selection across several benchmarks and compound-system configurations.

Result: On three held-out benchmarks, UnitBoost’s unit-wise maximization exceeds the best single complete candidate (selected with access to gold labels) by 0.060–0.195 absolute task-score points, and outperforms input-matched generative managers by 0.048–0.076. When only the management step is replaced with UnitBoost in six existing compound systems, performance improves by 0.013–0.182. For FanOutQA, using residual-directed rounds increases cell F1 from 0.4778 to 0.5524. Control experiments show that using the true residual as a target is better than random residuals or simple rereading, and a label-free supply signal can detect when additional rounds are no longer useful.

Conclusion: A generative LLM manager is not strictly necessary for coordinating compound LLM systems. By treating outputs as units and using a deterministic, order-invariant meta-operator (UnitBoost) to select and combine them, one can improve performance, gain unit-level provenance, and obtain clear conditions under which additional computation is or is not beneficial. The tradeoff is reduced semantic flexibility in the manager, but in return the system gains transparency, robustness to call ordering, and analytically testable failure modes. The authors also characterize settings where UnitBoost offers no advantage, such as when tasks lack decomposable units, unit identities cannot be tracked, or costs scale with each emitted unit.

Abstract: Compound LLM systems often solve a coordination problem by adding a higher-level LLM. The resulting meta-agent reads workers' outputs, writes the final answer, allocates later calls, and decides when to stop. It is expressive, but it also concentrates three control decisions in an opaque, order-sensitive model call. We ask whether the manager needs to be generative at all. UnitBoost replaces that model with a defined meta-level operator: a task-given unit map turns worker outputs into slot-value proposals, a constrained argmax assembles the output, and the slots left unfilled or unsupported become an explicit residual for the next round. The operator is order-free, records unit provenance, and gives a simple guarantee: without coupling constraints, unit-wise maximization under the same admission score dominates selection of any complete candidate. On three held-out benchmarks, it exceeds the best single candidate chosen with gold labels by 0.060-0.195 absolute task-score points and input-matched generative managers by 0.048-0.076. Replacing only the management step improves six compound-system configurations by 0.013-0.182. Residual-directed rounds raise FanOutQA cell F1 from 0.4778 to 0.5524; matched controls show that the true residual outperforms random targets and ordinary rereading, while a label-free supply signal flags exhaustion after one unproductive round. The same analysis measures three conditions in which no such gain is available (one indivisible unit, unavailable unit identity, and an endpoint that charges for every emitted unit) and quantifies cross-unit coupling as a repair cost. The manager gives up semantic freedom and gains order invariance, unit provenance, and testable failure conditions.

</details>


### [95] [The Era by Eon Benchmark: A Generated Enterprise Estate with Exact Ground Truth for Benchmarking LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09853)
*Benjamin Gruenbaum,Doron Porat,Assaf Natanzon,Roy Zavida,Chen Dinachi,Or Itzahary*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Era by Eon Benchmark, a synthetic yet realistic evaluation framework for LLM agents that interact with enterprise tools, providing exact ground-truth grading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating LLM agents that operate over enterprise systems of record is difficult because real customer production data are private and cannot be used, and there is no existing high-fidelity substitute with reliable ground truth. The authors aim to create a realistic, consistent, and fully labeled synthetic environment in which such agents can be rigorously benchmarked.

Method: They construct a benchmark around complete fictional companies, parameterized by industry, size, business model, application portfolio, and a random seed. A seeded entity graph defines shared company data, which is consumed by simulators for tools like Salesforce, Zendesk, Slack, Gong, etc. A question-conditioned generator then creates schemas and records for internal databases, drawing entities, keys, and values from the same graph to ensure consistency. Expected answers to benchmark questions are computed directly from the final records, enabling exact grading. They further apply design and answer-key checks to validate internal databases, and use a realism scorecard plus an adversarial detector to validate the entity graph. They generate 23 such companies and run multiple LLMs on a simulator track with repeated questions to estimate accuracy and compare models.

Result: Across 23 generated companies, the mean realism score of the synthetic enterprise data improved from 61.8 to 97.0, with no records flagged as synthetic by their detector. In a simulator-track benchmark using 33 questions, each asked three times, nine different models achieved accuracy estimates between 42.4% and 76.8%. After statistical correction for multiple comparisons, three out of 36 pairwise accuracy differences between models remained significant.

Conclusion: The Era by Eon Benchmark provides a realistic, internally consistent, and fully labeled synthetic environment for evaluating enterprise LLM agents that use tools. Its generation pipeline yields highly realistic data with strong anti-synthetic signals, and its exact answer computation enables reliable grading and statistically meaningful model comparisons. This benchmark can serve as a standardized way to compare and improve LLM agents for enterprise tool use without relying on sensitive production data.

Abstract: LLM agents for enterprise systems of record cannot be evaluated on customer production data, and no existing substitute provides ground truth. We present the Era by Eon Benchmark for evaluating LLM agents that use enterprise tools. The benchmark is built around a complete fictional company. It includes product simulators, company-specific internal databases, benchmark questions, and computed answer keys. Industry, company size, business model, application portfolio, and a seed define each company. One seeded entity graph supplies shared company data to simulators of Salesforce, Zendesk, Slack, Gong, and other products. A questionconditioned generator creates the schemas and records for internal databases. It takes shared entities, keys, and values from the same graph before generating database-specific facts. Both mechanisms therefore describe one consistent enterprise estate. Every expected answer is computed from the final records, so grading is exact. Design and answer-key checks validate the internal databases. A realism scorecard and adversarial detector validate the entity graph. Across 23 generated companies, the mean realism score rose from 61.8 to 97.0, with zero records flagged as synthetic. In the reported simulator-track comparison, nine models answered the same 33 questions three times each. Accuracy estimates ranged from 42.4% to 76.8%, and three of 36 pairwise differences remained supported after correction.

</details>


### [96] [Shifting Relational Paradigms for Affective Computing: Affective Resonance, Vitality Affects, and Vocal Interaction Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09864)
*Cy Gorman,Yihang Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They shift affective computing from labeling solo speaker emotions to modeling relational affective dynamics via vocal coupling in conversation, and demonstrate a preliminary method using self-supervised speech features and directional coupling analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard affective computing treats emotions as individual, static states, which misses how affect emerges in and through interaction between people. The authors want a framework that captures affect as a relational, dynamic phenomenon (affective resonance) in multi-party conversation.

Method: Conceptual: Propose a relational affect framework centered on interactional fields, affective resonance, and vitality contours. Empirical: Use continuous self-supervised speech representations in multi-party conversation to estimate directional expressive coupling at sub-second scales, compare across interaction regimes, and validate with exclusive-speech negative controls and null-calibrated coupling analyses.

Result: They find measurable directional expressive coupling in multi-party conversations, which is regime-specific, strongest at sub-second timescales, and disappears under exclusive-speech negative controls, supporting their relational account. They also show that self-supervised speech representations are suitable for capturing these dynamics.

Conclusion: Affective phenomena in interaction are better modeled as properties of an interactional field—relational vocal dynamics—rather than just individual emotional states. Their preliminary coupling analysis and design frameworks for "Artificial Affective Resonance Intelligence" and "Affective Resonance Dynamic Ontologies" illustrate how to build computational systems grounded in this relational, dynamic view of affect.

Abstract: Affective computing has largely followed an individual-state paradigm, extracting discrete emotion labels or arousal/valence from isolated speakers. We argue this framing is incomplete for interaction. Drawing on affective resonance and vitality-contour accounts, we propose a relational framework in which the primary unit of affective analysis is the interactional field constituted within vocal dynamics. As a proof of concept, we present a preliminary empirical study using continuous self-supervised speech representations to detect directional expressive coupling in multi-party conversation. Coupling is regime-specific, concentrated at sub-second timescales, and collapses under exclusive-speech negative controls, consistent with a relational account of affective dynamics. We introduce design frameworks for Artificial Affective Resonance Intelligence grounded in Affective Resonance Dynamic Ontologies, supported by null-calibrated directional coupling analyses across interaction regimes.

</details>


### [97] [AgentAudit: An Open, Extensible Framework for Full-Lifecycle Trust Evaluation of AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09875)
*Shrey Nag,Sachita,Abhishek Kumar Singh,Lipi Goel,Rajeshwar Singh Janwar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentAudit is a trace-based evaluation framework that diagnoses where AI agents fail across the full pipeline instead of just checking success or robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing agent benchmarks only measure one aspect, like task success or security, and treat agents as black boxes, so they can’t tell at which step—planning, tool use, memory, reasoning—things go wrong or how trustworthy the agent’s behaviour is on adversarial tasks.

Method: Introduce AgentAudit, which attaches non-invasively to any LLM-based agent, reads its full execution trace, and scores each step along ten dimensions (instruction integrity, planner, memory, tool selection/invocation/correctness, alignment, tool faithfulness, security, execution integrity), plus behavioural labels and failure attribution; then apply it to five LLMs on nine capability and adversarial tasks using a single judge model.

Result: Claude Sonnet 5 and GPT-5 achieve the highest Composite Trust Scores (≈95 and 81), while Sarvam 105B, Llama 3.3 70B and Gemini 2.5 Flash score much lower (≈58, 46, 23); AgentAudit also reveals that some non-frontier models often show Unsafe_Compliance on adversarial tasks even when task-level performance looks similar, something standard pass/fail benchmarks miss; the use of a single judge model (one of the evaluated models) is flagged as a limitation.

Conclusion: Trace-level, multi-dimensional auditing exposes important differences in agent trustworthiness and failure modes that simple task-completion or robustness benchmarks overlook, but reliance on a single in-distribution judge model remains an open limitation.

Abstract: Existing evaluation frameworks mostly assess only one part of AI agents, such as task completion (AgentBench) or security robustness (AgentDojo, ASB), rather than the complete pipeline of planning, tool selection, tool execution, memory and reasoning. Failures can occur at any stage, yet existing benchmarks rarely identify their precise source. AgentAudit evaluates the entire execution trace across ten capability, grounding, security and behavioural dimensions, namely instruction integrity, planner, memory, tool selection, tool invocation, tool correctness, alignment, tool faithfulness, security and execution integrity, combined with behavioural classification and failure attribution to pinpoint the exact stage responsible for an observed failure. AgentAudit can evaluate any LLM-based AI agent, since it attaches to the agent instead of replacing it. It reads only the recorded execution trace and does not interfere with how the agent runs, so it places no constraint on the agent's internal implementation. We evaluate five language models (OpenAI GPT-5, Claude Sonnet 5, Sarvam 105B, Llama 3.3 70B and Gemini 2.5 Flash) across nine capability and adversarial tasks. Claude Sonnet 5 and GPT-5 obtain the highest mean Composite Trust Scores (95.1 and 80.6 out of 100, respectively), while Sarvam 105B, Llama 3.3 70B and Gemini 2.5 Flash trail substantially (57.6, 45.7 and 22.6). All traces were scored by a single fixed judge model, which was itself one of the evaluated models, a limitation discussed in Section VII.E. More importantly, models with similar task-completion behaviour can diverge sharply in trustworthiness, as several non-frontier models are repeatedly classified Unsafe_Compliance on adversarial tasks rather than merely failing them, a distinction that pass/fail benchmarks cannot surface.

</details>


### [98] [Scored vs. Generated Readouts in Behavioral Language Models: An Empirical Study of Elicitation Format](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09882)
*Touchapon Kraisingkorn,Krittin Pachtrachai,Wachiravit Modecrua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper compares different ways of reading out predictions from fine-tuned language models and finds that directly scoring answer tokens usually ranks outcomes better than using probabilities derived from generated rationales, while an elicited probability readout can improve calibration in some cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although language models can both predict customer-related outcomes and generate natural language explanations, practitioners often treat different prediction readouts (token scores, rationale-based verdicts, explicit probabilities) as interchangeable. The authors aim to rigorously test whether these readouts are actually equivalent in quality, particularly for ranking and calibration in practical retail prediction tasks.

Method: The authors hold the model checkpoint and prompt content fixed and systematically compare three readout modes across 13 model-domain cells over four retail prediction tasks in three markets. (1) Scored readout: using token-level probabilities for answers. (2) Rationale readout: using predictions obtained after the model generates a written rationale. (3) Elicited probability readout: asking the model for a probability estimate before giving a verdict. They evaluate performance using AUC for ranking and Brier score for calibration, perform a two-sided sign test across cells, and use paired bootstrap confidence intervals. They also analyze around 9,000 generated rationales to uncover correlates of performance differences, such as reliance on dominant features and convergence to stock phrases, and examine the role of supervision format and train–serve mismatch.

Result: Across the 13 model-domain cells, the scored readout outperforms the rationale-based readout in 12 of 13 cases, with AUC gains of 1.5–14.5 points and statistically significant differences (sign test p≈0.003; bootstrap CIs excluding zero). The performance gap depends on supervision and formatting, varying from −2.2 AUC for an untuned base model to +13.7 for models supervised with rationale-format labels. Rationale analysis shows these explanations tend to underuse the most predictive feature and converge on generic formulations. The elicited probability readout greatly improves calibration in one setting (Brier score from 0.47 to 0.15) and ranks outcomes similarly to the scored readout when training covers the outcome rate range, but it underperforms when the scored head is already well calibrated or when extrapolating beyond trained outcome frequencies.

Conclusion: Different readout mechanisms from the same fine-tuned language model are not interchangeable: scoring answer tokens generally yields better ranking performance, while rationale-based predictions can degrade both ranking and dependence on key features. Explicit probability elicitation can improve calibration but is sensitive to the training distribution. The authors recommend using the scored head for ranking while still generating rationales for interpretability and highlight training and supervision choices that can reduce performance gaps between readouts.

Abstract: Language models fine-tuned on customer behavior can predict outcomes and generate explanations, but these readouts are often treated as interchangeable. Holding model checkpoint and prompt content fixed, we compare probabilities obtained by scoring answer tokens with predictions generated after a written rationale. Across 13 model-domain cells covering four retail tasks in three markets, including two using fully public data and checkpoints, the scored readout ranks outcomes more accurately in 12 of 13 cells (two-sided sign test, p approximately 0.003), by 1.5 to 14.5 points in area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC). Paired bootstrap confidence intervals exclude zero in every newly measured cell. The gap varies with task-specific supervision and mismatch between training and serving formats, ranging from -2.2 points for an untuned base model to +13.7 for rationale-format supervision. Analysis of approximately 9,000 rationales identifies two correlates: reduced reliance on the dominant predictive feature and convergence on stock formulations. Probability saturation does not track the gap. A third readout, eliciting a probability before any verdict, improves calibration (Brier score from 0.47 to 0.15) while ranking within noise of scoring, but only for outcome rates represented in training; it is worse than scoring when the scored head is already calibrated. We interpret these differences through the objectives matched by each readout, identify training choices that narrow the gap, and propose retaining generated rationales while sourcing ranking from the scored head.

</details>


### [99] [Decision Transformer for UAV-Mounted RIS-Assisted Dynamic D2D Communications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09885)
*Yaxuan Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a learning-based framework to jointly optimize UAV trajectory/attitude and RIS phase shifts for D2D communication, achieving strong cross-scenario generalization via Decision Transformer with expert demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RIS-assisted UAV communications can enhance D2D links, but realistic issues like UAV motion/attitude dynamics, time-varying channels, angle-dependent RIS reflection, and stochastic link activation are complex to model and optimize jointly, especially under mobility, energy, and hardware constraints. Existing DRL methods often overfit to a single scenario and transfer poorly across environments.

Method: Model a UAV-mounted RIS-assisted D2D network with stochastic link activation, time-varying Rician fading, and angle-dependent RIS gain. Formulate a long-term average sum-rate maximization under UAV mobility, energy budget, and RIS hardware constraints via joint optimization of UAV trajectory, UAV attitude, and RIS phase shifts. Solve this high-dimensional sequential decision problem using a Decision Transformer (sequence modeling RL) that is trained on expert trajectories generated by DRL across multiple scenarios, enabling offline learning and zero-shot transfer; optionally apply limited online fine-tuning in new scenarios.

Result: The proposed Decision Transformer-based policy trained on multi-scenario expert data can directly generalize to unseen environments (zero-shot), achieving significantly better performance than directly transferring DRL policies trained in source scenarios. With modest online fine-tuning, its performance approaches or matches that of scenario-specific DRL with far fewer online interactions, indicating improved sample efficiency and robustness.

Conclusion: A UAV-mounted RIS-assisted D2D communication system with realistic dynamics can be effectively controlled via a Decision Transformer trained on expert demonstrations, enabling joint optimization of UAV trajectory, attitude, and RIS phases. This approach offers strong cross-scenario generalization, superior zero-shot transfer compared to conventional DRL, and competitive performance with substantially reduced fine-tuning data, making it attractive for practical deployment in dynamically changing wireless environments.

Abstract: This paper studies unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV)-mouted reconfigurable intelligent surface (RIS)-assisted device-to-device (D2D) communication with stochastic link activation. It models UAV motion and attitude, time-varying Rician angles, and angle-dependent RIS reflection. A joint optimization of UAV trajectory, attitude, and RIS phases is formulated to maximize average sum rate under mobility, energy, and hardware constraints. The problem is addressed using deep reinforcement learning and a Decision Transformer trained on expert trajectories from multiple scenarios. Results demonstrate effective cross-scenario generalization, with zero-shot transfer outperforming direct DRL transfer and online fine-tuning achieving competitive performance with fewer interactions.

</details>


### [100] [Grounded Evaluation and Repair for NL-to-PDDL Problem Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09898)
*Joana Rosa,Pedro Santos,Valdemar Oliveira,Romão Silva,L. Miguel Silveira,Bruno Martins*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build and evaluate an end-to-end pipeline that uses LLMs, automatic checks, and iterative repair to translate natural language planning descriptions into faithful PDDL problem instances, showing that simple success metrics can be misleading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work shows LLMs can generate PDDL problems from natural language, but common evaluation metrics—like whether the output parses, whether a planner can solve it, or overall syntactic validity—do not guarantee that the generated problem is actually faithful to the original natural language description. A problem might be solvable yet encode a different initial state, goals, or objects than intended. The authors want to understand and improve the reliability and faithfulness of NL-to-PDDL translation beyond these coarse operational metrics.

Method: They design an end-to-end NL-to-PDDL pipeline that starts with LLM-generated PDDL problems and then subjects them to multiple layers of checking and repair. The pipeline includes: (1) basic checks such as PDDL parsing, planning, and plan validation; (2) a domain-conformance checker to ensure consistency with the domain definition; (3) an LLM-based critic that examines mismatches; and (4) iterative repair loops where the generator revises the PDDL using fine-grained feedback. This feedback is built from the domain description, the generated problem, the natural language description, and operational diagnostics. For evaluation, they perform offline, reference-based comparisons against curated benchmark PDDL problems, using renaming-invariant structural matching and, where supported, semantic equivalence tests. They test on Planetarium, AutoPlanBench, and curated PDDL 2.1 benchmarks.

Result: They find that metrics based solely on operational success (e.g., parsability and planner solvability) can differ substantially from metrics based on how well the generated PDDL matches the benchmark reference problems at a structural or semantic level. In other words, an instance may pass operational checks yet still inaccurately encode the task described in natural language. They also observe that structured, feedback-driven repair processes can improve performance, but that accurately reconstructing reference problems, particularly in PDDL 2.1 domains with durative and numeric features, remains difficult.

Conclusion: Faithful NL-to-PDDL translation cannot be reliably judged by syntactic validity or planner success alone; more nuanced structural and semantic evaluations are necessary. The proposed pipeline, integrating LLM generation with multiple automatic checks, an LLM critic, and iterative repair, provides a more robust approach and shows benefits from structured repair. However, there is still a significant gap in faithfully reconstructing complex PDDL 2.1 benchmarks, indicating that NL-to-PDDL remains a challenging open problem.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promise for translating Natural Language (NL) planning descriptions into PDDL problem instances. However, standard evaluation criteria such as syntactic validity or planner success can substantially overestimate faithfulness to the described task: a generated problem may be parseable and solvable while misrepresenting the intended initial state, goal, object structure, or optimization target. This paper studies an end-to-end NL-to-PDDL pipeline that combines LLM generation, checks in terms of PDDL parsing, planning and validation, a domain-conformance checker, an LLM critic, and iterative repair. Fine-grained repair feedback is constructed from the domain description, the generated problem, the natural language problem description, and operational diagnostics. Reference-based comparisons against curated benchmark PDDL problem descriptions are used for post-hoc benchmark analysis, and these offline checks include renaming-invariant structural matching and semantic equivalence, where domain support is available. Across Planetarium, AutoPlanBench, and curated PDDL~2.1 problems, results show that operational success and benchmark-reference reconstruction can diverge substantially. Results also show that structured repair can be useful, and that PDDL~2.1 remains challenging for reference reconstruction, even when operational success improves.

</details>


### [101] [Time-Frequency Geometric Cross-Attention for Chunked Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09925)
*Shengye Dong,Haochen Niu,Hao Liu,Peiwen Lin,Chuang Wang,Shanmin Pang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They identify missing motion structure in current vision-language-action policies and propose a new time-frequency geometric cross-attention module that better captures multiscale and cross-phase motion relationships, significantly improving performance and robustness, especially out-of-distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLA policies output short action chunks but internally represent them as generic per-timestep tokens decoded linearly, which fails to exploit two key motion structures: (1) multi-scale temporal behavior where smooth global trends and fine corrective motions are entangled in a single token, and (2) geometric relationships across different motion phases (reach, contact, adjustment, settling) that are nearly orthogonal in representation space and thus poorly captured by standard dot-product attention. This leads to suboptimal exploitation of motion regularities and weaker generalization, particularly OOD.

Method: They introduce Time-Frequency Geometric Cross-Attention (TFGCA), a drop-in module for VLA policies. First, a learnable per-dimension stationary wavelet transform decomposes each action chunk into time-frequency tokens. Then, each time token attends to these time-frequency tokens via a cross-attention mechanism whose score combines dot-product similarity with a wedge-product magnitude term that is sensitive to near-orthogonal relationships, with a learnable weighting between the two. The module is inserted as a zero-initialized residual, reproducing the base model’s behavior at start so it can be added to pretrained VLAs and then fine-tuned end-to-end.

Result: When added to the same base VLA models, TFGCA improves success rates across benchmarks: +1.5 average on in-distribution LIBERO, +6.3 on OOD LIBERO-Plus, +28.5 in randomized settings under RoboTwin domain randomization, and +11.67 percentage points on three real-robot AgiBot A2 tasks. Gains are larger in out-of-distribution and randomized conditions, indicating better robustness and generalization.

Conclusion: Encoding action chunks with a time-frequency decomposition and a geometric cross-attention that is sensitive to both similarity and near-orthogonality provides a substantially better inductive bias for VLA policies than plain per-timestep tokens with dot-product attention. This structural prior helps capture multi-scale temporal patterns and cross-phase motion geometry, yielding consistent and especially strong out-of-distribution improvements, and can be integrated as a simple residual module onto existing pretrained VLAs.

Abstract: Modern vision-language-action (VLA) policies predict a whole chunk of actions: one to two seconds of coordinated motion emitted in a single forward pass. Yet an action chunk is essentially a short multivariate trajectory, but inside these models it is a sequence of generic per-timestep hidden tokens decoded by a linear head. This under-serves two motion structures. First, frequency: a chunk superimposes a smooth global trend and fine corrective motion across time scales, and a single token entangles them. Second, cross-phase geometry: motions of different phases (reach, contact, grasp adjustment, settling) unfold along very different, near-orthogonal directions in representation space, yet are tightly related for the task and arise across the time axis. Dot-product attention scores alignment by an inner product, so it favors aligned tokens and is least sensitive near orthogonality, leaving such relationships for the network to recover through a detour.
  We introduce Time-Frequency Geometric Cross-Attention (TFGCA), a drop-in module repairing both blind spots. TFGCA uses a per-dimension learnable stationary wavelet transform to decompose the action chunk into time-frequency tokens, and each time token retrieves information from them via a cross-attention that fuses the dot product (similarity) with the wedge-product magnitude (sensitive to near-orthogonality) through a learnable weight. A zero-initialized residual reproduces the base behavior at initialization, so it can be dropped onto a pretrained VLA and fine-tuned jointly. Relative to the same-source base, TFGCA improves in-distribution LIBERO by +1.5 on average, the OOD LIBERO-Plus by +6.3, the randomized average under RoboTwin domain randomization by +28.5, and the overall success rate on three real-robot AgiBot A2 tasks by +11.67 points, with larger gains out of distribution.

</details>


### [102] [Structural Process Supervision for Latent Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09928)
