*Yiqi Li,Xu Chen,Chen Ju,Jiangchao Yao,Zhaoyang Li,Jinsong Lan,Xiaoyong Zhu,Bo Zheng,Yu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Prototype-Mediated Process Supervision (PMPS), a method for supervising latent reasoning representations using learnable prototypes and progressive alignment, achieving shorter outputs and better accuracy than prior latent reasoning and even some explicit CoT methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latent reasoning aims to replace long chain-of-thought token sequences with compact continuous embeddings to improve efficiency and robustness. However, current latent reasoning methods lack direct process-level supervision over these latent embeddings, which leads to issues such as representation collapse and uneven information distribution across the latent sequence. The motivation is to provide structured, process-aware supervision that preserves reasoning quality while keeping the reasoning latent and compact.

Method: The method introduces Prototype-Mediated Process Supervision (PMPS), which uses learnable reasoning prototypes as semantic anchors. Both latent reasoning embeddings and explicit CoT token embeddings are projected into a shared prototype space. A many-to-many, soft assignment of embeddings to prototypes creates an indirect alignment between latent and explicit reasoning processes, even when their lengths differ. Additionally, a Progressive Sequential Alignment (PSA) module imposes positional priors early in training to encourage roughly sequential alignment, and these priors are gradually relaxed so that the model can learn more flexible, adaptive matching patterns later on.

Result: On GSM8K-Aug, PMPS reduces the length of generated outputs to less than half of explicit CoT while improving accuracy. It yields an average accuracy gain of 2.08% over SIM-CoT (a strong latent reasoning baseline) across various model families. When applied to GPT-2, PMPS even outperforms CoT-SFT, a supervised fine-tuning method that uses explicit CoT. On larger models and harder tasks, PMPS maintains the best accuracy among latent reasoning methods with similar output lengths.

Conclusion: Prototype-mediated process supervision with progressive sequential alignment offers an effective way to provide structural, process-level guidance to latent reasoning representations. This reduces token-level reasoning overhead while improving or maintaining accuracy, avoiding representation collapse and uneven information distribution. PMPS sets a new state of the art among latent reasoning approaches with comparable output lengths and can outperform even explicit CoT supervision in some settings.

Abstract: Latent reasoning approaches enhance token-level efficiency and robustness by replacing verbose, explicit chain-of-thought (CoT) tokens with compact continuous-space embeddings. However, existing methods lack direct process supervision over these latent embeddings, which often leads to representation collapse and uneven information distribution. To address this, we propose Prototype-Mediated Process Supervision (PMPS), which introduces learnable reasoning prototypes as semantic anchors to provide structural process-level supervision for latent reasoning. PMPS projects latent embeddings and explicit CoT embeddings into a shared prototype space, achieving many-to-many soft alignment between unequal-length representations through prototype assignment. Meanwhile, we introduce a Progressive Sequential Alignment (PSA) module to further guide training: positional priors initially encourage sequential alignment structure, then gradually relax to permit adaptive matching. Experimental results show that PMPS compresses output token length to under 50% of explicit CoT on GSM8K-Aug. Compared to leading baseline SIM-CoT, our method achieves average accuracy gains of 2.08% across different model families. On GPT-2, PMPS even surpasses CoT-SFT. On larger models and a more challenging task, PMPS consistently attains the highest accuracy among all latent reasoning methods with comparable output length.

</details>


### [103] [Belief-State Engine: Augmenting LLMs for Principled Planning Under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10036)
*Arnab Chattopadhayay,Debdipta Halder*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They identify a core structural flaw in typical LLM agents in partially observable environments and fix it by adding an external Bayesian belief-state engine that feeds only a posterior over states to the LLM, yielding more optimal and consistent decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard LLM agents condition directly on the full history of actions and observations. In partially observable settings, this leads to systematic failures: premature commitment to hypotheses, overreaction to single observations, and policy drift as histories grow. These problems stem from lacking an explicit, principled representation of uncertainty over hidden states. The paper is motivated by the need to give LLM agents a sound belief state, so that their decisions in POMDPs can enjoy the theoretical guarantees of classical planning under uncertainty.

Method: They introduce the Belief-State Engine (BSE), an external inference module that maintains a Bayesian posterior over latent states of a known POMDP model. At each step, instead of giving the LLM the raw action-observation history, the system feeds only the current posterior (belief state) to the LLM, which then selects an action. They formalize four axioms that any internally consistent belief representation must satisfy and prove that an LLM coupled with the BSE implements a Markov policy over the belief MDP, inheriting Bellman-style optimality guarantees if the LLM never accesses the raw history. Empirically, they implement this architecture and compare it with several baselines across benchmark POMDP tasks.

Result: On the Tiger POMDP and a red-team attack-graph task, the BSE-augmented LLM agent outperforms six baselines: a purely reactive LLM, Chain-of-Thought prompting, ReAct, a natural-language belief tracker, QMDP, and POMCP. The improvements are seen in cumulative task return, calibration of beliefs about hidden states, and the consistency of decisions across runs. Ten ablation studies show which design components matter and demonstrate that the benefits are robust and not tied to a particular underlying LLM.

Conclusion: Embedding LLM agents in a principled POMDP framework by externalizing Bayesian belief tracking corrects structural weaknesses that arise from naively conditioning on full histories. The Belief-State Engine yields agents whose policies are Markov in belief space and can, in principle, achieve Bellman-optimal behavior, while empirically delivering more accurate beliefs and more reliable decisions across tasks. This suggests that pairing LLMs with explicit probabilistic state estimators is a powerful pattern for building robust decision-making agents in partially observable environments.

Abstract: Large language model agents produce fluent action sequences across a wide range of tasks, yet they fail in characteristic ways once the environment becomes partially observable. Ambiguous feedback pushes them into premature commitments. A single informative observation can collapse their uncertainty onto the wrong hypothesis. Policies drift as the history grows. We trace these symptoms to a common structural cause. An LLM agent, as commonly deployed, is a history-conditioned policy with no explicit belief over hidden state.
  We propose an architectural fix. The Belief-State Engine (BSE) is an inference module placed outside the LLM. It maintains a Bayesian posterior over the latent states of a given POMDP (Partially Observable Markov Decision Process) model, and at each decision step it exposes only that posterior to the LLM. The raw action-observation log is not shown. We set out a minimal four-axiom specification of what a belief-consistent internal state must satisfy, and prove that the LLM paired with the BSE is a sound Markov policy on the belief MDP induced by the underlying POMDP. It therefore inherits the Bellman optimality guarantees of classical POMDP theory, provided the LLM is never exposed to the raw history.
  We evaluate the architecture on the Tiger POMDP and a red-team attack-graph task, against six baselines: a reactive LLM, Chain-of-Thought, ReAct, a natural-language belief tracker, QMDP, and POMCP. Across both domains, the BSE-augmented agent improves task return, belief calibration, and decision consistency. Ten targeted ablations isolate the contribution of each architectural choice confirms that the effect is not specific to any one model. Code, environment specifications, prompt templates, and seed logs accompany this paper.

</details>


### [104] [OntologyAligner: Ontology-Aligned Retrieval and Hierarchy-Guided Large Language Model Reranking for Biomedical Ontology Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10055)
*Jie Song,Zhichuan Xu,Ziyu Lu,Meng Xiao,Cheng Bi,Yuxin Zhang,Xin Zheng,Xiaoran Li,Qiongfang Cao,Hao Yang,Bairong Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces OntologyAligner, a three-stage framework that achieves state-of-the-art performance for mapping biomedical text to ontology concepts, and PhenoNormBench, a new benchmark for phenotype normalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical ontology normalization is essential for consistent integration and analysis of biomedical data, but is difficult due to lexical variation and fine-grained distinctions among closely related, hierarchically structured concepts. Existing methods struggle to accurately disambiguate and align free text with the correct ontology entries across different resources.

Method: The authors propose OntologyAligner, a three-stage normalization framework: (1) ontology-aligned retrieval to generate candidate concepts from the target ontology, (2) large language model (LLM)-based reranking of these candidates to prioritize the most plausible matches, and (3) selective hierarchy-guided refinement that uses the ontology’s hierarchical structure to resolve subtle distinctions and refine predictions. They also build PhenoNormBench, a unified benchmark dataset with 13,390 samples aggregated from seven Human Phenotype Ontology (HPO) resources to systematically evaluate normalization methods.

Result: OntologyAligner achieves state-of-the-art performance on Human Phenotype Ontology normalization, with 88.78% Macro Top-1 and 86.75% Micro Top-1 Accuracy, surpassing the strongest prior baseline by 4.85 and 5.07 percentage points, respectively. Ablation studies show that each of the three stages contributes complementary performance gains, and sensitivity analyses indicate robustness to different candidate set sizes and underlying model backbones. Experiments on MONDO, MEDIC, and NCBITaxon demonstrate that the approach generalizes to other biomedical ontologies beyond HPO.

Conclusion: OntologyAligner is an effective and generalizable three-stage framework for mapping biomedical free text to structured ontology concepts, delivering clear performance improvements over existing methods and remaining robust across configurations. The newly introduced PhenoNormBench benchmark, together with released code, provides a standardized resource for evaluating and comparing ontology normalization systems, particularly for phenotype-related text.

Abstract: Biomedical ontology normalization maps free-text expressions to standardized concepts, enabling consistent integration and analysis of biomedical data. This task remains challenging because lexical variation and subtle distinctions among hierarchically related concepts can obscure concept boundaries. We present OntologyAligner, a three-stage framework that combines ontology-aligned retrieval, large language model candidate reranking, and selective hierarchy-guided refinement. We also construct PhenoNormBench, a unified benchmark comprising 13,390 samples from seven Human Phenotype Ontology datasets. OntologyAligner achieved state-of-the-art performance on HPO normalization, with 88.78% Macro Top-1 Accuracy and 86.75% Micro Top-1 Accuracy, exceeding the strongest baseline by 4.85 and 5.07 percentage points, respectively. Ablation analyses showed complementary contributions from all three stages, and sensitivity analyses demonstrated stability across candidate-set sizes and model backbones. Applications to MONDO, MEDIC, and NCBITaxon further established portability to other ontologies. OntologyAligner offers a generalizable framework for accurate mapping of biomedical text to structured ontology concepts. PhenoNormBench and the code are publicly available at https://github.com/zhelishisongjie/OntologyAligner.

</details>


### [105] [Reference-Based Bias Detection in LLMs via Relative Representations of Hidden States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10060)
*Marek Jeliński,Jan Dubiński,Maciej Chrabaszcz,Sebastian Cygert*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a fast, reference-based method (ΔB) to audit bias shifts in hidden representations of language models across training variants, without relying on text outputs or task-specific benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing bias audits largely depend on model outputs and external benchmarks, which are expensive, need judge models, and may miss internal representational shifts that don't surface in generated text. The authors want a way to directly inspect how internal representations of target groups change with fine-tuning, across model checkpoints, in a scalable and benchmark-free manner.

Method: They compare related model variants (e.g., before vs. after fine-tuning) by encoding each sentence not as its raw hidden state (which is incomparable across variants) but as a vector of similarities to a fixed set of anchor sentences, placing all variants into a shared comparison space. In this space, they quantify how target groups (e.g., demographic terms) shift in association with positive vs. negative attributes, defining this as the Representational Bias Shift ΔB. They then study ΔB’s behavior across multiple model families, training paradigms (full fine-tuning vs. parameter-efficient adaptation), and bias benchmarks, and test robustness to changes in anchors, attributes, and templates. They also measure compute cost and runtime vs. standard output-level auditing.

Result: In 15 of 18 evaluated settings, ΔB strongly correlates with changes in output-level bias, reaching |r| = 0.84 (p < 0.001) under full fine-tuning, though correlations weaken and become more model-dependent under parameter-efficient methods. Using ΔB thresholds to flag checkpoints with increased bias yields ROC AUCs between 0.65 and 0.99, and on WildGuardMix and DecodingTrust it outperforms a SEAT-based baseline across all three model families. The method is robust to changes in anchor sets, attribute sets, and target templates. It requires no task-specific evaluation data and runs in about three minutes per audit, using 3–50× less compute than comparable output-based benchmarks.

Conclusion: Representational Bias Shift ΔB is an efficient, mostly reliable proxy for output-level bias changes, enabling quick, benchmark-free auditing of how fine-tuning affects internal associations in language models. It should not replace output-based audits but complements them by exposing internal representational changes that may not yet manifest clearly in generated outputs, while significantly reducing computational and data requirements.

Abstract: Existing bias auditing methods typically rely on model outputs, requiring costly benchmarks or judge models and potentially missing internal shifts that never appear in generated text. We propose a reference-based method that audits bias in hidden-state representations across related model variants, for example before and after fine-tuning. Because fine-tuning reshapes representation geometry, absolute hidden states are not directly comparable, so we encode each sentence by its similarities to a fixed set of anchor sentences, yielding relative representations in a shared comparison space. There we measure how target groups shift in their association with positive and negative attributes, a quantity we call the Representational Bias Shift $ΔB$. Across three model families and the WildGuardMix, DecodingTrust and ToxiGen benchmarks, $ΔB$ correlates with output-level bias change in 15 of the 18 settings we test, reaching $|r| = 0.84$ ($p < 0.001$) under full fine-tuning and becoming more model-dependent under parameter-efficient adaptation. Thresholding $ΔB$ detects checkpoints whose bias increased with ROC AUC between $0.65$ and $0.99$, and on WildGuardMix and DecodingTrust it separates them better than a SEAT-based baseline for all three families. $ΔB$ is also stable under changes to the anchor set, attribute sets and target templates. Our method requires no task-specific evaluation data and audits a model in about three minutes, using $3$-$50\times$ less compute than the output-level benchmarks considered here. We view it as complementary to output-based auditing rather than a replacement for it.

</details>


### [106] [RAP: Research Attention Prediction Reveals Target-Conditioned Evidence Acquisition Biases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10092)
*Yingqian Wu,Jingcong Liang,Siyuan Wang,Zhenfei Yin,Philip Torr,Junchi Yu,Zhongyu Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new benchmark, Research Attention Prediction (RAP), to test how well large language models can predict short‑term shifts in research focus across many AI/ML subfields, finding that simple statistical baselines still outperform current LLM agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as research assistants and agents, but it is unclear how well they can anticipate where research attention will move next because standard tasks like reviewing or proposing ideas lack clear, verifiable outcomes. The authors want a concrete, measurable way to test whether LLMs can track and forecast evolving research trends over time.

Method: They build RAP, a rolling benchmark over 278 AI/ML fields and 1,390 time episodes. At each time cut‑off, an LLM agent can search a time‑restricted slice of arXiv, then must predict the distribution of papers over the next six months across eight fixed research directions. They compare several diagnostic LLM setups—with and without search, with different ways of using history (State carry‑forward vs direct Forecast), and with frozen‑evidence replay—against an exponentially weighted moving average (EWMA) baseline based on exact historical counts. They also study the effect of fine‑tuning an LLM (Qwen3‑4B) on realized outcomes.

Result: Search generally improves LLM performance, but all four diagnostic LLM setups still underperform the simple EWMA baseline in compositional accuracy. When given cumulative history, using a State carry‑forward strategy beats direct Forecast for all models; analysis with frozen‑evidence replay shows that Forecast‑oriented policies tend to retrieve less recent evidence, contributing to worse performance. Even when given exact historical activity, models struggle to update in a future‑specific way: only GPT‑5.5 with search re‑enabled slightly outperforms EWMA. Fine‑tuning Qwen3‑4B on realized outcomes boosts its forecast Spearman correlation by 0.105 on held‑out fields at later time origins, with particular gains on episodes where research attention changes a lot.

Conclusion: The RAP benchmark reveals that current LLM research agents are not yet competitive with simple statistical baselines at predicting near‑term shifts in research attention across AI/ML fields, even when augmented with search and historical data. Their main limitations lie in how they use retrieved evidence and update from history to make future‑specific forecasts. Nonetheless, targeted fine‑tuning on outcome data can meaningfully improve forecasting skill, indicating that better training and retrieval strategies might eventually close the gap with strong classical baselines.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act as research agents, yet their ability to track shifts in research attention is difficult to evaluate because reviews and research ideas lack uniquely verifiable outcomes. We introduce Research Attention Prediction (RAP), a rolling benchmark covering 278 AI/ML fields and 1,390 episodes. At each cut-off, an LLM agent searches a temporally restricted arXiv corpus and predicts the next six months' paper shares across eight frozen research directions. Search generally helps, but all four diagnostic models perform worse than an exact-count exponentially weighted moving average (EWMA) baseline in compositional accuracy. We identify two linked bottlenecks. Under cumulative-history access, State carry-forward outperforms direct Forecast for all four diagnostic models; frozen-evidence replay links a shared component of this reversal to Forecast-oriented policies retrieving a smaller share of recent evidence. Even with exact historical activity, future-specific updating remains limited, with only GPT-5.5 plus reopened Search slightly surpassing EWMA. Fine-tuning on realised outcomes improves Qwen3-4B's forecast Spearman correlation by 0.105 on held-out fields at later origins, with gains also on change-rich episodes.

</details>


### [107] [Agent-Based ML-LLM Fusion with Self-Optimizing Prompts for Plateau Weather Alerts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10135)
*Shuai Yan,Yang Xu,Shan He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SmartWeatherAgent is a three-stage, LLM-enhanced system for tourism meteorological services that combines intent recognition, machine-learning-based hazard prediction, and reasoning-focused text generation to produce scientifically rigorous, scenario-adaptive weather warnings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tourism meteorological services lack deep contextual understanding of user needs, struggle to generalize across scenarios, and adapt poorly to specific tourism contexts such as highland weather, leading to shallow, generic, and sometimes uninformative warnings. The authors aim to build a system that can understand user intent, accurately predict hazards, and generate high-quality, professional, and explainable warnings tailored to complex meteorological and tourism scenarios.

Method: The paper proposes SmartWeatherAgent, a unified three-stage architecture: (1) intent recognition using a fusion of rule-based methods and large language models to parse user queries at multiple granularities; (2) hazard prediction using a LightGBM model enhanced with highland-specific meteorological features (e.g., wind speed abruptness rate) to classify events like high wind, precipitation, and low temperature; and (3) reasoning-enhanced generation via a large language model guided by a 12-round micro-step prompt self-optimization loop, which iteratively improves the quality of generated warnings in terms of data citation, causal explanation, scientific rigor, and uncertainty communication.

Result: The LightGBM hazard prediction module achieves an F1-Macro score of 0.605 with very low latency (1.60 ms) on key hazardous weather events. The reasoning-enhanced generation, optimized through 12 prompt refinement rounds (B01–B12), improves the composite warning quality score S_final from 4.2 to 8.9, a 112% relative increase. Specific gains include better data source citation (score 6.5 to 8.5 on B08), consistently strong performance in physical mechanism explanations (B10), and a peak scientific rigor score of 9.2 in B12 due to explicit uncertainty statements.

Conclusion: SmartWeatherAgent can autonomously produce structured, high-quality meteorological warnings that integrate causal mechanisms, spatiotemporal evolution, quantitative evidence, regulatory references, and explicit confidence levels. This architecture enhances professional depth, logical rigor, and scientific soundness of tourism weather services and represents a step toward proactive, explainable, and agent-like intelligent meteorological decision support.

Abstract: To address insufficient contextualization, weak generalization, and poor scenario adaptation in tourism meteorological services, we propose SmartWeatherAgent--a unified three-stage architecture integrating intent recognition, hazard prediction, and reasoning-enhanced generation. The system fuses rule-based methods with large language models to parse queries at multiple granularities and employs a LightGBM model enriched with highland-specific features (e.g., wind speed abruptness rate), achieving an F1-Macro score of 0.605 with 1.60 ms latency on high-wind, precipitation, and low-temperature events. A 12-round micro-step prompt self-optimization loop boosts the composite warning quality score S_final from 4.2 (B01) to 8.9 (B12, +112%). Key improvements include a sharp rise in B08 from data source citation (6.5 -> 8.5), sustained high performance in B10 via physical mechanism explanation, and a peak scientific rigor score of 9.2 in B12 through explicit uncertainty statements. The system autonomously generates structured warnings that integrate causal mechanisms, spatiotemporal evolution, quantitative evidence, regulatory references, and confidence statements--enhancing professional depth, logical rigor, and scientific soundness, and advancing meteorological services toward proactive perception, explainable decision-making, and intelligent agency.

</details>


### [108] [Kernel-Managed Shared Memory for System-Wide Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10144)
*Ryan Lum,Yongfeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a kernel-managed shared memory abstraction for multi-agent AI systems that centralizes retrieval, privacy, and prompt injection, achieving near-maximal personalization benefits at much lower latency and cost than naive full-context approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-agent AI systems, different agents often learn useful user-specific or task-specific context, but that context remains siloed. Existing approaches either leave retrieval and privacy policy to each agent, leading to inconsistent behavior and safety risks, or rely on naive full-context concatenation, which is costly and doesn’t scale. The authors aim to provide a system-level mechanism that lets agents share and reuse structured memories while enforcing consistent privacy and efficient retrieval, to improve personalization and efficiency across agents and models.

Method: They introduce kernel-managed shared memory as a system-level abstraction: agents write structured, tagged memories to a shared store, but only the agent-system kernel manages how those memories are retrieved and injected into prompts, and how privacy and prompt injection policies are enforced. They implement this design in the AIOS framework and experimentally compare it with three baselines: (1) an unmanaged external memory backend (Mem0) using the same storage but without kernel-managed retrieval/injection; (2) standard retrieval-augmented generation with injected results; and (3) naive, full, unfiltered concatenation of all relevant context. They test three assistant models (GPT-4o, Llama-3.1:8B, Qwen-2.5:7B) across 1,800 trials, measuring personalization quality, latency, and token usage/cost, and run significance tests on outcomes.

Result: Relative to an unmanaged external memory backend using the same underlying storage, the kernel-managed approach improves personalization scores by 2.4–4.0 points on a 5-point scale, such as raising profile usage scores for GPT-4o from 1.05 to 4.69; all such comparisons are statistically significant at p < 10^-18. Compared with standard retrieval-augmented injection, it yields similarly large, consistent gains across the three evaluated models. When compared against full, unfiltered context concatenation—a strong but expensive upper bound—the kernel-managed approach matches performance statistically on two of three models and shows only a small, model-specific deficit on the third. At the same time, it achieves 15–61% lower end-to-end latency across all models, with parallel reductions in token usage and inference cost.

Conclusion: Centralizing memory management in the multi-agent system’s kernel—rather than delegating retrieval, context selection, and privacy enforcement to individual agents—can deliver nearly all of the personalization benefits of unconstrained, full-context prompts while substantially reducing latency and token cost. Kernel-managed shared memory thus offers a scalable, efficient, and safer way to share and exploit user-specific context across agents in multi-agent AI systems.

Abstract: AI systems become more useful when they can adapt to the people using them, but in multi-agent systems, useful context learned by one agent often remains unavailable to others. We present kernel-managed shared memory, a system-level abstraction in which specialized agents write structured, tagged memories while the agent-system kernel, not individual agents, governs retrieval, privacy enforcement, and prompt injection. We implement and evaluate this design on AIOS and compare it against three alternatives across three assistant models (GPT-4o, Llama-3.1:8B, Qwen-2.5:7B) and 1,800 total trials. Against an unmanaged external memory backend (Mem0) using identical underlying storage, kernel-managed retrieval and injection improve personalization scores by 2.4-4.0 points on a 5-point scale (e.g., 1.05 to 4.69 profile usage on GPT-4o), with every comparison significant at p < 10^-18. Against standard retrieval-augmented injection, gains are similarly large and consistent across all three models. Against full, unfiltered context concatenation, a soft ceiling on available context rather than on response quality, kernel-managed injection statistically matches performance on two of three models and shows a small, model-specific deficit on the third, while using substantially shorter prompts: end-to-end latency is 15-61% lower across all three models, with corresponding reductions in per-call token usage and inference cost. These results indicate that centralizing memory management in the agent-system kernel, rather than leaving retrieval and privacy enforcement to individual agents, delivers most of the personalization benefit of unconstrained context at a fraction of its cost.

</details>


### [109] [Beyond Surface Imitation: Contrastive Modeling for Reasoning Path Alignment in Multimodal In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10177)
*Mingbo Yang,Wenqiang Wang,Zhaolu Kang,Peng Chen,Yannan Chen,Sunshang Wang,Yan Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new in-context learning framework for multimodal large language models that uses contrastive demonstrations, response-conditioned retrieval, and a refinement controller to better align model reasoning with complex multimodal tasks, significantly improving performance, especially on VQA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal large language models perform well with in-context learning but tend to imitate example outputs at a surface level, failing to follow the correct reasoning path, particularly in complex multimodal tasks. This weakens alignment between the model’s responses and the reasoning actually required by the multimodal input, limiting further performance gains.

Method: The authors introduce a multimodal ICL framework that (1) reformulates each demonstration as a contrast between a suboptimal and a better response for the same input, plus an explicit reasoning path explaining how to refine the suboptimal answer; (2) adds a response-conditioned retrieval mechanism that, given the model’s current response, selects demonstrations whose reasoning paths are most relevant to refining that response; and (3) employs a lightweight alignment controller that predicts the quality of the current response and decides whether additional refinement iterations are necessary.

Result: Across three categories of multimodal tasks, the proposed framework consistently boosts performance of MLLMs, with especially strong improvements reported on visual question answering benchmarks, indicating more accurate and better-reasoned answers than baselines using standard multimodal ICL.

Conclusion: Making refinement reasoning explicit via contrastive demonstrations, tailoring retrieved examples to the model’s current response, and controlling iterative refinement can push MLLMs beyond superficial imitation in in-context learning, leading to more faithful reasoning and substantial performance gains on complex multimodal tasks like VQA.

Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) is widely used in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and achieves strong performance across a wide range of multimodal tasks. However, existing multimodal ICL methods often rely on surface level imitation of in-context demonstrations, making it difficult for MLLMs to align their responses with the reasoning path required by the given multimodal input. This limitation becomes more pronounced in complex multimodal tasks, thereby restricting further improvements in MLLM performance. To address this issue, we propose a new multimodal ICL framework that combines contrastive demonstration modeling with the self-refinement capability of MLLMs. Specifically, our framework reformulates each demonstration by explicitly contrasting a suboptimal response with a better response under the same input, together with a reasoning path that reveals how the response should be refined. This contrastive formulation makes the reasoning path toward the desired response more explicit and guides the MLLM beyond superficial imitation. Furthermore, because effective refinement depends on the current response, we introduce a response-conditioned retrieval mechanism to select demonstrations whose reasoning paths are more relevant to the current response. In addition, we use a lightweight alignment controller to predict response quality and determine whether further refinement is needed. Experiments on three types of multimodal tasks show that the proposed framework consistently improves MLLM performance, with particularly notable gains on visual question answering (VQA).

</details>


### [110] [Why Sample What You Can Enumerate? Exact Policy Optimization for Genomic Tool Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10221)
*Haoyue Liu,Xiaoyu Ma,Ye Chen,Zhichao Wang,Xiaoying Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies a structural failure mode of GRPO-style reinforcement learning for tool selection in specialized scientific domains and proposes FGPO, which exactly optimizes over an enumerable space of tool subsets, yielding better performance and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In specialist scientific domains like genomics, a small, well-defined set of computational tools recurs across questions, making the space of possible tool subsets combinatorial but fully enumerable. Conventional GRPO-style methods treat tool selection as standard RL over a large action space and rely on sampled rollouts with a frozen reasoner to estimate expected rewards. However, as training progresses and the policy concentrates on certain tool subsets, reward estimates degrade: repeated sampling of the same subsets leads to reward collisions and vanishing group-normalized advantages, causing many questions to receive no learning signal. This mismatch hinders efficient and effective training of tool-selection policies in these settings.

Method: The authors analyze the behavior of GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) when used to train a policy that selects subsets of tools for a frozen reasoner in domains where all possible tool subsets can be enumerated. They empirically demonstrate how GRPO’s sampling-based expectation approximation fails as the policy sharpens, leading to sparse or zero advantages. They then introduce FGPO (Full-Group Policy Optimization), which explicitly enumerates all possible tool subsets, scores each subset for each question, and computes the exact expected reward for policy optimization. To avoid the computational overhead of repeated frozen-reasoner calls, they precompute an exhaustive reward table mapping each question–subset pair to a reward, so that training uses only table lookups instead of online evaluations. They evaluate FGPO against GRPO across five frozen reasoners and three genomic reasoning benchmarks.

Result: FGPO consistently outperforms GRPO across all evaluated configurations: in 15 combinations of five frozen reasoners and three genomic benchmarks, FGPO achieves an average improvement of 6.75 points and up to 14.20 points over GRPO. Furthermore, a standard GRPO training schedule that matches FGPO’s performance would require approximately 2.4 times more reward evaluations from the frozen reasoner, highlighting FGPO’s superior sample efficiency. On the GenomeQA benchmark specifically, FGPO reduces the average number of tools invoked per question from 2.36 to 1.40, indicating more targeted and efficient tool usage. The authors also report that under GRPO, the share of questions with no usable reward signal increases dramatically—from 0.2% under a uniform policy to 20.8% after training—illustrating the failure mode that FGPO avoids.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that GRPO-style reinforcement learning is structurally ill-suited for tool-subset selection in specialist scientific domains where the action space is small and enumerable. The observed reward-collision and vanishing-advantage phenomena cause many queries to lack learning signal as training advances. FGPO (Full-Group Policy Optimization) resolves this by fully enumerating tool subsets, computing exact action expectations, and decoupling reward computation from the training loop via a precomputed reward table. This leads to more stable optimization, better performance, fewer required reward evaluations, and more efficient tool usage. The authors argue that in domains with similar enumerable tool-subset structures, FGPO-like methods should replace GRPO as the default approach for training tool-selection policies.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning over a frozen reasoner has become a common recipe for teaching a policy which external tools to invoke. We show that this recipe becomes structurally mismatched in specialist scientific settings where the complete tool-subset space is enumerable. There, a small set of recurring computational capabilities covers the domain, so the space of tool subsets is combinatorial yet small enough to enumerate, and GRPO still estimates an action expectation from a handful of sampled rollouts. Worse, the approximation degrades as training succeeds: as the policy concentrates on preferred subsets it resamples them, sampled rewards collide, and the group-normalized advantage vanishes. On genomic reasoning the fraction of questions yielding no reward signal rises from 0.2% under a uniform reference policy to 20.8% after GRPO training. As a remedy, we introduce FGPO (Full-Group Policy Optimization), which (1) scores every tool subset and optimizes the exact action expectation, so each update sees the complete action space, and (2) precomputes the reward of each question--subset pair into an exhaustive table, removing frozen-reasoner calls from the training loop entirely. Across five frozen reasoners and three genomic benchmarks, FGPO outperforms GRPO in all 15 settings by 6.75 points on average and up to 14.20, while a standard on-demand GRPO schedule would require 2.4 times as many frozen-reasoner reward evaluations and, on GenomeQA, FGPO cuts invoked tools per question from 2.36 to 1.40.

</details>


### [111] [What Should an Agent Forget? Separating What Is Stored from What Is Used](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10263)
*Yuhang Li,Yuchen Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RD-Forget is a training-free framework that separates what a language agent stores from what it uses, enabling it to keep full historical data while dynamically constructing query-specific memories that handle updated or obsolete facts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language agents that persist over time need to both remember past information and adapt to changing facts. Conventional approaches struggle because outdated information can corrupt current answers, yet is still necessary for historical or temporal questions. The authors aim to design a memory system that retains full history but avoids obsolete information misguiding present-time answers, without retraining the language model.

Method: RD-Forget introduces a separated memory architecture: (1) a retained source archive that stores all observations; (2) a query-conditioned memory view built at answer time. A frozen language-model curator selects relevant evidence from the archive, groups related facts into semantic slots, and preserves relations needed for multi-hop reasoning. Within each slot, same-slot replacement mechanisms suppress superseded values for current-state queries, while intent-aware retrieval reactivates older facts for historical questions. The process of building the query-specific memory is framed as a rate-distortion optimization under a memory budget, balancing compactness with faithfulness to relevant evidence.

Result: Through experiments across conversational memory, knowledge updating, fact consolidation, long-context reasoning, and personalization, RD-Forget improves answer accuracy compared with baselines that either do not forget obsolete information or do not condition memory on the query. Ablation studies show that lack of forgetting or query conditioning leads to the largest performance drops, while semantic slot grouping, preserving relations, and enabling historical access each add complementary gains.

Conclusion: RD-Forget demonstrates that maintaining a full historical archive while explicitly controlling which parts affect each answer is an effective strategy for dealing with evolving knowledge. By separating storage from use and using query-conditioned, rate-distortion-guided memory views with semantic slot replacement and historical recovery, language agents can better support both up-to-date answers and temporally-aware queries.

Abstract: Persistent language agents need stored experience to remain available across time, while each answer requires evidence suited to a particular question. A superseded fact can mislead a current-state answer and still be essential for a historical query. We present RD-Forget, a training-free framework that separates what an agent stores from what it uses. A retained source archive preserves observations, and a query-conditioned memory view controls their influence on the current answer. A frozen language-model curator extracts relevant evidence, groups facts into semantic slots, and preserves the relations needed for multi-hop reasoning. Same-slot replacement links suppress superseded values in current-state contexts, while intent-aware retrieval makes earlier evidence eligible again. A rate-distortion formulation guides construction of the answer-time view within a memory budget. Experiments span conversational memory, knowledge updating, fact consolidation, long-context reasoning, and personalization under a shared answering pipeline. The results associate accurate answers with both query-relevant evidence construction and control over obsolete alternatives. Configurations without forgetting or query conditioning have the largest score deficits, while slot grouping, historical access, and relation preservation contribute complementary functions. Retaining history while selectively controlling its use offers a practical way to accommodate changing facts and future questions.

</details>


### [112] [TRACE: Training Reasoning Agents for Causal Exploration with Synthesized Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10315)
*Rui Sun,Zhan Shi,Bing He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They create a simulated diagnostic environment (TRACE) where hidden interventions give ground-truth labels, enabling reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards to improve complex diagnostic reasoning beyond large prompted models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL with verifiable rewards works well for math/code where answers are cheap to check, but real-world diagnostic reasoning (like root-causing anomalies) often lacks clear, inexpensive ground truth. The authors want to engineer a setting where they can still obtain objective rewards for such tasks, to see if scalable training signals can matter more than model scale.

Method: They propose engineering verification asymmetry by sampling interventions in a controlled simulator, injecting them, and generating corresponding observations. This makes the hidden intervention an oracle label, while the agent still sees noisy, confounded evidence. They instantiate this as TRACE, a digital advertising diagnostic simulator with 12 root causes and segment attribution. Agents use Python and SQL tools to investigate and must output the root cause and affected segment. They compare prompted LLM baselines with a smaller open model that’s first supervised fine-tuned, then trained with RL using the synthetic rewards from the simulator.

Result: In TRACE, the best prompted baseline (Claude Opus 5) achieves 0.686 FullAttr@1. A Qwen3.5-35B-A3B model improves from 0.159 (base) to 0.637 after supervised fine-tuning and further to 0.757 after RL with synthesized rewards, surpassing all prompted baselines, including larger and closed models and a prompted Qwen3.5-122B-A10B. The RL-trained policy also needs substantially fewer tool calls than the prompted 35B base model.

Conclusion: Simulation-based verification can create scalable, objective reward signals for inherently ambiguous diagnostic reasoning, enabling RL to substantially improve performance and efficiency. Access to high-quality, scalable rewards may be more critical than raw model size, and this engineered verification approach can make complex diagnostic tasks amenable to reinforcement learning advances.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has advanced language-model reasoning in domains such as mathematics and code, where objective answers are inexpensive to check. Diagnostic reasoning over complex data lacks this advantage: establishing the true cause of an anomaly often requires costly expert investigation and may remain ambiguous after the fact. We ask whether this asymmetry of verification can instead be engineered. We sample an intervention, inject it into a controlled simulator, and generate the observations it would produce. The hidden intervention provides an oracle label and objective reward, while the agent must still investigate noisy, confounded, and distributed evidence.
  We instantiate this approach in TRACE, a digital-advertising diagnostic environment with 12 root causes and fine-grained segment attribution. Agents investigate each episode using Python and SQL and must identify both the root cause and, when applicable, the affected segment assignment. On a held-out 235-episode test set, the strongest prompted baseline, Claude Opus 5, reaches 0.686 FullAttr@1. Supervised fine-tuning raises Qwen3.5-35B-A3B from 0.159 to 0.637, and subsequent RL with synthesized rewards reaches 0.757, outperforming all evaluated prompted baselines, including frontier closed-source models and a prompted Qwen3.5-122B-A10B model. The resulting policy also uses substantially fewer tool calls than the prompted 35B base. These results provide evidence that access to a scalable, objective training signal can be a more important constraint than model scale alone. More broadly, simulation-based verification can make otherwise ambiguous diagnostic reasoning tasks amenable to scalable reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [113] [From Symbolic Perception to Logical Deduction: A Framework for Guiding Language Models in Geometric Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10335)
*Weichen Dai,Rafael Medeiros Cabral,Ziyi Shou,Yan Cao,Xin Shen,Dongcai Lu,Yi Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a modular framework that enables a pure LLM, augmented with a geometric vision parser and symbolic solver, to solve plane geometry problems competitively with large multimodal models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plane geometry requires both visual understanding of diagrams and symbolic mathematical reasoning, posing a challenge for current AI systems. Existing large multimodal models can handle visuo-linguistic inputs but are often computationally heavy and lack interpretability. There is a need for a more efficient and transparent approach that still achieves high performance on complex geometry tasks.

Method: The authors build a framework around a pure Large Language Model, adding two key modules: (1) a Geometric Vision Parser that converts geometry diagrams into symbolic representations, and (2) a Symbolic Solver that performs formal geometric deductions. This modular design separates perception from reasoning, reduces hallucinations, and makes the reasoning process interpretable. They also construct a new benchmark from difficult plane geometry problems taken from the 2025 Chinese Zhongkao exams to rigorously test deductive capabilities and ensure novelty with respect to training data.

Result: On the curated Zhongkao geometry benchmark, the proposed system achieves accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art Large Multimodal Models such as Gemini 2.5 Pro, while producing clearer, human-like solution steps. The combination of a vision parser and symbolic solver allows the LLM-based system to solve complex geometry problems effectively without relying on a full multimodal foundation model.

Conclusion: A pure LLM, when combined with dedicated perception and symbolic reasoning modules, can match the performance of leading multimodal systems on challenging plane geometry tasks, while offering better interpretability and potentially lower computational cost. This suggests that modular architectures may be a promising direction for advancing mathematical reasoning in AI without fully depending on large, opaque multimodal models.

Abstract: Plane geometry remains a significant challenge in AI, requiring the integration of visual perception and mathematical reasoning. While Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) naturally handle visuo-linguistic inputs, they are often computationally intensive and opaque. We demonstrate that a pure Large Language Model (LLM), when equipped with specialized modules, can rival state-of-the-art LMMs on complex geometry problems. Our framework integrates a Geometric Vision Parser, which translates diagrams into symbolic form, with a Symbolic Solver that performs formal deductions, thereby mitigating hallucinations and promoting interpretable reasoning. To enable rigorous evaluation, we curate a benchmark of challenging problems from the 2025 Chinese Zhongkao examinations, ensuring data novelty and testing deeper deductive skills. Experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves performance comparable to Gemini 2.5 Pro while delivering clearer, human-like solutions.

</details>


### [114] [Cyber-Financial Contagion: Modeling the Propagation of an AI Vendor Compromise Through the Banking System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10350)
*Alex Leytes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how cybersecurity failures at shared AI service vendors can propagate through the banking system and become systemic, and proposes quantitative models to simulate cascades and to detect high-risk vendors early.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Banks increasingly rely on a concentrated set of third‑party AI vendors for critical functions like fraud detection, credit scoring, AML, and analytics. This creates a cyber concentration risk: if one such vendor is compromised, many institutions may be affected simultaneously. Existing financial stability models and cyber‑risk models do not fully capture how such vendor‑side incidents can propagate through operational, informational, and financial linkages to produce system‑wide banking losses. Regulators and supervisors lack quantitative tools to evaluate how serious this concentration risk is and to identify which vendors pose the greatest systemic threat.

Method: The authors construct a four‑layer heterogeneous network consisting of AI vendors, banks, interbank exposures, and customer accounts. On top of this network they introduce CFC‑Prop, a stochastic epidemic‑style propagation and clearing model that simulates how a compromise at an AI vendor spreads through operational failures and financial linkages, producing losses and defaults. They use a synthetic but realistic dataset with 60 vendors, 220 banks, about 2,500 vendor‑bank service relationships, and 1,400 interbank exposures to run these simulations and study loss distributions and sensitivity to patch latency. They further propose CFC‑GNN, a graph neural network early‑warning system that ingests vendor incident telemetry and network structure to predict which vendors have high cascade risk, and they benchmark it against four baseline models while assessing both discrimination and calibration performance.

Result: CFC‑Prop generates loss patterns that exhibit heavy‑tailed distributions and a strong sensitivity to how quickly vendors patch compromises, in line with prior empirical evidence on cyber‑financial risk. The CFC‑GNN early‑warning model effectively identifies high‑cascade‑risk vendors, achieving an AUROC of 0.82 and AUPRC of 0.60 while maintaining bounded calibration error, outperforming baseline methods. The authors provide open‑source code, synthetic data, and reproducible scripts for the community.

Conclusion: The study concludes that cyber concentration risk arising from dependence on a small set of AI vendors is a first‑order financial stability concern rather than a narrow cybersecurity issue. The proposed CFC‑Prop and CFC‑GNN frameworks give supervisors and policymakers practical, quantitative tools to simulate potential systemic cascades, explore the impact of mitigation measures such as reduced concentration or faster patching, and to prioritize oversight of vendors whose failure would be most destabilizing. Openly releasing the tooling facilitates further research and potential policy experimentation around managing AI‑driven cyber‑financial concentration risk.

Abstract: The banking system now depends on a small set of shared artificial intelligence vendors for fraud screening, credit decisioning, anti-money-laundering triage, customer analytics, and internal decision support. This paper studies how a compromise inside one of those vendors can propagate along a chain of operational, informational, and financial linkages until it triggers losses that look, from the outside, like a classical banking crisis. We build a four-layer heterogeneous network that couples AI vendors, financial institutions, interbank exposures, and customer accounts, and we propose CFC-Prop, a stochastic epidemic-and-clearing model that runs on that network. On a synthetic dataset with 60 vendors, 220 banks, roughly 2,500 vendor-bank service edges, and 1,400 interbank exposures, CFC-Prop reproduces the heavy-tailed loss distributions and the sharp dependence on patch latency that are consistent with prior cyber-financial evidence. We also train an early-warning model, CFC-GNN, that uses vendor-side incident telemetry and graph structure to flag high-cascade-risk vendors before impact. Across four baselines the proposed model reaches AUROC 0.82 and AUPRC 0.60 while keeping calibration errors bounded. We release the full code, synthetic data, and reproducible scripts. The results argue that cyber concentration among AI vendors is a first-order financial-stability problem and give supervisors a concrete quantitative tool for reasoning about it.

</details>


### [115] [Fortunate Recall: Ontology-Driven Memory Lifecycle Management for Persistent Coherence in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10413)
*Ansuman Mullick,Eray Tüzün*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose Fortunate Recall, a lifecycle-aware memory policy layer for LLMs that classifies personal facts into behavior-based categories and applies tailored retention and retrieval rules, improving temporal reasoning and reducing confabulations without hurting standard retrieval performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM memory systems treat all personal facts the same, causing unbounded memory growth and degraded retrieval precision, especially for time- or state-dependent information. There is a need for principled lifecycle management of memories: deciding what to keep, what to replace, and at what rate, depending on how each fact behaves over time.

Method: They introduce Fortunate Recall (FR), a composable policy layer that assigns personal facts to a 10+1 behavioral ontology and attaches category-specific lifecycle rules such as differential temporal decay, slot-key supersession, event-time validity, and category-aware retrieval routing. These policies are implemented as deterministic functions on metadata extracted by an LLM. They implement FR-Bank, an infrastructure-agnostic memory system embodying these policies, and evaluate it on new and existing benchmarks (LifecycleBench, LongMemEval-S, BEAM). They run ablations contrasting the full typed ontology with a version using only generic lifecycle primitives to disentangle the contribution of structured metadata vs. ontology typing.

Result: FR-Bank achieves a 76.9% pass rate on the new LifecycleBench temporal-disambiguation benchmark, outperforming baseline systems (Mem0, A-MEM, Memory-R1, MemoryOS) which score 61–70.5%. On LongMemEval-S with the standard Wu et al. judging protocol, FR-Bank scores 75.2%, showing that lifecycle policies do not harm conventional retrieval metrics. An ablation shows that replacing the typed ontology with generic lifecycle primitives keeps accuracy essentially unchanged (-1.7 percentage points, 95% CI [-6.0, +2.7]), indicating that generic lifecycle metadata explains most correctness gains, while the ontology mainly improves calibration: it halves confabulation rates (12.0% vs 24.2%, p<0.001). Overall, FR-Bank reduces confabulations compared to Mem0 from 45.1% to 22.4% on answered queries and from 32.2% to 13.0% on all queries, while improving correct answers (31.2% vs 18.6%). Similar performance gains and rankings are observed on the open-weight Kimi K2.5 model. On the independent BEAM benchmark, FR-Bank gets 46.8% accuracy vs Mem0's 32.9%, with ontology benefits concentrated in resolving contradictions and saturating around seven policy clusters.

Conclusion: Lifecycle-aware memory policies, instantiated by a behavioral ontology plus generic lifecycle metadata, materially improve LLM long-term memory: they enhance temporal disambiguation, reduce confabulations, and do so without sacrificing standard retrieval accuracy. The structured ontology mainly yields better calibration and contradiction handling, while the generic lifecycle primitives drive correctness gains. The approach is implementation-agnostic and validated across benchmarks and models, and the authors release their ontology, benchmark, and code to facilitate further research.

Abstract: Current LLM memory systems treat all personal facts identically, so stores grow without bound while retrieval precision degrades. The core challenge is lifecycle management: which memories should persist, which should be replaced, and at what rate, conditioned on the behavioral type of each fact. Fortunate Recall (FR) is a composable policy layer that classifies personal facts into a 10+1 behavioral ontology and applies category-specific lifecycle policies (differential temporal decay, slot-key supersession, event-time validity, and category-aware retrieval routing) as deterministic functions over LLM-extracted metadata. FR-Bank, our infrastructure-independent implementation, reaches a 76.9% pass rate on LifecycleBench, a new 516-question temporal-disambiguation benchmark, ahead of Mem0, A-MEM, Memory-R1, and MemoryOS (61% to 70.5%), and 75.2% on the full LongMemEval-S under the canonical Wu et al. judge protocol, so lifecycle policies impose no measurable cost on standard retrieval. A pre-registered ablation locates the gains: replacing the typed layer with three generic lifecycle primitives leaves correctness statistically unchanged (-1.7pp, 95% CI [-6.0, +2.7]), so the generic lifecycle metadata carries the correctness advantage, while the behavioral ontology carries calibration, halving downstream confabulation (12.0% vs 24.2%, p<0.001). End-to-end, FR-Bank cuts confabulation from Mem0's 45.1% to 22.4% over answered queries and from 32.2% to 13.0% over all queries while answering more of them correctly (31.2% vs 18.6%); the ranking replicates on the open-weight Kimi K2.5. The decomposition transfers to BEAM, an independently built benchmark: 46.8% correct vs Mem0's 32.9% over 280 questions, with the ontology's benefit concentrated in contradiction resolution and saturating near seven policy clusters. The ontology, benchmark, and code are released.

</details>


### [116] [ConvMem: Convolutional Memory for Long-Context Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10441)
*Hongming Zhang,Zhaozhen Gu,Fengshuo Bai,Ming Hao,Qingyang Zhang,Yuanyuan Wang,Shiyang Tang,Yanna Wang,Bo Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ConvMem is a training-free, CNN-inspired framework that lets LLMs handle very long contexts efficiently by hierarchically summarizing text segments in parallel instead of sequentially.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs have fixed context windows and struggle with extremely long documents. Sequential memory agents like MemAgent extend context but suffer from high latency, need expensive RL training, and often overfit to specific datasets, especially hurting out-of-distribution performance. The authors want a long-context solution that is efficient, parallelizable, and does not require task-specific training.

Method: They model long-context reasoning as a hierarchical convolution over text segments. An LLM, prompted with a query, is treated as a convolutional kernel that processes segments and passes summarized information up a tree. ConvMem shortens the reasoning path from linear (segment-by-segment) to logarithmic depth via hierarchical aggregation. Key design elements: (1) Configurable Strides: control how densely segments are sampled to balance coverage vs efficiency; (2) Skip Connections: allow summaries or raw segments to bypass intermediate layers to prevent information loss and support robust evidence propagation; (3) Multi-Kernel Convolution: decompose a complex query into multiple semantic channels, each handled by a separate kernel, to disentangle reasoning threads and reduce interference. The entire process is training-free and highly parallelizable both across segments and across kernels.

Result: On long-context multi-hop benchmarks RULER-HotpotQA and RULER-2WikiMultiHopQA, ConvMem achieves better performance than other training-free long-context baselines. It also compares favorably to RL-trained agents by avoiding their tendency to overfit parametric priors, particularly on out-of-distribution tasks.

Conclusion: ConvMem offers an efficient, parallel, and training-free alternative to sequential memory agents for long-context reasoning with LLMs. By treating the LLM as a convolutional kernel in a hierarchical architecture with strides, skip connections, and multi-kernel decomposition, it reduces reasoning depth, mitigates error accumulation, and scales to very long inputs while retaining robustness across datasets.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities, they often struggle with extremely long contexts due to fixed context limits. To address this, sequential approaches like MemAgent extend the effective context by reading text in segments and iteratively updating a fixed-size memory. However, this sequential paradigm suffers from high latency and requires costly reinforcement learning (RL) training, which can lead to overfitting on specific datasets. To overcome these limitations, we propose ConvMem, a training-free, highly parallelizable framework that reformulates long-context reasoning as a hierarchical convolution. Inspired by CNNs, ConvMem treats an LLM prompted with a specific query as a convolutional kernel. This kernel summarizes text segments hierarchically, shortening the reasoning path from a linear chain into a logarithmic tree. Specifically, ConvMem integrates \textit{Configurable Strides} and \textit{Skip Connections} to ensure robust evidence capture and propagation, while employing \textit{Multi-Kernel Convolution} to decompose complex queries into disentangled semantic channels. This design not only mitigates error accumulation but also enables massive parallelization across both text segments and reasoning threads. Experiments on RULER-HotpotQA and RULER-2WikiMultiHopQA demonstrate that ConvMem outperforms training-free baselines and avoids the risk of overfitting to parametric priors often observed in RL-trained models on out-of-distribution tasks.

</details>


### [117] [JarvisGUI: Towards Cross-Device GUI Agents with Dynamic Task Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10451)
*Zixiang Chen,Yuheng Lu,Zihao Cheng,Zeming Liu,Jizeng Bai,Ziye Huang,Zhiyin Lin,Zihan Li,Yuhang Guo,Yunhong Wang,Haifeng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose JarvisGUI, a benchmark that evaluates GUI agents on realistic, cross-device workflows across Android, Windows, and Ubuntu, revealing major shortcomings in current agents that single-device benchmarks miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world GUI use involves multi-device, cross-platform workflows with state transfer and coordination, but existing benchmarks mostly test agents on single-device, static tasks. This mismatch can create an overly optimistic view of GUI agents’ readiness for real applications, so there is a need to evaluate agents under realistic cross-device conditions.

Method: They design JarvisGUI, a dynamic benchmark that models GUI tasks as input-output transformations under a lightweight type system. This representation enables automatic composition of multi-step, cross-device workflows. The benchmark runs agents in virtualized environments covering multiple OSes (Android, Windows, Ubuntu) and dynamically evaluates their performance on these coordinated tasks in a unified framework.

Result: Using JarvisGUI to test state-of-the-art open-source GUI agents, they find that these agents perform poorly on cross-device workflows. Specifically, the agents fail at state-transfer awareness, cross-platform contextual reasoning, and handling long-horizon dependencies, even though such weaknesses are not apparent on traditional single-device benchmarks.

Conclusion: JarvisGUI exposes a critical capability gap in current GUI agents that is invisible to existing benchmarks, demonstrating that single-device evaluations are insufficient and that robust cross-device, long-horizon reasoning is needed for real-world GUI automation.

Abstract: Real-world GUI usage frequently involves workflows that span multiple devices and platforms, requiring the transfer of intermediate results, maintenance of shared state, and coordination across heterogeneous environments. However, existing GUI benchmarks overwhelmingly evaluate agents on single-device, statically defined tasks, thus leaving such cross-device capabilities largely unexamined, resulting in an overly optimistic assessment of agents' readiness for real-world usage. We introduce JarvisGUI, a dynamic benchmark that evaluates GUI agents on cross-device workflows requiring coordinated interaction across heterogeneous platforms, including Android, Windows, and Ubuntu. Specifically, JarvisGUI formulates GUI tasks as input-output transformations under a lightweight type system, which allows us to automatically compose multi-step, cross-device workflows and dynamically evaluate agent performance within a unified framework. By evaluating agents in virtual environments spanning multiple operating systems, JarvisGUI reveals that state-of-the-art open-source GUI agents struggle with the state-transfer awareness, cross-platform contextual reasoning, and long-horizon dependency management required for real-world workflows, exposing a critical capability gap invisible to existing benchmarks.

</details>
